[
BCL09
]
. Suitably extended, this notion plays a central role in the “concrete” consequences of the results in this paper, and the authors would like to thank Antoine Chambert-Loir for this very helpful discussion.

0.6.
Conventions and notation

0.6.1.
Notation

For every
x\in\mathbb{R},
we use the notation:

x^{+}:=\max(0,x)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lfloor x\rfloor:=\max\{n\in\mathbb{Z}\mid n\leq x\}.

For every
a\in\mathbb{C}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},
we note:

\mathring{D}(a;r):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z-a|<r\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{D}(a;r):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z-a|\leq r\}.

If
A
is an integral domain, we denote its field of fractions by
\mathrm{Frac}\,A
.

When
K
is a number field, we will usually denote by
\{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\}
the family of field embedding of
K
in
\mathbb{C}
, and by
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
its ring of integers.

If
M
is a module over some ring
A
, and if
B
is some commutative
A
-algebra, we denote by
M_{B}
the “base changed” module
M\otimes_{A}B
. Similarly, if
\varphi:M\longrightarrow N
is a morphism of
A
-modules, we let:

\varphi_{B}:=\varphi\otimes_{A}{{\mathrm{Id}}}_{B}:M_{B}\longrightarrow N_{B},

and if
{\mathcal{X}}
is some
A
-scheme, we let:

{\mathcal{X}}_{B}:={\mathcal{X}}\times_{{\rm Spec\,}A}{\rm Spec\,}B.

When
K
is a number field, with ring of integers
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},
and
\sigma:K{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}
is a field embedding, these base change constructions may be applied to
A:={{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
and
B:=\mathbb{C}
, considered as an
A
-algebra by means of
\sigma.
Then we denote
M_{B}
(resp.
{\mathcal{X}}_{B}
) by
M_{\sigma}
(resp.
{\mathcal{X}}_{\sigma}
).

If
D
is a Cartier divisor on a scheme or a complex analytic space
X,
we denote by
\mathbbm{1}_{D}
the canonical section of the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)
over
X
, which admits
D
as divisor. When moreover
D
is effective, the
normal bundle

N_{D}X
is defined as the restriction to
D
of the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)
.

A line bundle
L
over a scheme
X
projective over some field
k
is
nef
when, for every closed integral subscheme
C
of dimension 1 in
X
, we have:

\deg_{C}L_{|C}:=\deg_{k}c_{1}(L)\cap[C]\geq 0;

see for instance
[
Ful98
, Section 2.5 and Definition 1.4]
for the definition of the operation
c_{1}(L)\cap\cdot
and of the degree
\deg_{k}
of
0
-cycles.

If
X
is an integral projective surface over
k
, then a nef line bundle
L
over
X
is
big
if and only if:

L\cdot L:=\deg_{k}c_{1}(L)^{2}\cap[X]

is positive;
see
[
Laz04
, section 2.2]
. The nef and big line bundles over
X
are sometimes called
numerically positive
; see for instance
[
Szp81
]
.

A
Hermitian vector bundle over a reduced analytic space
M
is a pair
{\overline{E}}:=(E,\|.\|)
where
E
is a complex analytic vector bundle over
M
, and
\|.\|
is a continuous Hermitian metric on
E
over
M
.

A
Hermitian vector bundle over a reduced, separated scheme
X
of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
is defined as a pair
{\overline{E}}:=(E,\|.\|)
where
E
is a vector bundle over
X
and
\|.\|
is a continuous Hermitian metric on the complex analytic vector bundle
E_{\mathbb{C}}^{{\rm an}}
over
X(\mathbb{C})
; the metric
\|.\|
is moreover required to be invariant under complex conjugation. We shall denote by
{\overline{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}
the Hermitian vector bundle
(E_{\mathbb{C}}^{{\rm an}},\|.\|)
over the analytic space
X(\mathbb{C}).

A
Hermitian line bundle
is a Hermitian vector bundle of rank one.

If
{\overline{L}}:=(L,\|.\|)
is Hermitian line bundle with a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|
over a complex manifold
M
, its
first Chern form

c_{1}({\overline{L}})
is the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
real
(1,1)
-form on
M
defined by:

c_{1}({\overline{L}})_{\mid U}:=(2\pi i)^{-1}\partial\overline{\partial}\log\|s\|^{2},

where
s
is a non-vanishing complex analytic section of
L
over some open subset
U
of
M
.

We shall denote by
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
the ring of meromorphic functions (resp. of formal meromorphic functions) on some complex analytic space (resp. on some formal scheme)
{\mathcal{V}}
.

We shall sometimes use the expression
arithmetic scheme
to designate a separated scheme of finite type over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, not necessarily flat.

0.6.2.
Riemann surfaces with boundary

As in
[
Bos20
, 10.5.1]
, we define a
Riemann surface with boundary
as a pair
(V,V^{+})
where
V^{+}
is the germ of a Riemann surface along a closed
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
submanifold with boundary
V
of
V^{+}
, of codimension
0
in
V^{+}
. The
interior
of
(V,V^{+})
is defined as the interior
\mathring{V}
of
V
in
V^{+}
. It is a Riemann surface without boundary. The
boundary
of
(V,V^{+})
is defined as

\partial V:=V\setminus\mathring{V}.

We say that
(V,V^{+})
is compact (resp. connected) when
V
is. For simplicity’s sake, we will often write
V
for the Riemann surface with boundary
(V,V^{+}),
but will write:

\alpha:V^{+}\longrightarrow N

for a complex analytic map to emphasize that
\alpha
is analytic up to the boundary.

A
real structure
on the Riemann surface with boundary
(V,V^{+})
is an antiholomorphic involution of
V^{+}
that preserves
V
.

A complex analytic vector bundle
E
on the Riemann surface with boundary
(V,V^{+})
is a complex analytic vector bundle on the germ of Riemann surfaces
V^{+}
. A Hermitian vector bundle
{\overline{E}}:=(E,\|.\|)
on the Riemann surface with boundary
(V,V^{+})
is the data of a complex analytic vector bundle
E
on
V^{+}
and of a continuous Hermitian metric on
E
over
V
.

0.6.3.
Arithmetic surfaces

We recall some basic definitions and some classical facts concerning arithmetic surfaces that will be used freely in this article. We refer to
[
Sha66
,
Lic68
,
Lip78
,
Art86
,
MB89
]
for proofs and additional references.

An
arithmetic surface

X
is a scheme separated of finite type and flat over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, such that every component of
|X|
has Krull dimension 2. An
integral curve
in
X
is a closed integral subscheme of
X
of Krull dimension 1. An integral curve
C
in
X
is either
horizontal
, when the morphism
C\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
is flat, hence quasi-finite, or
vertical
, when the morphism
C\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
factorizes as

C\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{F}_{p}{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}

for some prime
p
.

Let
X
be a normal arithmetic surface. Its contains a maximal open regular subscheme
X_{\mathrm{reg}}
, and the complement
X\setminus X_{\mathrm{reg}}
is a finite union of closed points of
X
. As a consequence of the resolution of singularities for arithmetic surfaces, the local Picard group at each of these points is finite (
[
MB89
]
). This admits the following consequences:

(1)

Any Weil divisor on
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier; in other words, the scheme
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
–factorial. More precisely, there exists a positive integer
N(X)
such that, for every Weil divisor
Z
on
X
, the divisor
N(X)Z
is a Cartier divisor.

(2)

If
X
is an affine normal arithmetic surface, an open subscheme
V
of
X
is affine if and only if the complement
|X|\setminus V
has pure dimension 1, or equivalently is a finite union of integral curves.

By Nagata’s compactification theorem, any arithmetic surface
X
may be embedded as an open subscheme in some arithmetic surface
\overline{X}
that is proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
. When
X
is normal,
\overline{X}
may be chosen to be normal. Any such normal proper arithmetic surface is actually projective over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed a horizontal effective Cartier divisor
14
14

14

that is, an effective Cartier divisor flat over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
.

D
in a projective normal integral arithmetic surface
X
is ample if and only if its support
|D|
meets every vertical integral curve (
[
Lic68
]
).

Let
X
be an integral normal arithmetic surface. Then
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a smooth integral
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme of dimension 1. Its field of constants
K
, defined as the algebraic closure of
\mathbb{Q}
in the field of rational functions
\kappa(X_{\mathbb{Q}})=\kappa(X)
, is a number field. The scheme
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is canonically a
K
-scheme, and as such, is a smooth geometrically integral curve. If we denote by
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
the ring of integers of
K
, the image of the inclusion
{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\hookrightarrow\kappa(X)
lies in the subring
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(X)
of the field
\kappa(X)
consisting of the regular functions on
X
, since
X
is integral and normal. The inclusion
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(X)
defines a morphism of schemes:

\pi:X\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{\mathcal{O}}_{K}.

The morphism
\pi
is smooth with geometrically connected fibers over some non-empty open subscheme of
{\rm Spec\,}{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.

Conversely, for any integral normal arithmetic surface
X
and every number field
K
, if a morphism of schemes:

\pi^{\prime}:X\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{\mathcal{O}}_{K}

has an integral geometric generic fiber
X_{\overline{K}}
, then
K
“is” the field of constants of
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
\pi^{\prime}
is the morphism
\pi
described above. This is the case for instance when the morphism
\pi^{\prime}
admits a section.

When the integral normal arithmetic surface
X
is projective, the morphism
\pi
coincides with the Stein factorization of the morphism
X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
.

The Riemann surface
X(\mathbb{C})
whose points are the complex points of the
\mathbb{Z}
-scheme
X
may be identified with the disjoint union:

\coprod_{\sigma:K{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}}X_{\sigma}(\mathbb{C}),

where
X_{\sigma}
denotes the connected smooth complex curve
X\times_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma}\mathbb{C}.

The results and techniques in
[
MB89
]
lead to the following descriptions of open or closed affine subschemes of an integral normal projective arithmetic surface
X
.

For every non-empty open subscheme
U
of
X
, with complement the reduced closed subscheme
F:=|X\setminus U|
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

the scheme
U
is affine;

(2)

the scheme
F
is purely one-dimensional; every vertical integral curve of
X
meets
F
; moreover, if
F=H\cup V
where
H
(resp.
V
) is a finite union of horizontal (resp. vertical) integral curves, then every connected component of
V
meets
H
;

(3)

there exists an ample effective Cartier divisor
D
in
X
whose support
|D|
coincides with
F
.

When these conditions are satisfied, if
D_{0}
is an effective divisor on
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
whose support is
F_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the ample effective divisor
D
in (iii) may be chosen such that
D_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a positive multiple of
D_{0}
.

For every closed subscheme
Y
of
X
, distinct of
X
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

the scheme
Y
is affine;

(2)

every irreducible component of
|Y|
is either a closed point or a horizontal integral curve in
X
;

(3)

there exists an affine open subscheme
U
of
X
containing
Y
.

When these conditions hold, the affine open subscheme
U
in (iii) may be chosen such that the complement
|X\setminus U|
is a finite union of horizontal integral curves in
X
.

0.6.4.
Green functions, Arakelov divisors, and
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
regularity

Starting from Chapter
5
,
Green function
will mean Green function with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
regularity, and
Arakelov divisor
will mean Arakelov divisor with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
Green function, etc.; see page
4.5.3
.

Part I
Projective curves in analytic surfaces, Nori’s finiteness theorems, and pseudoconcavity

Chapter 1
Projective surfaces, pseudoconcave analytic surfaces, and étale fundamental groups

Part 1 of this memoir is devoted to a series of results concerning complex algebraic and analytic varieties that will constitute geometric models for the arithmetic results, concerning projective and formal analytic projective surfaces, that will be established in Parts 2 and 3.

Most of the geometric ideas underlying our work are presented in this first chapter. Notably we discuss diverse results concerning the geometry and topology of complex surfaces that appear (some of them implicitly) in Nori’s famous paper
[
Nor83
]
on the fundamental groups of quasi-projective complex surfaces. In this paper, Nori investigates the topology of these surfaces and of their embedded curves by considering some auxiliary complex analytic surfaces satisfying a suitable positivity condition, which turns out to be related to the pseudoconcavity of these surfaces, in the sense of Andreotti and Grauert (
[
AG62
]
,
[
And63
]
).

More specifically, in this chapter we elaborate on the “easy” part of
[
Nor83
]
— which avoids the sophisticated arguments of deformation theory in complex analytic geometry that constitute the bulk of
[
Nor83
]
1
1

1

The “transcendental” results in
[
Nor83
]
, concerning usual (and not only étale) fundamental groups, have been given an alternative proof by Napier and Ramachandran
[
NR98
]
, relying on
L^{2}
estimates for the
\overline{\partial}
operator instead of deformation theory. Napier and Ramachandran actually establish a stronger version of Nori’s results, notably a “weak Lefschetz theorem” concerning suitable immersion of codimension greater than one. Degree estimates
à la
Nori do not seem to have been established in the framework studied in
[
NR98
]
, which however suggests interesting developments of the results of this monograph concerning étale fundamental groups of arithmetic schemes.
— and we establish various extensions of Nori’s results whose proofs require only basic techniques of algebraic and analytic geometry. These techniques also allow us to establish some avatars of the finiteness and algebraicity results in
[
And63
]
concerning pseudoconcave complex analytic surfaces.

The content of Part 1 is not explicitly used in Parts 2 and 3. From a strictly logical perspective, Part 1 could be skipped by a reader interested in the arithmetic results of this memoir only. However we believe that, independently of their own geometrical interest, the results presented in Part 1 shed some light on the constructions and arguments developed in the arithmetic framework of Parts 2 and 3. Let us briefly discuss this in more detail.

In Part 1, a key role is played by complex analytic surfaces
{\mathcal{V}}
containing a (possibly singular) connected projective curve
C
. In most of our results, it is not the analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
itself but its germ
{\mathcal{V}}^{\rm an}_{C}
along
C
that indeed matters. Actually our results admit variants involving the formal germ
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
along
C
, and may be generalized to the situation where
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C}
is an arbitrary smooth formal complex surface containing
C
as scheme of definition.
2
2

2

To stay closer to the geometric intuition and to the classical results in
[
And63
]
and
[
Nor83
]
, we stick to the “analytic” point of view and mention the “formal” results only incidentally. The comparison of the constructions in the formal and analytic contexts actually leads to delicate questions; see Subsection
2.2.4
.

The formal-analytic arithmetic surfaces investigated in this memoir are precisely the arithmetic counterparts of these formal complex surfaces
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C}
admitting a projective algebraic curve
C
as scheme of definition. The pseudoconcave formal-analytic arithmetic surfaces, to which our main results in Part 3 are devoted, are the counterparts of those formal complex surfaces
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C}
such that the normal bundle of
C
in
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C}
satisfies a suitable positivity assumption, whose complex analytic avatars are investigated in Part 1.

Moreover the elementary character of our algebro-geometric arguments in Part 1 — by opposition to the transcendental arguments in
[
And63
]
and
[
Nor83
]
— explains why we are able to transpose
them in the arithmetic setting of Part 3.

A superficial reading of Chapter 1 should be enough to grasp the geometric ideas underlying this memoir. Its simple framework — with the above notation, the projective curve
C
in the complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
is supposed to be smooth — is however too restrictive when one wishes to pursue in detail the comparison with the arithmetic situation investigated in Parts 2 and 3. More general versions of the results of Chapter 1, which constitute a more complete analogue of our later arithmetic results, will be established in Chapter 2.

1.1.
Degree of morphisms between projective surfaces and the Hodge index inequality

The following proposition is a straightforward generalization of an estimate established by Nori in
[
Nor83
, Proof of Lemma 5.1]
. Its arithmetic conterpart in Arakelov geometry, stated in Theorem
3.5.10
below, will play a central role in this monograph.

Proposition 1.1.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two integral normal projective surfaces over an algebraically closed field
k
, and let:

f:Y\longrightarrow X

be a dominant morphism of
k
-schemes.

Let
B
be a divisor on
Y
, and let
A:=f_{*}B
be its direct image in
X
. If the self-intersection
B\cdot B
is positive, then the degree
\deg f:=[k(Y):k(X)]
of
f

satisfies the following inequality:

(1.1.1)

\deg f\leq\frac{A\cdot A}{B\cdot B}.

In (
1.1.1
),
A.A
and
B.B
denote the respective self-intersections of the Weil divisors
A
and
B
on
X
and
Y
respectively, defined by means of intersection theory on projective normal surfaces as in
[
Mum61
, II.(b)]
; see also
[
Ful98
, Example 7.1.16]
and
[
Bos99
, 2.4]
.

Proof.

Let us denote:

\delta:=\deg f.

For every divisor
D
on
X
, we have:

(1.1.2)

(f^{*}A-\delta B)\cdot f^{*}D=f^{*}A\cdot f^{*}D-\delta\,B\cdot f^{*}D=\delta\,A\cdot D-\delta\,f_{*}B\cdot D=0.

Applied to
D=A
, (
1.1.2
) implies:

(1.1.3)

(f^{*}A-\delta B)\cdot(f^{*}A-\delta B)=-\delta\,f^{*}A\cdot B+\delta^{2}\,B\cdot B=-\delta\,A\cdot f_{\ast}B+\delta^{2}\,B\cdot B=-\delta\,A\cdot A+\delta^{2}\,B\cdot B.

If
H
is an ample Cartier divisor on
X
, we have:

(1.1.4)

f^{*}H\cdot f^{*}H=\delta\,H\cdot H>0.

Moreover, according to (
1.1.2
) applied to
D=H
, we have:

(1.1.5)

(f^{*}A-\delta B)\cdot f^{*}H=0.

From (
1.1.4
), (
1.1.5
) and the Hodge index inequality on the projective surface
Y
, we obtain:

(f^{*}A-\delta B)\cdot(f^{*}A-\delta B)\leq 0.

Together with (
1.1.3
), this proves (
1.1.1
) when
B\cdot B
is positive.
∎

The following amplification of Proposition
1.1.1
will be useful in applications:

Proposition 1.1.2
.

Let
U
and
V
be two connected normal quasi-projective surfaces over an algebraically closed field
k
, and let:

f:V\longrightarrow U

be a dominant morphism of
k
-schemes.
Let
B
be a divisor on
V
, whose support is proper over
k
, and let
A:=f_{*}(B)
be its direct image.

If the self-intersection
B\cdot B
is positive, then the degree
\deg f:=[k(V):k(U)]
of
f

satisfies the following inequality:

(1.1.6)

\deg f\leq\frac{A\cdot A}{B\cdot B}.

Observe that the self-intersections
{A\cdot A}
and
{B\cdot B}
are well defined since the support of
B
, and therefore the support of
A
also, is proper over
k
.

Proof.

We may assume that
U
and
V
are open subschemes of some integral projective surfaces
X
and
Y
. After possibly replacing
X
by the closure in
X\times_{k}Y
of the closure of the graph of
f
, we may also assume that the morphism
f
extends to a
k
-morphism from
X
to
Y
. Finally, after possibly replacing
X
and
Y
by their normalization, we may also assume that they are normal. Then (
1.1.1
) follows from Proposition
1.1.1
applied to the morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
.
∎

1.2.
Applications: connectedness theorems and Nori’s theorem on étale fundamental groups of nodal curves in smooth surfaces

1.2.1.

With the notation of Proposition
1.1.1
, let
C
be a closed integral
1
-dimensional subscheme of
Y
such that the restriction of
f
to
C
defines an isomorphism:

(1.2.1)

f_{\mid C}:C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}f(C).

Assume additionally that
f
is étale at every point of
C
or, equivalently, that
f
defines an isomorphism:

\widehat{Y}_{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{X}_{f(C)}

between the formal completions of
Y
and
X
along
C
and
f(C)
respectively.

In this situation, the self-intersection numbers
C\cdot C
and
f(C)\cdot f(C)
coincide, and the inequality (
1.1.1
) applied to
B=C
asserts that,
when the self-intersection
C\cdot C
is positive, then

\deg f=1
, or equivalently
the morphism
f
is birational
. When this holds,
f
induces an isomorphism between some Zariski open neighborhoods of
C
and
f(C)
in
Y
and
X
respectively, and the following equality holds:

f^{-1}(f(C))=C.

In particular

f^{-1}(f(C))
is connected. Conversely, since
f
is étale at every point of
C
, the connectedness of
f^{-1}(f(C))
implies that
f
is birational.

This discussion shows that, in the situation we consider, the inequality (
1.1.1
) applied to
B=C
follows from the following connectedness statement à la Fulton-Hansen
3
3

3

see for instance
[
FL81
]
and
[
Laz04
, 3.3-4]
for presentations of these connectedness theorems and their applications and for references.
:
for every dominant morphism
f:Y\rightarrow X
of connected normal projective surfaces, and every closed integral subscheme
D
of
X
of dimension
1
such that
D.D>0
, the inverse image
f^{-1}(D)
is connected.

We refer to
[
Bos99
, Section 2]
for a more complete discussion of related connectedness theorems, due notably to Zariski and C.P. Ramanujam. Let us also indicate that arithmetic counterparts of these theorems, established in the context of Arakelov geometry, play a key role in
[
Bos99
]
and
[
BCL09
]
.

1.2.2.

Let us keep the notation of Proposition
1.1.1
and, for the sake of simplicity, let us assume that the surfaces
X
and
Y
are both smooth. Let
B
be a closed integral subscheme of dimension
1
of
Y
such that
the restriction:

f_{\mid B}:B\longrightarrow f(B)

is a birational morphism
.

The image
A:=f(B)
of
B
defines a Cartier divisor on
X
. It coincides with the cycle theoretic image
f_{\ast}B
, and the inverse image
f^{*}A
of
A
is an effective Cartier divisor on
Y
containing
B
as a component. It may be written:

f^{*}A=eB+R,

where
e
is a positive integer — this is by definition the ramification index of
f
along
B
— and
R
is an effective Cartier divisor on
Y
that intersects
B
properly.

We have the following equalities of intersection numbers:

A\cdot A=A\cdot f_{\ast}B=f^{\ast}A\cdot B=e\,B\cdot B+R\cdot B.

Together with Proposition
1.1.1
, this shows that,
if the self-intersection

B\cdot B:=\deg_{B}N_{B}Y

is positive, then:

(1.2.2)

\deg f\leq\frac{A\cdot A}{B\cdot B}=e\frac{A\cdot A}{A\cdot A-R\cdot B}=e+\frac{R\cdot B}{\deg_{B}N_{B}Y}.

1.2.3.

To highlight the geometric significance of the degree estimate (
1.2.2
), let us present its application by Nori to the fundamental group of nodal curves embedded in smooth projective surfaces.

In
[
Nor83
, section 5]
, Nori considers the situation of
1.2.2
, where moreover
A:=f(B)

is a nodal curve
, with
r(A)
singular points, and
f
is étale at every point of
B
.

Then
e=1
, and
B
also is a nodal curve, with
r(B)\leq r(A)
singular points. Moreover the effective divisor
R:=f^{\ast}A-B
intersects
B
transversally precisely at those smooth points
P
of
B
such that
A
is singular at
f(P)
.
Consequently:

R\cdot B=2(r(A)-r(B)),

and therefore:

B\cdot B=A\cdot A-R\cdot B=A\cdot A-2r(A)+2r(B)\geq A\cdot A-2r(A).

The conclusion of
1.2.2
shows that,
when moreover
A\cdot A>2r(A),
then
:

(1.2.3)

\deg f\leq\frac{A\cdot A}{A\cdot A-2r(A)}.

This upper bound on
\deg f
is the content of
[
Nor83
, Lemma 5.1]
. Remarkably the right-hand side of (
1.2.3
) depends only of the geometry of the curve
A
embedded in the surface
X
, and not on the morphism
f
. As pointed out by Nori, when applied to étale morphisms
f
, it may be rephrased as the following result concerning étale fundamental groups:
4
4

4

The derivation of Proposition
1.2.1
from the upper-bound (
1.2.3
) is similar to the derivation of Proposition
1.3.2
from the degree bound (
1.3.7
) below.

Proposition 1.2.1

(
[
Nor83
]
, Proposition 5.4)
.

Let
A
be a connected nodal projective curve embedded in a connected projective smooth surface
X
over some algebraically closed field
k
. Let us denote by
r(A)
its number of singular points, and by:

i\circ\nu:\widetilde{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\longrightarrow}}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}X

the normalization of
A
composed with the inclusion in
X
.

If the inequality:

A\cdot A>2r(A)

holds, then the image of the morphism of étale fundamental groups:

(1.2.4)

(i\circ\nu)_{\ast}:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{a})\longrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(X,x)

induced by
i\circ\nu
is a subgroup of finite index; moreover:

(1.2.5)

[\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(X,x):(i\circ\nu)_{\ast}(\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{a}))]\leq\frac{A\cdot A}{A\cdot A-2r(A)}.

In (
1.2.5
),
\widetilde{a}
denotes a geometric point of
\widetilde{A}
and
x
its image by
i\circ\nu
.

To put Proposition
1.2.1
in perspective, observe that, when
A
is smooth,
r(A)
vanishes, and Proposition
1.2.1
asserts that, if the self-intersection:

A\cdot A=\deg_{A}N_{A}X

is positive, then the inclusion morphism
i:A{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}X
induces a surjective morphism of étale fundamental groups:

i_{\ast}:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}({A},{a})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(X,x).

This surjectivity is a consequence of the Lefschetz theorems in SGA2, and actually holds for any integral curve
A
in
X
with positive self-intersection. It also follows from the connectedness results in
1.2.1
above; see
[
Gro68
]
and
[
Bos99
]
.

Observe also that, in striking contrast with
Nori’s finiteness result (
1.2.5
) and the surjectivity of
i_{\ast}
, the image of the morphism:

\nu_{\ast}:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{a})\longrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}({A},{a})

is a closed subgroup of
infinite
index when
r(A)
is positive.

1.3.
Analytic maps from analytic thickening of projective curves to algebraic surfaces

When
k=\mathbb{C}
, Nori establishes in
[
Nor83
]
various generalized versions of the finiteness results on étale fundamental groups in Proposition
1.2.1
. These generalizations concern topological fundamental groups, and rely on subtle arguments of deformation theory in complex analytic geometry.

It turns out that diverse variants of Nori’s results, concerning étale fundamental groups instead of topological fundamental groups, may be established by some variations on the simple algebraic arguments establishing the degree bound (
1.2.3
) and Proposition
1.2.1
.
These variants, like the more difficult transcendental results in
[
Nor83
]
, involve complex analytic maps from some auxiliary (germ of) complex analytic surface containing a projective curve, with range the complex algebraic varieties under study (which will play the role of
X
and
Y
in
1.2.2
, or of
X
in Proposition
1.2.1
).

In this subsection, we establish some simple but significant instances of these variants. They will constitute “geometric models” for the upper bound on the degree of a morphism between arithmetic surfaces and for the finiteness results concerning their étale fundamental group established in Theorems
8.1.2
and
9.2.1
below.

The framework of this section and of the next one, where we consider an auxiliary analytic surface containing a
smooth
projective curve, makes the derivation of our results especially straightforward. As discussed in
1.4.3
below, this simple framework also highlights the relation between the geometric results in this first part — and consequently of their arithmetic counterpart established in this article — with the classical properties of pseudoconcave complex analytic spaces, introduced in the seminal works of Andreotti and Grauert (
[
AG62
]
,
[
And63
]
).

1.3.1.

We place ourself in a special instance of Proposition
1.1.2
with
k=\mathbb{C}
, and we consider a dominant morphism:

f:V\longrightarrow U

of connected smooth quasi-projective complex surfaces.

We also consider a connected smooth complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
containing a smooth connected projective curve
C
, and we assume that we are given complex analytic maps
\alpha
and
\beta
from
{\mathcal{V}}
to
U
and
V
respectively, that fit into a commutative diagram:

(1.3.1)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise We finally assume that
the image
\alpha({\mathcal{V}})
of
\alpha
is Zariski dense in
U
— this holds if and only if
\alpha
is étale at some point of
{\mathcal{V}}
, or equivalently outside a proper closed analytic subset of
{\mathcal{V}}
— and that
the restriction of
\alpha
to
C
:

\alpha_{\mid C}:C\longrightarrow\alpha(C)

is birational.
This implies that
f
is dominant, that the image
\beta({\mathcal{V}})
of
\beta
is Zariski dense in
V
, and that the restriction of
\beta
to
C
:

\beta_{\mid C}:C\longrightarrow\beta(C)

is birational.

Then we
may define the ramification index
e(\alpha)
of
\alpha
along
C
by the equality of analytic divisors on
{\mathcal{V}}
:

(1.3.2)

\alpha^{*}(\alpha(C))=e(\alpha)\,C+R,

where
R
is an effective analytic divisor on
{\mathcal{V}}
that intersects
C
properly. The integer
e(\alpha)
is positive, the intersection number
R\cdot C
is non-negative, and the following equalities of intersection numbers hold:

(1.3.3)

\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)=C\cdot\alpha^{*}(\alpha(C))=e(\alpha)\,C\cdot C+R\cdot C.

As a consequence, when the self-intersection:

C\cdot C=\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}

is positive, we have:

(1.3.4)

\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)\geq e(\alpha)\,C\cdot C>0,

and, similarly:

(1.3.5)

\beta(C)\cdot\beta(C)\geq e(\beta)\,C\cdot C>0.

Finally, Proposition
1.1.1
applied to
B=\beta(C)
and
A=\alpha(C)
, together with the relations (
1.3.3
) and (
1.3.5
), establishes the following:

Proposition 1.3.1
.

With the above notation, if the self-intersection
C\cdot C=\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}
is positive, then:

(1.3.6)

\deg f\leq\frac{\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)}{e(\beta)\,C\cdot C}=\frac{e(\alpha)}{e(\beta)}+\frac{R\cdot C}{e(\beta)\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}},

and consequently:

(1.3.7)

\deg f\leq\frac{\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)}{C\cdot C}=e(\alpha)+\frac{R\cdot C}{\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}.

1.3.2.

In turn, the degree bound (
1.3.7
) implies the following result concerning étale fundamental groups:

Proposition 1.3.2

(compare
[
Nor83
]
, Weak Lefschetz Theorem, B and C)
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a smooth connected complex analytic surface containing a smooth connected projective curve
C
, and let:

\alpha:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow{U}

be a complex analytic map, with Zariski dense image, from
{\mathcal{V}}
to some smooth connected complex algebraic surface
{U}
.

If the morphism

\alpha_{\mid C}:C\rightarrow\alpha(C)

is birational and the self-intersection

C\cdot C=\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}

is positive, then the image of the morphism between étale fundamental groups:

(1.3.8)

\alpha_{\mid C\ast}:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(C,c)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}({U},x)

induced by
\alpha_{\mid C}:C\rightarrow{U}

is a subgroup of finite index; moreover:

(1.3.9)

[\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}({U},x):\alpha_{\mid C\ast}(\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(C,c))]\leq\frac{\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)}{C\cdot C}=e(\alpha)+\frac{R\cdot C}{\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}.

In (
1.3.8
),
c
denotes a complex point of
C
, and
x:=\alpha(C)
its image in
{U}
. In the right-hand side of (
1.3.9
), the ramification index
e(\alpha)
and the effective analytic divisor
R
— which meets
C
properly — are defined as above by (
1.3.2
).

The degree bound (
1.3.7
) and Proposition
1.3.2
contain the results in
1.2.1
and
1.2.3
when
k=\mathbb{C}
as special cases.
5
5

5

Concerning
1.2.1
, at least when
X,Y
and
C
are assumed to be smooth.

Indeed, to recover the equality
\deg f=1
in
1.2.1
, simply apply the bound (
1.3.7
) to
{\mathcal{V}}=Y,

\alpha=f
, and
\beta={\mathrm{Id}}_{Y}
.
To recover the results on nodal curves in
1.2.3
, apply (
1.3.7
) and Proposition
1.3.2
with
{\mathcal{V}}
a “tubular neighborhood” of the morphism
i\circ\nu:\widetilde{A}\rightarrow X
, and with
\alpha
the canonical morphism from
{\mathcal{V}}
to
U:=X
, which is étale and coincides with
\nu
on
C:=\widetilde{A}
; see Figure
1
. Then
e(\alpha)=1
and the self-intersection
C\cdot C=\widetilde{A}\cdot\widetilde{A}
is easily seen to be
A\cdot A-2r(A)
.

\alpha

A
\widetilde{A}
{\mathcal{V}}

Figure 1.
An analytic tubular neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of the immersion

.

Proof of Proposition
1.3.2
.

To establish the finiteness of the index:

[\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}({U},x):\alpha_{\mid C\ast}(\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(C,c))]

and the upper bound (
1.3.9
), we have to show that any open subgroup
H
of the profinite group
G:=\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}({U},x)
that contains the closed subgroup
\alpha_{\mid C\ast}(\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(C,c))
satisfies:

(1.3.10)

[G:H]\leq\frac{\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)}{C\cdot C}.

Any such subgroup
H
gives rise to a commutative diagram:

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.38266pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{U},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
{V}
is a smooth connected complex algebraic surface,
{f}
is a finite étale morphism of degree

(1.3.11)

\deg{f}=[G:H],

and where
\beta_{\mid C}
is a morphism of complex algebraic varieties
6
6

6

Since the curve
C
is projective, by GAGA any complex analytic map from
C
to a complex algebraic variety defines a morphism of complex algebraic varieties.
.

After possibly shrinking
{\mathcal{V}}
, we may assume that the inclusion
C\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
induces an isomorphism of fundamental groups. Since
{f}
defines a complex analytic unramified covering, the map
\beta_{\mid C}
uniquely extends to some complex analytic map:

\beta:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow{V}

such that the following diagram is commutative:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.07013pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.0979pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{f}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.0979pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.94957pt\raise-26.82637pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.05359pt\raise-9.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.23364pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{U}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Since the image of
\alpha
is Zariski dense in
{U}
, when moreover
\alpha_{\mid C}
is birational and the self-intersection
C\cdot C
is positive, the upper bound
(
1.3.7
) applies to
f
and therefore:

\deg f\leq\frac{\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)}{C\cdot C}.

Combined with (
1.3.11
), this completes the proof of (
1.3.10
).
∎

1.4.
Germs of pseudoconcave analytic surfaces

1.4.1.
Sections of line bundles on germs of pseudoconcave analytic surfaces: dimension estimates and a new proof of the degree bound (
1.3.7
)

Proposition
1.3.1
admits an alternative proof which relies on the special properties of the analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
— more precisely of its germ along the projective curve
C
— when the condition:

(1.4.1)

\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}>0.

is satisfied. The positivity condition (
1.4.1
) turns out to be closely related to the pseudoconcavity properties of the germ of the analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
along the projective curve
C
; we shall discuss this in more details in
1.4.3
below.

The central ingredient of this alternative proof will be the following finiteness result concerning sections of analytic line bundles on the analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proposition 1.4.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a smooth connected complex analytic surface containing a smooth connected projective curve
C
such that the positivity condition (
1.4.1
) is satisfied. For every complex analytic line bundle
M
over
{\mathcal{V}}
, the complex vector space
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
of holomorphic sections of
M
over
{\mathcal{V}}
is finite dimensional. Moreover:

(1.4.2)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)\leq C(\deg_{C}M_{|C}),

where, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let:

(1.4.3)

C(n):=\sum_{i\geq 0}(n+1-i\deg N_{C}{\mathcal{V}})^{+}.

In particular, the following implication holds:

(1.4.4)

\deg_{C}M_{\mid C}<0\Longrightarrow\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)=0,

and when the integer
D
goes to
+\infty
, we have:

(1.4.5)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M^{\otimes D})\leq\frac{(\deg_{C}M_{|C})^{2}}{2\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}D^{2}+O(D).

Proposition
1.4.1
will be a consequence of the following elementary fact concerning spaces of sections of analytic lines bundles over a smooth projective complex curve:

for every complex analytic line bundle
L
over
C
, the complex vector space
\Gamma(C,L)
of its complex analytic sections is finite dimensional; moreover:

(1.4.6)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma(C,L)\leq\left(1+\deg_{C}L\right)^{+}.

The reader may compare the following proof to
[
CP16
, Lemma 4.2]
and
[
Bos20
, 10.2.4]
.

Proof.

The complex vector space
E:=\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
of analytic sections of
M
over
{\mathcal{V}}

admits a decreasing filtration
(E^{i})_{i\geq 0}
defined by the order of vanishing along
C
, namely by setting:

E^{i}:=\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M(-iC)),\quad\mbox{for every $i\in\mathbb{N}.$}

For any nonnegative integer
i
, the space
E^{i+1}
is the kernel of the restriction map:

\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M(-iC))\longrightarrow\Gamma(C,M(-iC)_{|C})\simeq\Gamma(C,M_{\mid C}\otimes N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes(-i)}).

As recalled above, the vector space
\Gamma(C,M(-iC)_{|C})
is finite-dimensional, of dimension bounded above by:

\left(1+\deg_{C}M(-iC)_{|C}\right)^{+}=\left(1+\deg_{C}M_{|C}-i\deg N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}\right)^{+}.

This implies the estimate:

(1.4.7)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}E^{i}/E^{i+1}\leq\left(1+\deg_{C}M_{|C}-i\deg N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}\right)^{+}

The positivity of
\deg N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}
implies that, when
i
is large enough, the right-hand side of (
1.4.7
) vanishes. Moreover
E^{0}=E
and the intersection of the
E^{i}
’s is reduced to
\{0\}
. This proves that
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
is finite-dimensional, as well as the upper bound (
1.4.2
).

From the definition (
1.4.3
) of
C(n)
, it is readily seen that
C(n)
vanishes when
n
is negative, which implies (
1.4.4
). Moreover we have:

C(0)=1

and, when
n
goes to
+\infty
:

C(n)=\int_{0}^{\frac{n+1}{\deg N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}}(n+1-x\deg N_{C}{\mathcal{V}})\,dx+O(n)=\frac{n^{2}}{2\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}+O(n).

This implies the asymptotic estimate (
1.4.5
).
∎

Corollary 1.4.2
.

If
\gamma:{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow Z
is a complex analytic map, with Zariski dense image, from
{\mathcal{V}}
to an integral projective complex surface
Z
, and if
L
is a big and nef line bundle
L
over
Z
, the following inequality holds:

(1.4.8)

L\cdot L\leq\frac{(\deg_{C}\gamma^{*}L)^{2}}{\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}.

Proof.

For any integer
D
, we may consider the “evaluation map”:

\eta_{D}:\Gamma(Z,L^{\otimes D})\longrightarrow\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},\gamma^{*}L_{|C}^{\otimes D})

defined by sending a section
s
of the line bundle
L^{\otimes D}
over
Z
to its pull-back
\gamma^{\ast}s
, which is an analytic section of the complex analytic line bundle
\gamma^{\ast}L^{\otimes D}
over
{\mathcal{V}}
. Since
\gamma
has Zariski dense image,
\eta_{D}
is injective, so that:

(1.4.9)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma(Z,L^{\otimes D})\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},\gamma^{*}L^{\otimes D}).

Moreover, since
L
is big and nef, when
D
goes to
+\infty
, we have:

(1.4.10)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma(Z,L^{\otimes D})\sim\frac{L\cdot L}{2}D^{2}.

The inequality (
1.4.8
) follows from (
1.4.9
) and the asymptotic relations (
1.4.10
) and (
1.4.5
) by letting
D
go to infinity.
∎

Alternative proof of the degree bound (
1.3.7
).

We keep the notation of
1.3.1
. As observed in the proof of Proposition
1.1.2
, we may introduce projective compactifications
X
and
Y
of
U
and
V
such that
f
extends to a morphism from
X
to
Y
. The diagram:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.38194pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.39583pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.39583pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.09853pt\raise-26.82637pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.2437pt\raise-9.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.48323pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

is clearly commutative.

As shown by (
1.3.4
), the self-intersection
\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)
is positive, and therefore the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\alpha(C))
on
X
is big and nef. Since
f
is dominant, its pull-back:

L:=f^{*}\mathcal{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}(\alpha(C))

is a big and nef line bundle over
Y
. Its self-intersection satisfies:

L\cdot L=f^{*}\mathcal{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}(\alpha(C))\cdot f^{*}\mathcal{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X}(\alpha(C))=(\deg f)\,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\alpha(C))\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\alpha(C))=(\deg f)\,\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C).

Moreover the degree along
C
of its pull-back by
\beta
is:

\deg_{C}\beta^{\ast}L=\deg_{C}\beta^{\ast}f^{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\alpha(C))=\deg_{C}\alpha^{\ast}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\alpha(C))=\alpha^{\ast}(\alpha(C))\cdot C=\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C).

Therefore, applied to the map
\beta:{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow Y
and to the line bundle
f^{\ast}L
over
Y
, Corollary
1.4.2
establishes the inequality:

(\deg f)\,\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)\leq\frac{(\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C))^{2}}{\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}}.

Since
\alpha(C)\cdot\alpha(C)
is positive, this is equivalent to (
1.3.7
).
∎

1.4.2.
Sections of line bundles on germs of pseudoconcave analytic surfaces: application to algebraicity

Remarkably enough, a simple variation on the above proof of Corollary
1.4.2
allows one to derive the following algebraicity result from Proposition
1.4.1
:

Proposition 1.4.3

(compare
[
Bos20
]
, Theorem 10.2.8)
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a smooth connected complex analytic surface containing a smooth connected projective curve
C
such that
\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}
is positive.

If
\gamma:{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow Z
is a complex analytic map from
{\mathcal{V}}
to some complex quasi-projective variety
Z
, then either
f({\mathcal{V}})
coincide with
f(C)
—
hence is a point or an irreducible projective curve
— or is contained, as a Zariski dense subset, in some irreducible closed algebraic surface in
Z
.

Proof.

We shall actually only use the following weak form the dimension estimates of Proposition
1.4.1
:

(1.4.11)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M^{\otimes D})=O(D^{2})\quad\mbox{when $D$ goes to $+\infty$}.

To establish Proposition
1.4.3
, we may clearly assume that
Z
is projective and reduced, and that
f({\mathcal{V}})
is Zariski dense in
Z
, which is therefore irreducible since
{\mathcal{V}}
is connected. We shall prove the estimate:

d:=\dim Z\leq 2.

This will complete the proof when
d=2
, and we shall leave to the reader the easy proof that
f({\mathcal{V}})=f(C)
when
d\leq 1
.

To achieve this, let us choose an ample line bundle
L
over
Z
, and consider the evaluation maps:

\eta_{D}:\Gamma(Z,L^{\otimes D})\longrightarrow\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},\gamma^{*}L_{|C}^{\otimes D}),

as in the proof of Corollary
1.4.2
. They are injective, and the estimates (
1.4.9
) still holds. Moreover, since
L
is ample, we have:

(1.4.12)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma(Z,L^{\otimes D})\sim\frac{\deg c_{1}(L)^{d}\cap[Z]}{d!}\,D^{d}\quad\mbox{when $D$ goes to $+\infty$}.

The inequality
d\leq 2
follows from (
1.4.9
) and from the asymptotic relations (
1.4.11
) and (
1.4.12
) by letting
D
go to infinity.
∎

The derivation of the dimension estimates in Proposition
1.4.1
and its application to algebraicity in Proposition
1.4.3
only involve the germ
{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C}
of the analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
along the projective curve
C
. Actually they may be formulated in terms of the
formal germ

\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
along
C
and, in this form, may be generalized to formal germs of surfaces along a projective curves, defined over an arbitrary algebraically closed field
k
. Indeed the above proofs of Proposition
1.4.1
and
1.4.3
, which only involve a few basic results of algebraic geometry, immediately extends to this framework; see
[
Har68
, section 5]
,
[
Bos01
, section 3.3]
, and
[
Bos20
, section 10.2.4]
for related results in this context.

1.4.3.
Positivity of
\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}
and pseudoconcavity

In the complex analytic setting, several of the results established in the previous subsections are actually consequences of classical theorems of complex geometry, established by analytic techniques, concerning pseudoconcave complex analytic manifolds. This alternative approach sheds some light on the geometric meaning of the key positivity assumption (
1.4.1
), and we want to discuss it briefly.
7
7

7

We assume some familiarity with complex analysis in several complex variables at the level of the last chapters in
[
Gun90
]
, or of
[
FG02
, Chapters I to V]
.

Consider
a smooth connected complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
containing a smooth connected projective curve
C
, as in Proposition
1.4.1
, and let us choose a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|
on the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(C)
over
{\mathcal{V}}
. Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
C
that is relatively compact in
{\mathcal{V}}
. For every
r
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}
, we may consider the “tube of radius
r
around
C
in
{\mathcal{V}}
”:

\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r}:=\{x\in U\mid\|\mathbf{1}_{C}(x)\|\leq r\}.

There exists
r_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast}
such that, for every
r\in(0,r_{0}],

{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
is a compact
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
submanifold with boundary of
{\mathcal{V}}
, with interior:

{{\mathcal{V}}_{r}}:=\{x\in U\mid\|\mathbf{1}_{C}(x)\|<r\}

containing
C
as a deformation retract, and with boundary:

\partial{\mathcal{V}}_{r}:=\{x\in U\mid\|\mathbf{1}_{C}(x)\|=r\}.

The following proposition is established by a straightforward computation that we leave as an exercise for the reader:

Proposition 1.4.4
.

If the metric
\|.\|_{\mid C}
on the line bundle
N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(C)_{|C}
over
C
, deduced by restriction from
\|.\|
, satisfies the pointwise positivity condition:

(1.4.13)

c_{1}(N_{C}{\mathcal{V}},\|.\|_{|C})>0\quad\mbox{ over $C$,}

then there exists
r_{1}\in(0,r_{0}]
such that, for every
r\in(0,r_{1})
, the Levi form of the boundary
\partial{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
of
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r}
is everywhere negative.

The existence of a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{V}}}(C)
satisfying the positivity condition (
1.4.13
) is easily seen to be equivalent to the positivity (
1.4.1
) of
\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}
.
Moreover the negativity of the Levi form of
\partial{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
is a
pseudoconcavity
condition: it asserts that locally, around every boundary points of
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r}
, there exists a holomorphic chart that maps
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r}
to the complement of a strictly convex domain with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
boundary.

For these reasons, we refer
to the positivity condition (
1.4.1
) on
\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}

as a pseudoconcavity condition.

From now on, we assume that the positivity condition (
1.4.1
) holds,
that the Hermitian metric
\|.\|
satisfies (
1.4.13
), and that
r_{1}
satisfies the conclusion of Proposition
1.4.4
.

An application of Hartogs’ extension theorem at boundary points of
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
shows that, for every analytic line bundle
M
over
{\mathcal{V}}
and for every
r\in(0,r_{1})
, an analytic section of
M
over
{{\mathcal{V}}_{r}}
extends analytically to some open neighborhood of
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r}
in
{\mathcal{V}}
. This implies that, for every
r\in(0,r_{1}),
the restriction map:

\Gamma({{\mathcal{V}}}_{r_{1}},M)\longrightarrow\Gamma({{\mathcal{V}}}_{r},M)

is an isomorphism.
Consequently, if we introduce the space of germs of analytic sections of
M
along
C
:

\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C},M):=\varinjlim_{r>0}\Gamma({{\mathcal{V}}}_{r},M),

then, for every open connected submanifold
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
such that:

C\subset{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r_{1}},

the restriction map defines an isomorphism:

\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}^{\prime},M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C},M).

Similarly, E. E. Levi’s extension theorem
8
8

8

concerning the extension of meromorphic functions across pseudoconcave boundaries; see for instance
[
Siu74
, Chapter 1]
.
implies that the restriction map defines an isomorphism:

{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C})

between the algebra of meromorphic functions on
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
and the algebra of germs of meromorphic functions along
C
.

The negativity of the Levi form of
\partial{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
also implies that, for every
r\in(0,r_{1})
, the complex manifold
{{\mathcal{V}}}_{r}
is pseudoconcave in the sense of Andreotti
[
And63
]
. As shown in
loc. cit.
, this implies by purely analytic arguments that, for every complex analytic line bundle
M
over
{\mathcal{V}}
, the space of analytic sections
\Gamma({{\mathcal{V}}}_{r},M)
— which, as observed above, is independent of
r\in(0,r_{1})
— is finite dimensional. It also implies the dimension estimates:

\dim_{C}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}_{r},M^{\otimes D})=O(D^{2})\quad\mbox{when $D$ goes to $+\infty$}.

Andreotti also proves that the pseudoconcavity of
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
implies that the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}_{r})(\simeq{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}_{C}^{{\rm an}})
) of meromorphic functions over
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
is an extension of finite type of
\mathbb{C}
and satisfies:

\deg{\rm tr}_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}_{r})\leq\dim{\mathcal{V}}_{r}=2.

He deduces from this fact the “algebraicity” of the image of a complex analytic embedding of
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
in a complex projective space.

For later comparison with the arithmetic situation (see
6.4.2
below), let us emphasize the contrast between the properties of pseudoconcave germs of analytic surfaces that we have just discussed, and the ones of pseudoconvex germs, which one encounters when one replaces the positivity condition (
1.4.1
) by the opposite condition:

(1.4.14)

\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}<0.

When (
1.4.14
) holds, we may choose the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(C)
such that, instead of (
1.4.13
),
it satisfies:

(1.4.15)

c_{1}(N_{C}{\mathcal{V}},\|.\|_{|C})<0\quad\mbox{ over $C$,}

and the computation leading to Proposition
1.4.4
shows that, for some
r_{1}\in(0,r_{0}]
, the Levi form of
\partial{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
is everywhere positive for
r\in(0,r_{1})
. In other words, the
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
have strictly pseudoconvex boundary.

As shown by Grauert in
[
Gra62
]
, this implies that the
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
are holomorphically convex, and actually that there exists a “contraction” of the curve
C
, namely a proper holomorphic map:

c:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{S}}

with range a normal complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{S}}
, that maps
C
to a point
O
of
{\mathcal{S}}
and induces an isomorphism of complex manifolds:

c_{\mid{\mathcal{V}}\setminus C}:{\mathcal{V}}\setminus C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{S}}\setminus\{O\}.

Moreover, for every
r\in(0,r_{1}),
the image:

{\mathcal{S}}_{r}:=c({\mathcal{V}}_{r})

of
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
is a Stein space, containing
{\mathcal{S}}_{r^{\prime}}
as a Runge subdomain for every
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
.
The pull-back of functions by the contraction
c
establishes isomorphisms of algebras:

c^{\ast}:\Gamma({\mathcal{S}}_{r},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{S}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}_{r},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}),

and therefore the algebras
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}_{r},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}})
for
r\in(0,r_{1})
are “very large” — notably every pair of points in
{\mathcal{V}}_{r}
, not both in
C
, may be separated by a function in
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}_{r},{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}})
— and increase strictly when
r
decreases to zero.

This is in striking contrast with the finite dimensionality and the independence of
r
of the spaces
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}}_{r},M)
and
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}_{r})
in the pseudoconcave case discussed in
1.4.3
above.

Chapter 2
\mathbf{CNB}
-divisors and fibered analytic surfaces

In the analogy between number fields and function fields, the counterpart of algebraic varieties over a number field
K
are varieties over the function field
k(S)
of some curve
S
over a base field
k
, or equivalently the generic fibers of
k
-varieties that are fibered over
S
. From this perspective, the geometric analogues of the arithmetic results established in this memoir specifically concern analytic and algebraic varieties fibered over a projective curve. However, in this “fibered” context, the simple framework of Subsections
1.3.1
,
1.3.2
, and
1.4.1
excludes significant examples. Indeed the self-intersection of sections of surfaces fibered over a projective curve is in general negative, as established by Arakelov
[
Ara71
]
and Szpiro
[
Szp81
, Chapitre III]
.

It turns out that the results in Subsections
1.3.1
,
1.3.2
, and
1.4.1
, dealing with a smooth connected projective curve
C
embedded in a smooth analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
, may be extended to situations where
C
is replaced by a general connected projective curve, possibly non-reduced, satisfying a suitable positivity property that generalizes the positivity (
1.4.1
) of the self-intersection of
C
.

These generalizations of our previous results will allow us to establish proper analogues of our later arithmetic results in Sections
2.2
and
2.3
. These are established by some amplifications of our earlier arguments in Subsections
1.3.1
,
1.3.2
, and
1.4.1
, which still only relies on basic properties of algebraic curves and surfaces.

The content of this chapter is more technical than the one of Chapter 1, and could be skipped by a reader mainly interested by the arithmetic results of this memoir.

The reader with an interest in complex analytic geometry will observe that the analytic discussion in Subsection
1.4.3
, where the positivity of
\deg_{C}N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}
is related to the pseudoconcavity properties of the (germ of) analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
, is not generalized to the more general framework of this chapter, although it seems likely that it could be.

For instance one expects that, if an effective divisor
D
in a connected smooth complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
satisfies the condition
\mathbf{CNB}
introduced in Subsection
2.1.1
below, then
its support
|D|
admits a basis of open neighborhoods in
{\mathcal{V}}
that are pseudoconcave. This would follow from a suitable generalization of the simple argument on Levi forms in Proposition
1.4.4
.

However, at this stage, we do not know whether such a basis of pseudoconcave open neighborhoods exists for a general
\mathbf{CNB}
divisor. This difficulty with the analytic approach confers a special interest to the algebraic approach in this chapter when dealing with general
\mathbf{CNB}
divisors.

2.1.
Compact, connected, nef and big effective divisors in analytic surfaces

2.1.1.
The condition
\mathbf{CNB}
and its consequences

In this section, we denote by
{\mathcal{V}}
a connected smooth complex analytic surface. For every effective divisor
D
in
{\mathcal{V}}
, we may introduce the following conditions:

\mathbf{CNB}
:
The support
|D|
of
D
is compact and connected.

If we denote by
(D_{i})_{i\in I}
the irreducible components of
D
, then we have:

(2.1.1)

\mbox{\emph{for every }$i\in I$, }D_{i}\cdot D\geq 0.

Moreover:

(2.1.2)

D\cdot D>0.

When
|D|
is compact and connected, the non-negativity condition (
2.1.1
) is equivalent to the following one:

(2.1.3)

\mbox{\emph{for every effective divisor $\widetilde{D}$ in ${\mathcal{V}}$, } }\widetilde{D}\cdot D\geq 0.

When the first two conditions in
\mathbf{CNB}
are satisfied, the validity of (
2.1.2
) is equivalent to the existence of
i\in I
such that the inequality (
2.1.1
) is strict.

Observe also that, when the surface
{\mathcal{V}}
is projective, an effective divisor
D
satisfies the conditions
\mathbf{CNB}
if and only if
D
, or equivalently the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(D)
, is nef and big.
1
1

1

The connectedness of
|D|
when
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(D)
is nef and big follows from the Hodge index inequality, by an argument of C.P. Ramanujam which actually establishes that
D
is numerically connected. See for instance
[
Szp81
, Chapter II]
or
[
Bos99
, Proposition 2.2]
.
The name
\mathbf{CNB}
for the above condition stands for
c
ompact, connected,
n
ef and
b
ig, although the general formalism of big line bundles and divisors does not really makes sense on a general analytic surface.

From now on, we suppose that
D
is an effective divisor in
{\mathcal{V}}
that satisfies the conditions
\mathbf{CNB}
, we denote by
C
an irreducible component of
D
such that:

(2.1.4)

C\cdot D>0,

and we denote by
\mu
the multiplicity of
C
in
D
. We may decompose
D
as a sum:

(2.1.5)

D=\mu\,C+E,

where
E
is an effective divisor with compact support not containing
C
.

Proposition 2.1.1
.

For every complex analytic line bundle
M
over
{\mathcal{V}}
, the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space of its analytic sections
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
is finite dimensional. Moreover:

(2.1.6)

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)\leq C(c_{1}(M)\cdot D)

where, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
:

(2.1.7)

C(n):=\sum_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor n/C\cdot D\rfloor}\left(1+\lfloor(n-i\,C\cdot D)/\mu\rfloor\right)^{+}.

Observe that we still have
C(n)=0
if
n<0,
and
C(0)=1
, and that:

C(n)\sim\frac{n^{2}}{2\mu\,C\cdot D},\quad\mbox{when $n\longrightarrow+\infty$}.

Proof.

Let us choose a point
P
in
C_{\mathrm{reg}}.

As in the proof of Proposition
1.4.1
, we may consider the decreasing filtration
(E^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
defined by:

E^{i}:=\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(-iC)).

For every
i\in\mathbb{N},
we may also consider the restriction map:

\eta^{i}:E^{i}=\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(-iC))\longrightarrow\Gamma(C,(M\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(-iC))_{|C})=\Gamma(C,M_{|C}\otimes N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes(-i)})

defined by:

\eta^{i}(s):=s_{|C}.

Its kernel is
E^{i+1}
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we may introduce the
closed subscheme of
C
, supported by
P
, defined by the effective Cartier divisor
kP
in
C
. We shall denote it by
kP
, and consider the composite map:

{\eta}^{i}_{k}:E^{i}/E^{i+1}\longrightarrow\Gamma(C,M_{|C}\otimes N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes(-i)})\longrightarrow(M_{|C}\otimes N_{C}{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes(-i)})_{|kP},

defined by:

{\eta}^{i}_{k}([s]):=(\eta^{i}(s))_{|kP}.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

The map
\eta^{i}_{k}
is injective if the following inequality holds:

i\,C\cdot D+\mu k>c_{1}(M)\cdot D.

Proof of Lemma
2.1.2
.

Let us consider a non-zero class
[s]
in
E^{i}/E^{i+1}
that lies in
\ker{\eta}^{i}_{k}
.
The divisor of the analytic section
s
of
M
may be written:

(2.1.8)

{\mathrm{div}\,}s=iC+\widetilde{D}+R,

where
\widetilde{D}
and
R
are effective divisor, where
\widetilde{D}
is supported on
|E|
and
R
meets
D
properly.
The scheme-theoretic intersection
R\cap C
contains
kP
as a subscheme, and consequently:

R\cdot C\geq k.

Consequently:

R\cdot D=\mu\,R\cdot C+R\cdot E\geq\mu k.

Moreover, as already observed, the non-negativity condition (
2.1.1
) on
D
implies:

\widetilde{D}\cdot D\geq 0.

Together with (
2.1.8
), this implies:

c_{1}(M)\cdot D={\mathrm{div}\,}s\cdot D\geq i\,C\cdot D+\mu k.\qed

According to Lemma
2.1.2
, we have:

i\geq\lfloor(c_{1}(M)\cdot D)/(C\cdot D)\rfloor+1\Longrightarrow E^{i}/E^{i+1}=\{0\},

and:

i\leq\lfloor(c_{1}(M)\cdot D)/(C\cdot D)\rfloor\Longrightarrow\dim_{\mathbb{C}}E^{i}/E^{i+1}\leq 1+\lfloor(c_{1}(M)\cdot D-i\,C\cdot D)/\mu\rfloor.

Since the filtration
(E^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

E^{0}=\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
and
\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}E^{i}=\{0\}
, this implies the finite dimensionality of
\Gamma({\mathcal{V}},M)
and the upper bound (
2.1.6
) on its dimension.
∎

Proposition
2.1.1
notably implies the validity of the “weak dimension estimates” (
1.4.11
). In turn, it leads to the following generalization of the algebraicity result in Proposition
1.4.3
by a straightforward modification of its proof:

Proposition 2.1.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a smooth connected complex analytic surface containing an effective divisor
D
satisfying condition
\mathbf{CNB}
.
If
\gamma:{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow Z
is a complex analytic map from
{\mathcal{V}}
to some complex quasi-projective variety
Z
, then either
f
is constant, or
f({\mathcal{V}})
coincides with
f(|D|)
and is an irreducible projective curve, or
f({\mathcal{V}})
is contained, as a Zariski dense subset, in some irreducible closed algebraic surface in
Z
.

The computations of intersection numbers in Subsection
1.3.1
also may be generalized to the present setting.

Proposition 2.1.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a smooth connected complex analytic surface containing an effective divisor
D
satisfying condition
\mathbf{CNB}
, and let us keep the notation introduced in (
2.1.4
) and (
2.1.5
) above. For every complex analytic map
\gamma:{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow Z
with Zariski dense image from
{\mathcal{V}}
to a connected smooth complex algebraic surface
Z
, such that
\gamma_{\mid C}:C\rightarrow\gamma(C)
is birational, the following estimates are satisfied:

(2.1.9)

\gamma_{\ast}D\cdot\gamma_{\ast}D\geq e(\gamma)\,\mu\,C\cdot D\geq\mu\,C\cdot D,

where
e(\gamma)
denotes the ramification index of
\gamma
along
C
.

Proof.

According to the projection formula, the following equality of intersection numbers holds:

\gamma_{\ast}D\cdot\gamma_{\ast}D=\gamma^{\ast}\gamma_{\ast}D\cdot D.

Moreover we have:

\gamma_{\ast}C=\gamma(C),

and, according to the very definition of
e(\gamma),
the irreducible curve
C
occurs with multiplicity
e(\gamma)
in the effective divisor
\gamma^{\ast}\gamma(C).

This implies the following inequality of divisors on
{\mathcal{V}}
:

\gamma^{\ast}\gamma_{\ast}D=\mu\,\gamma^{\ast}\gamma_{\ast}C+\gamma^{\ast}\gamma_{\ast}E\geq\mu\,\gamma^{\ast}\gamma_{\ast}C\geq e(\gamma)\,\mu\,C.

Together with the non-negativity condition (
2.1.3
) on
D
, this implies:

\gamma^{\ast}\gamma_{\ast}D\cdot D\geq e(\gamma)\,\mu\,C\cdot D,

and (
2.1.9
) follows.
∎

Using Proposition
2.1.4
together with the general degree bound in Proposition
1.1.2
, we may derive a generalization of the degree bound (
1.3.7
) in Proposition
1.3.1
.

Indeed let us assume that
f:V\rightarrow U
is a morphism of complex algebraic varieties, where
U
and
V
are two smooth connected quasi-projective complex surfaces, and that we are given complex analytic maps
\alpha
and
\beta
from
{\mathcal{V}}
to
X
and
Y
respectively, that fit into a commutative diagram:

(2.1.10)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 31.51457pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.54234pt\raise-15.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.54234pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 31.77477pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Let us assume that
the image of
\alpha
is Zariski dense in
U
— this implies that the image of
\beta
is Zariski dense in
V
and that
f
is dominant — and that
the restriction of
\alpha
to
C
:

\alpha_{\mid C}:C\longrightarrow\alpha(C)

is birational
. Then
\beta_{\mid C}:C\longrightarrow\beta(C)
also is birational, and Proposition
2.1.4
applied to
\gamma:=\beta
implies:

\beta_{\ast}D\cdot\beta_{\ast}D\geq C\cdot D>0.

Therefore Proposition
1.1.2
applies with
A:=\alpha_{\ast}(D)
and
B=\beta_{\ast}(D)
. This proves that
\deg f

satisfies the following upper bound
:

(2.1.11)

\deg f\leq\frac{\alpha_{\ast}D\cdot\alpha_{\ast}D}{\beta_{\ast}D\cdot\beta_{\ast}D}\leq\frac{\alpha_{\ast}D\cdot\alpha_{\ast}D}{\mu\,C\cdot D}.

This generalizes Proposition
1.3.1
. In turn the upper bound (
2.1.11
) implies a generalization of the results
à la
Lefschetz-Nori concerning étale fundamental groups in Proposition
1.3.2
, where now the conclusion reads:

(2.1.12)

[\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(U,x):\alpha_{\mid|D|\ast}(\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(|D|,c))]\leq\frac{\alpha_{\ast}D\cdot\alpha_{\ast}D}{\mu\,C\cdot D}.

2.1.2.
Compatibility of
\mathbf{CNB}
divisors with modifications. Applications to meromorphic maps

Condition
\mathbf{CNB}
turns out to be preserved by modifications:

Proposition 2.1.5
.

Let
D
be an effective divisor in some connected smooth analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
, and let
\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
be a modification
2
2

2

Recall that a modification
\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
is a proper complex analytic map of degree 1 from a connected smooth complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
onto
{\mathcal{V}}
. Then there exists a closed discrete subset
Z
of
{\mathcal{V}}
such that:
c_{\mid{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\setminus\nu^{-1}(Z)}:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\setminus\nu^{-1}(Z)\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}\setminus Z
is an isomorphism, and
\nu^{-1}(P)
is a compact connected analytic curve in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
for every
P\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

of
{\mathcal{V}}
. If
D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
, then the divisor
D^{\prime}:=\nu^{\ast}(D)
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
also satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
.

Proof.

The compactness and the connectedness of
|D|
imply the compactness and the connectedness of
|D^{\prime}|=\nu^{-1}(|D|)
since
\nu
is proper with connected fibers. The validity of (
2.1.2
) and (
2.1.3
) for the divisor
D
, together with the projection formula, implies its validity for
D^{\prime}
.
∎

Observe also that, with the notation of Proposition
2.1.5
, the projection formula also implies the equality:

(2.1.13)

D^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=D\cdot D.

Moreover, if
C
is an irreducible component of
D
of mutiplicity
\mu
, then its strict transform
C^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of multiplicity
\mu
in
D^{\prime}
,
and we have:

(2.1.14)

C^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=C\cdot D.

Proposition
2.1.5
and these observations allow one to generalize our previous results to situations, where instead of
complex analytic maps from the analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
to a smooth algebraic surface, one considers meromorphic maps.

To formulate this generalization, recall that, for every
meromorphic map:

\alpha:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow X

of range a smooth projective complex surface
X
, there exists a modification:

\nu^{\prime}:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}

of
{\mathcal{V}}
that is “adapted to
\alpha
”, namely such that, composed with
\nu
, the meromorphic map
\alpha
defines an actual complex analytic map:

\alpha^{\prime}:=\alpha\circ\nu:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow X.

The meromorphic map
\alpha
defines a correspondence
\Gamma_{\alpha}
from
{\mathcal{V}}
to
X
, proper over
{\mathcal{V}}
, and for every divisor
D
with compact support in
{\mathcal{V}}
, we may consider the image
\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D
of
D
by this correspondence. It is a divisor in
X
, effective when
D
is effective, and in terms of a modification
\nu
adapted to
\alpha
as above, it may defined by the equality:

\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D=\alpha^{\prime}_{\ast}\nu^{\ast}D.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a connected smooth complex analytic surface, and let
D
be an effective divisor in
{\mathcal{V}}
satisfying
\mathbf{CNB}
. Let
C
be an irreducible component of
D
such that:

C\cdot D>0,

and let
\mu
be its multiplicity in
D
. Consider a commutative diagram::

(2.1.15)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.38194pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.39583pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.39583pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.09853pt\raise-26.77083pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.2437pt\raise-9.52779pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.39716pt\raise-5.55554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
X
and
Y
are connected smooth projective complex surfaces,
f
is a dominant morphism, and
\alpha
and
\beta
are meromorphic maps.

If the image of
\alpha
is Zariski dense in
X
, and if the restriction
\alpha_{\mid C}
of
\alpha
to
C
is birational onto its image, then the degree of
f
satisfies the following upper bound:

(2.1.16)

\deg f\leq\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{\Gamma_{\beta\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\beta\ast}D}\leq\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{\mu\,C\cdot D}.

Proof.

The degree bound (
2.1.16
) follows from the estimates (
2.1.11
) — which generalizes the estimates in Proposition
1.3.1
to general
\mathbf{CNB}
divisors — applied to the commutative diagram:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.15279pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 32.67361pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.6875pt\raise-15.83778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.6875pt\raise-24.34225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-8.15279pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.89194pt\raise-25.77777pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.8978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.15279pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
