7.93616pt\raise-9.25943pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.79639pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 32.15279pt\raise-31.67557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

defined by some modification
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
adapted both to
\alpha
and
\beta
, and to the
\mathbf{CNB}
divisor
D^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{V}}
.
∎

2.1.3.
Generalization to
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors

Observe finally that, if
D
is an effective divisor in a connected smooth analytic complex surface
{\mathcal{V}}
and
n
a positive integer,
D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
if an only if
nD
does. Consequently the condition
\mathbf{CNB}
makes sense, not only for effective divisors in
{\mathcal{V}}
, but for any effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor.

Moreover the results of
2.1.1
and
2.1.2
extend to this more general setting, notably the degree bound (
2.1.11
), the index bound
à la
Nori (
2.1.12
), the compatibility with modifications, and Proposition
2.1.6
. This is a formal consequence of the definitions and of the fact that the quotients of intersection numbers in the right-hand side of the estimates (
2.1.11
), (
2.1.12
), or (
2.1.16
) are unchanged when the divisor
D
is replaced by some positive multiple.

2.2.
Application to analytic and algebraic varieties fibered over a projective curve

2.2.1.
Complex analytic surfaces fibered over a projective curve:
\mathbf{CNB}
divisors and equilibrium divisors

In this section we consider the following data:
a connected smooth projective complex curve
C
, a connected smooth complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
, a surjective (necessarily flat) complex analytic map, with connected fibers:

\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}}:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow C,

a complex analytic section of
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}}
:

\varepsilon:C\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}},

and its image:

C_{\mathcal{V}}:=\varepsilon(C).

Let also
\Sigma
be a non-empty finite subset of
C
, and let :

\mathring{C}:=C\setminus\Sigma

be its complement. It defines a smooth connected affine complex algebraic curve. We shall assume that,
for every
x\in\Sigma
, the (possibly non-reduced) connected curve
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(x)
is non-compact
and that we are given
a compact connected curve
F_{x}\subset|\pi_{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(x)|
such that:

F_{x}\cap C_{\mathcal{V}}\neq\emptyset.

From the data of the section
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{V}}
and of the “vertical curves”
(F_{x})_{x\in\Sigma}
, we may carry out the following construction.

Recall that our assumptions on the curves
\pi^{-1}(x)
and
F_{x}
imply that the intersection form restricted to divisors in
{\mathcal{V}}
supported on

F:=\bigcup_{x\in\Sigma}F_{x}

is negative definite. In turns, this implies that a divisor
\Delta
supported on
F
is effective if
\Delta\cdot W\leq 0
for every irreducible component of
F
.
3
3

3

See for instance
[
MB89
, Lemme 3.7]
, where
(D.C)\geq 0
should read
(D.C)\leq 0
.

This implies that there exists a unique
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
V
supported on
F
such that, for every irreducible component
W
of
F
:

(2.2.1)

(C_{\mathcal{V}}+V)\cdot W=0,

and moreover that
V
is effective. The support
|D|
of the divisor

D:=C_{\mathcal{V}}+V

is easily seen to be connected, again as a consequence of the negativity of the intersection form on divisors supported by
F
. In turn, this implies the equality:

|V|=F.

Moreover we have:

D\cdot D=C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D,

and consequently
D
satisfies condition
\mathbf{CNB}
if and only if
D\cdot D
is positive.

The construction of the divisor
D
starting from the section
\varepsilon
and the vertical curves
(F_{x})_{x\in\Sigma}
is a geometric counterpart of the construction in Subsection
6.2.2
below of the Arakelov divisor:

(P,(g_{V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma}})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}})

attached to some formal-analytic surface:

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}:=({\widehat{\mathcal{V}}},(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma},\iota_{\sigma})_{\sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}})

by means of the equilibrium potentials
g_{V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma}}
associated to the pointed compact Riemann surfaces with boundary
(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})
. Accordingly the divisor
D
could be referred to as the
equilibrium divisor
associated to
\varepsilon
and
(F_{x})_{x\in\Sigma}
.

Among the
\mathbf{CNB}

\mathbb{Q}
-divisors of the form
C_{{\mathcal{V}}}+V
with
V
supported on
F
, when they exist, the equilibrium divisor
D
satisfies the following extremal property:

Proposition 2.2.1
.

If there exists an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
\widetilde{V}
supported on
F
such that the divisor

(2.2.2)

\widetilde{D}:=C_{\mathcal{V}}+\widetilde{V}

satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
, then the divisor
D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
. Moreover, we have:

(2.2.3)

V\geq\widetilde{V},

(2.2.4)

C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D\geq C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot\widetilde{D},

and:

(2.2.5)

D\cdot D\geq\widetilde{D}\cdot\widetilde{D},

and equality holds in (
2.2.5
) if and only if
\widetilde{D}=D
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{V}
an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor supported by
F
such that the divisor

\widetilde{D}
defined by (
2.2.2
) satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
, and let:

\Delta:=V-\widetilde{V}.

Then, for every irreducible component
W
of
F
, we have:

\Delta\cdot W=-(\widetilde{D}-D)\cdot W=-\widetilde{D}.W\leq 0.

Since
\Delta
is supported by
F
, it is therefore effective. This implies (
2.2.3
), and the inequality (
2.2.4
) immediately follows. In particular, we have:

(2.2.6)

C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D\geq 0.

Moreover, according to (
2.2.1
), we also have:

D\cdot\Delta=0.

Consequently:

\widetilde{D}\cdot\widetilde{D}=(D-\Delta)\cdot(D-\Delta)=D\cdot D+\Delta\cdot\Delta.

Since
\Delta\cdot\Delta
is non-positive and vanishes if and only if
\Delta=0,
this establishes the inequality (
2.2.5
) and the last assertion of Proposition
2.2.1
.

Finally the relations (
2.2.1
), (
2.2.6
), (
2.2.5
), and the positivity of
\widetilde{D}\cdot\widetilde{D}
imply that
D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
.∎

Corollary 2.2.2
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F
is the support of a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor satisfying
\mathbf{CNB}
;

(2)

D\cdot D>0;

(3)

D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
.

The analogue of Corollary
2.2.2
in the arithmetic setting will be established in Section
6.3
.

2.2.2.
Compatibility with modifications

In the present “fibered” situation, the compatibility of condition
\mathbf{CNB}
with modifications discussed in
2.1.2
may be complemented by the following remarks.

Let us keep the notation of
2.2.1
, and let us consider a modification
\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{V}}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
. Then the morphism:

\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}:=\pi_{\mathcal{V}}\circ\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow C

is surjective with connected fibers, and the strict transform
C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}
of
C_{{\mathcal{V}}}
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
is the image of a section:

\varepsilon^{\prime}:C\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}

of
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}
.

The indetermination locus of
\nu^{-1}
meets
\mathring{C}_{\mid{\mathcal{V}}}:=\varepsilon(\mathring{C})
in a finite subset
\varepsilon(\widetilde{\Sigma})
. Let us define:

\Sigma^{\prime}:=\Sigma\cup\widetilde{\Sigma},

and for every
x
in
\Sigma
(resp. in
\widetilde{\Sigma}
):

F^{\prime}_{x}:=\nu^{-1}(F_{x})\quad\mbox{(resp. $F^{\prime}_{x}:=\nu^{-1}(\varepsilon(x))$).}

The
F^{\prime}_{x},

x\in\Sigma^{\prime},
are compact connected curves in
|\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}^{-1}(x)|
such that:

F^{\prime}_{x}\cap C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset.

The effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D^{\prime}:=\nu^{\ast}(D)
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
may be written:

D^{\prime}=C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}+V^{\prime}

where
V^{\prime}
is a divisor with support:

F^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{x\in\Sigma^{\prime}}F^{\prime}_{x}.

Moreover the projection formula together with (
2.2.1
)
imply that, for every irreducible component
W^{\prime}
of
F^{\prime}
, we have:

(C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}+V^{\prime})\cdot W^{\prime}=0.

This shows that the divisor
D^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
coincides with the equilibrium divisor associated to the section
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and the family of vertical curves
(F^{\prime}_{x})_{x\in\Delta^{\prime}}
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
.

In particular, we have:

D^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}\cdot D^{\prime}.

Moreover, as already observed:

D^{\prime}\cdot D^{\prime}=D\cdot D,

and therefore
D^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
if and only if
D
satisfies it.

2.2.3.

In this subsection and in the next one, we keep the notation of Subsection
2.2.1
and we assume that
the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D

has positive self-intersection
:

D\cdot D=C\cdot D>0,

and therefore satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
.

Observe that, if

is a connected smooth projective complex surface and if

is a commutative diagram where
\alpha
is a meromorphic map and
\pi_{X}
is a morphism of complex algebraic varieties,
4
4

4

or equivalently, by GAGA, of complex analytic manifolds. The morphism
\pi_{X}
is necessarily non-constant, hence surjective and flat.
then
\alpha_{\mid C_{\mathcal{V}}}
is trivially birational onto its image. Moreover the image of
\alpha
is easily seen to be contained in some algebraic curve in
X
if and only if
\alpha
factorizes through
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}}
. In other words, the image of
\alpha
is Zariski dense in
X
if and only if
\alpha
is not constant on the fibers of
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Applied to the present setting, Proposition
2.1.6
for
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors takes the following form:

.

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-62.55554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-62.55554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
X
and
Y
are two projective complex surfaces, with
X
smooth and connected and
Y
integral, where
\alpha
and
\beta
are meromorphic maps, and where
f
and
\pi_{X}
are morphisms of complex algebraic varieties.

If
\alpha
is not constant on the fibers of
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}
, then
f
is dominant and its degree satisfies:

(2.2.8)

\deg f\leq\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D}=\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{D\cdot D}.

Actually Proposition
2.1.6
immediately establishes Theorem
2.2.3
when the surface
Y
is smooth. By considering a resolution of
Y
, this implies Theorem
2.2.3
in general.

Similarly, the amplification of Proposition
1.3.2
concerning
\mathbf{CNB}
divisors discussed at the end of Section
2.1.1
, implies the following result
à la
Lefschetz-Nori concerning étale fundamental groups:

Theorem 2.2.4
.

Consider a commutative diagram:

(2.2.9)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.93124pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 13.88742pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.01459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.9736pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{U}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.9736pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.93124pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.9239pt\raise-25.34444pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}_{C}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
U
is a connected smooth quasi-projective complex surface,
\alpha
a complex analytic map, and
\pi_{V}
a morphism of complex algebraic varieties.
5
5

5

The morphism
\pi_{U}
is necessarily surjective and flat.

If
\alpha
is non constant on the fibers of
\pi_{\mathcal{V}},
then the image of the morphism between étale fundamental groups:

(2.2.10)

\alpha_{\mid C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F\ast}:\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F,c)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(U,x)

induced by the map:

\alpha_{\mid C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F}:C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F\rightarrow U

is a subgroup of finite index; moreover:

(2.2.11)

[\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(U,x):\alpha_{\mid C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F\ast}(\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}(C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F,c))]\leq\frac{\alpha_{\ast}D\cdot\alpha_{\ast}D}{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D}=\frac{\alpha_{\ast}D\cdot\alpha_{\ast}D}{D\cdot D}.

In (
2.2.10
) and (
2.2.11
),
c
denotes a complex point of
C
, and
x:=\alpha\circ\varepsilon(c)
its image in
{U}
.

2.2.4.
The field of meromorphic functions
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})

The map between fields of meromorphic functions:

\pi_{\mathcal{V}}^{\ast}:{\mathcal{M}}(C)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}),\quad f\longmapsto f\circ\pi_{\mathcal{V}}

allows one to identify the field

{\mathcal{M}}(C)=\mathbb{C}(C)

of rational functions over
C
with a subfield of the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
of meromorphic functions over
{\mathcal{V}}
. Then
\mathbb{C}(C)
is easily seen to be algebraically closed in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
. Moreover any meromorphic function
f\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
defines a meromorphic map “fibered over
C
,” namely:

f_{C}:=(\pi_{\mathcal{V}},f):{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C}:=C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C}.

Theorem 2.2.5
.

The following alternative holds: either
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})=\mathbb{C}(C),
or
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is an extension of finite type and of transcendence degree one of
\mathbb{C}(C)
.

Moreover, for every
f
in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})\setminus\mathbb{C}(C),

{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is a finite extension of the purely transcendental extension
\mathbb{C}(C)(f)
of
\mathbb{C}(C)
, and its degree satisfies the following upper bound:

(2.2.12)

[{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}):\mathbb{C}(C)(f)]\leq\frac{\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D}{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D}=\frac{\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D}{D\cdot D}.

The right-hand side of (
2.2.12
) is clearly unchanged when
{\mathcal{V}}
is replaced by some connected open neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
of
C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F
. This immediately implies that, in Theorem
2.2.5
, we may replace the complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{V}}
by its germ
{\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F}
along
C_{{\mathcal{V}}}\cup F
:

Corollary 2.2.6
.

Theorem
2.2.5
still holds when the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is replaced by the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})
of germs of meromorphic functions on
{\mathcal{V}}
along
C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F
. Moreover there exists a connected open neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
of
C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F
in
{\mathcal{V}}
such that any germ of meromorphic functions in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})
extends to a meromorphic function in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
.

As mentioned in
1.4.2
above, the results of the first part of this monograph admit variants in formal geometry. We shall leave this to the interested reader, and will only mention that Theorem
2.2.5
still holds when the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is replaced by the field
{\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})
of formal meromorphic functions, in the sense of
[
HM68
]
, on the formal completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
along the projective curve
C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F
. The field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})
naturally embeds into the field
{\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})
, and an intriguing question is whether these two fields always coincide.
6
6

6

These fields are easily seen to coincide when
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})\neq\mathbb{C}(C).
Accordingly the question is equivalent to the following one:
does the equality
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{{\rm an}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})=\mathbb{C}(C)
imply the equality

{\mathcal{M}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cup F})=\mathbb{C}(C)
? We refer the reader to
[
CG81
]
,
[
Hir81
]
,
[
Ste86
]
, and
[
GPR94
, Section VII.4]
for results and references concerning the validity of such a “formal principle” in similar situations.

Proof of Theorem
2.2.5
.

It is enough to show that, if
f\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
does not belong to
\mathbb{C}(C)
— or equivalently is not constant on the fibers of
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}}
— then, for every
g\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
, the subfield
\mathbb{C}(C)(f,g)
of
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{C}(C)(f)
, of degree at most:

(2.2.13)

\frac{\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D}{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D}=\frac{\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{f_{C}\ast}D}{D\cdot D}.

Indeed this will implies that any finitely generated extension of
\mathbb{C}(C)(f)
in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{C}(C)(f)
of degree at most (
2.2.13
), and consequently that
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
itself is a finite extension of
\mathbb{C}(C)(f)
of degree at most (
2.2.13
).

Let us consider
f
and
g
in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
as above. There exists a modification of
{\mathcal{V}},

\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}},

that is adapted both to
f
and
g
, namely such that the meromorphic functions
f^{\prime}:=f\circ\nu
and
g^{\prime}:=g\circ\nu
on
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
define actual analytic maps:

f^{\prime}:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\quad\mbox{and}\quad g^{\prime}:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C}).

We may consider the complex analytic map:

(2.2.14)

\gamma^{\prime}:=(\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}},f^{\prime},g^{\prime}):{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C}),

where
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}:=\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}}\circ\nu.
Since the divisor
D^{\prime}:=\nu^{\ast}(D)
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
, we may apply Proposition
2.1.3
to
\gamma^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
f^{\prime}
is not constant on the fibers of
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}}
, the image of

f^{\prime}_{C}:=(\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}},f^{\prime}):{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})=:\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C}

is Zariski dense. Consequently, we are in the last case in the conclusion of Proposition
2.1.3
, and there exists an irreducible closed surface
H
in
C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})^{2}
which contains
\gamma^{\prime}({\mathcal{V}})
as a Zariski dense subset.

If we denote by:

p_{12}:C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})^{2}\longrightarrow C\times\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})=:\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C}

the projection on the first two factors, we may consider the commutative diagrams:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.86311pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 32.15279pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

and:

(2.2.15)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 11.95715pt\raise-10.80583pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern where
\gamma:=\gamma^{\prime}\circ\nu^{-1}.
Then, by construction, the maps
f_{C}
and
\gamma
induce isomorphisms of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras:

f_{C}^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{C}(C)(f)

and:

\gamma^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}(H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{C}(C)(f,g),

and we have:

[\mathbb{C}(C)(f,g):\mathbb{C}(C)(f)]=\deg p_{12\mid H}:H\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}_{C}.

The degree bound (
2.2.8
) in Theorem
2.2.3
applied to the diagram (
2.2.15
) shows that this degree is bounded from above by (
2.2.13
). ∎

2.3.
Examples and complements – The universal meromorphic map
\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}

In this section, we complete the results of the previous section concerning analytic surfaces fibered over a projective curves endowed with a
\mathbf{CNB}
divisor. Notably in Subsection
2.3.1
, we discuss the construction of such analytic surfaces by blowing up points, starting from an arbitrary algebraic surface fibered over a projective curve, equipped with a section. We also pursue the study of the field of meromorphic functions on these analytic surfaces.

2.3.1.
Deleting infinitely near points and
\mathbf{CNB}
divisors

In this subsection, we consider the following analytic data: a connected smooth projective complex curve
C
, a connected smooth projective complex surface
X
, a surjective (necessarily flat) morphism of complex (algebraic or analytic) varieties:

\pi_{X}:X\longrightarrow C,

an (algebraic or analytic) section of
\pi_{X}
:

\varepsilon:C\longrightarrow X,

and its image:

C_{X}:=\varepsilon(C).

The connectedness of the projective variety
X
and the existence of the section
\varepsilon
imply the connectedness of the fibers of
\pi_{X}
.

Moreover we choose a point
x
of
C
whose fiber
X_{x}:=\pi_{X}^{-1}(x)
is smooth.

We may construct a system of modifications of
X
:

X=:X^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\;\;\nu^{1}}}{{\longleftarrow}}X^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\;\;\nu^{2}}}{{\longleftarrow}}X^{2}\longleftarrow\cdots{\longleftarrow}X^{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\;\;\nu^{n}}}{{\longleftarrow}}X^{n}\longleftarrow\cdots

by defining:

\nu^{n}:X^{n}\longrightarrow X^{n-1}

to be the blowing-up of
X^{n-1}
at some point
x_{n-1}
, for every
n\geq 1,
where the points
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}
are chosen as follows:

•

x_{0}
is a point in
X_{x}
distinct of the intersection
\varepsilon(x)
of
X_{x}
with
C_{X}
;

•

for every
n\geq 1,

x_{n}
is a point of the exceptional divisor of
\nu^{n}
:

Z_{n}:=(\nu^{n})^{-1}(x_{n-1})

distinct from the intersection of
Z^{n}
with the proper transform
\widetilde{Z}^{n-1}
of
Z^{n-1}
by
\nu^{n}
, where by convention
Z^{0}:=X_{x}
.

The section
\varepsilon
of
\pi_{X}
lifts to a section:

\varepsilon^{n}:C\longrightarrow X^{n}

of the morphism:

\pi_{X^{n}}:=\pi_{X}\circ\nu^{1}\circ\cdots\circ\nu^{n}:X^{n}\longrightarrow C.

Let us denote by
\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}
the proper transform of
X_{x}
by:

\nu^{1}\circ\cdots\circ\nu^{n}:X^{n}\longrightarrow X.

The divisor
X^{n}_{x}:=\pi_{X^{n}}^{-1}(x)
may be written:

X^{n}_{x}=\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}E^{n}_{i},

where

E^{n}_{n}=Z^{n}
is the exceptional divisor of
\nu^{n}
, and where
E^{n}_{i}
is the proper transform of
E^{n-1}_{i}
by
\nu^{n}
when
i<n
. (Observe that
E^{n}_{n-1}=\widetilde{Z}^{n-1}
, and that when
i<n-1
,
\nu^{n}
is actually an isomorphism over
E^{n-1}_{i}
.)

By construction, the
E^{n}_{i}
are smooth rational curves in
X^{n}_{x}
. Moreover the possibly non-vanishing intersection numbers between the divisors
C_{X^{n}}:=\varepsilon_{X^{n}}(C)
,
\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}
, and
E^{n}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, are as follows:

C_{X^{n}}\cdot C_{X^{n}}=C_{X}\cdot C_{X},\quad C_{X^{n}}\cdot\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}=1,\quad\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}\cdot\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}=-1,\quad\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}\cdot E^{n}_{1}=1,

E^{n}_{i}\cdot E^{n}_{i+1}=1\quad\mbox{and}\quad E^{n}_{i}\cdot E^{n}_{i}=-2\quad\mbox{if $1\leq i\leq n-1,$}

and:

E^{n}_{n}\cdot E^{n}_{n}=-1.

See Figure
2
. This is easily established by induction on
n
.

Figure 2.
The divisors
C_{X^{n}}
and
X^{n}_{x}=\tilde{X}^{n}_{x}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{i}^{n}
inside
X^{n}
. In red, the self-intersections of their components; in blue, their multiplicity in the divisor
D
.

For every
n\geq 1
, we may consider the compact connected curve in
X_{x}^{n}
:

F_{x}:=\widetilde{X}_{x}^{n}\cup\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq n-1}E^{n}_{i}.

If
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
is a connected open neighborhood, in the analytic topology, of
C_{X^{n}}\cup F_{x}
in
X^{n}
, we may apply the construction in
2.2.1
to
{\mathcal{V}}:={\mathcal{V}}_{n},

\pi_{\mathcal{V}}:=\pi_{X\mid{\mathcal{V}}_{n}}
,
\Sigma:=\{x\}
, and
\varepsilon:=\varepsilon^{n}
.

A straightforward computation shows that the “equilibrium divisor”
D=:D^{n}
defined by this construction is:

D^{n}=C_{X^{n}}+n\widetilde{X}^{n}_{x}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq n-1}(n-i)E^{n}_{i}.

Its self-intersection satisfies:

D^{n}\cdot D^{n}=C_{X^{n}}\cdot D^{n}=C_{X}\cdot C_{X}+n.

Consequently, if
n
satisfies the inequality:

n\geq 1+(-C_{X}\cdot C_{X})^{+},

then the self-intersection
D^{n}\cdot D^{n}
is positive, and
D^{n}
is a
\mathbf{CNB}
divisor in
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
. This contrasts with the fact that the self-intersection
C_{X}\cdot C_{X}
is in general negative. Indeed it follows from the work of Arakelov
[
Ara71
]
and Szpiro
[
Szp81
, Chapitre III]
that, if
C_{X}\cdot C_{X}
is positive (respectively vanishes), then the smooth fibers of
\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow C
are rational curves (resp.
\pi_{X}
is isotrivial).

2.3.2.
Non-constant morphisms to fibered algebraic surfaces

In this subsection, we use the notation of
2.2.3
and
2.2.4
. So we denote by
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow C
a connected smooth complex analytic surface fibered over
C,
and by
D:=C_{\mathcal{V}}+V
its “equilibrium divisor”, and we assume that
D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
.

A variant of the proof of Theorem
2.2.5
allows one to derive the following result from the degree bound in Theorem
2.2.3
.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

Consider a commutative diagram:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 13.88742pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.43889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.43889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 38.9736pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.9736pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.93124pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.9239pt\raise-25.34444pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}_{C}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.93124pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
X
is a smooth connected projective complex surface,
\alpha
is a meromorphic map, and
\pi_{X}
is a morphism of complex algebraic varieties.

If
\alpha
is not constant on the fibers of
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}
, then the map between fields of meromorphic functions:

\alpha^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}(X)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})

is a finite extension of fields, and its degree satisfies the following upper bound:

(2.3.1)

[{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}):\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)]\leq\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D}=\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{D\cdot D}.

Proof.

The field
\mathbb{C}(X)
is clearly an extension of transcendence degree one of
\mathbb{C}(C)
. According to Theorem
2.2.5
, the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
, which contains
\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)
, is therefore an extension of finite type and of transcendence degree one of
\mathbb{C}(C)
. This implies that
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is a finite extension of
\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)
. To complete the proof of (
2.3.1
),
we shall show that, for every
\varphi\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}),
the degree of the extension
\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)(\varphi)=\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)[\varphi]
of
\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)
satisfies the following inequality:

(2.3.2)

[\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)(\varphi):\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)]\leq\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{C_{\mathcal{V}}\cdot D}=\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D\cdot\Gamma_{\alpha\ast}D}{D\cdot D}.

To achieve this, we consider an integral complex projective surface
Y
whose field of rational functions
\mathbb{C}(Y)
, as a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, is isomorphic to
\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)(\varphi)
. We may assume that the composite map:

\mathbb{C}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha^{\ast}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)(\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{C}(Y)

is induced by a morphism
f:Y\rightarrow X
of complex algebraic varieties. Moreover the map:

\mathbb{C}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)(\varphi)\,{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}\,{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})

is induced by some meromorphic map:

\beta:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow Y.

The maps
\alpha,

\beta,
and
f
fit into a commutative diagram (
2.2.7
), and (
2.3.2
)
follows from the conclusion (
2.2.8
) of Theorem
2.2.3
, since:

\deg f=[\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)(\varphi):\alpha^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)].\qed

For later reference, we state the following straightforward consequence of Theorem
2.3.1
:

.

2.3.3.
The universal meromorphic map
\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}

In this subsection, as in
2.3.2
, we use the notation of 2.2.3-4. Moreover we suppose that the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is not reduced to
\mathbb{C}(C)
.

Under the above assumption, according to Theorem
2.2.5
, the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
is an extension of finite type and of transcendence degree one of
\mathbb{C}(C)
. Moreover the field
\mathbb{C}(C)
is easily seen to be algebraically closed in
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
, and the restriction of meromorphic functions on
{\mathcal{V}}
to the section
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{V}}
defines a place:

\varepsilon_{\mathcal{V}}^{\ast}:{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}(C)

of the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
with values in
\mathbb{C}(C)
that restricts to the identity on
\mathbb{C}(C)
.

Consequently there exists a smooth projective geometrically irreducible curve
{\mathcal{C}}
over
\mathbb{C}(C)
, a
\mathbb{C}(C)
-rational point
P
of
{\mathcal{C}}
, and an isomorphism of field extensions of
\mathbb{C}(C)
:

i:\kappa({\mathcal{C}})=\mathbb{C}(C)({\mathcal{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})

such that the place
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{V}}^{\ast}
of
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
coincides, via the isomorphism
i
, with the place of
\kappa({\mathcal{C}})
defined by the rational point
P
; in other words:

\varepsilon^{\ast}\circ i=P^{\ast}.

Moreover the triple
({\mathcal{C}},P,i)
is uniquely determined, up to a unique isomorphism, by the above conditions.

We may choose a smooth projective model over
C
of the curve
{\mathcal{C}}
over
\mathbb{C}(C)
— namely a smooth connected projective complex surface
S
, equipped with a surjective morphism of complex varieties:

\pi_{S}:S\longrightarrow C,

such that the generic fiber of
\pi_{X}
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{C}}
. Then the
\mathbb{C}(C)
-rational point
P
of
{\mathcal{C}}\simeq S_{\mathbb{C}(C)}
defines a section of
\pi_{S}
:

\varepsilon_{S}:C\longrightarrow X.

Moreover the isomorphism of field extensions of
\mathbb{C}(C)
:

i:\mathbb{C}(S)\simeq\kappa({\mathcal{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})

is induced by a meromorphic map

\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow S
which fits into a commutative diagram:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern The map
\varphi
is non-constant on the fibers of
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}
, and satisfies:

\varepsilon_{S}=\varphi\circ\varepsilon_{\mathcal{V}}.

The family
(S,\pi_{S},\varepsilon_{S},\varphi)
so constructed is unique up to a birational isomorphism of
S
fibered over
C
.

We shall denote by:

{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}:=S

for this projective variety over
C
— which is well defined up to fibered birational isomorphism
– and the meromorphic map:

\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}

will be called the
universal meromorphic map
from
{\mathcal{V}}
to a projective complex variety fibered over
C
. It is readily seen to satisfy the universal property indicated by this terminology.

30.93124pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
X
is a smooth connected projective complex surface,
\alpha
is a complex analytic map, and
\pi_{X}
is a morphism of complex algebraic varieties.

According to Corollary
2.3.2
, if
\alpha
is an open immersion, we may choose
X
for
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
, and the map:

\alpha:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow X=:{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}

is the universal meromorphic map. However we will show in Subsection
2.3.4
that in general the universal meromorphic map
\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
may be ramified along the section
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Once a model
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
has been chosen, we may introduce a modification:

\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}

that is adapted to the meromorphic map
\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
, and denote by:

\varphi^{\prime}:=\varphi\circ\nu:{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}

the associated complex analytic map. Then we may consider the following
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
:

\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D:=\varphi^{\prime}_{\ast}\nu^{\ast}D.

It is an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor which may be written:

\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D=C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}+W,

where
C_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}
is the image of the canonical section
\varepsilon_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}
of
\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}
, and where
W
is a vertical
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor in
{{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}
. Using that
D
and therefore
\nu^{\prime\ast}D
satisfy the condition
\mathbf{CNB}
and the projection formula, one easily sees that the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D
is actually big and nef.

This implies that the set of closed integral curves in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
disjoint from its support
|\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D|
is finite. We shall denote by
E
the union of these curves; it is the largest closed reduced subscheme of
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
of pure dimension one that is disjoint from
|\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D|
.

The previous discussion
admits a straightforward variant where the complex manifold
{\mathcal{V}}
(resp. the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
) is replaced by the germ of analytic manifold
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}
of
{\mathcal{V}}
along
C\cup F
(resp. by the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F})
of germs of meromorphic functions along
C\cup F
).

In this way, we define a universal (germ of) meromorphic map:

(2.3.3)

\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}\dashrightarrow[{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}]^{\mathrm{alg}}

from
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}
to a smooth projective complex surface fibered over
C
, which induces an isomorphism of field extensions of
\mathbb{C}(C)
:

\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}([{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}]^{\mathrm{an}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}_{C\cup F}).

The existence of this universal meromorphic map and the fact that the divisor
\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D
in
[{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}]^{\mathrm{alg}}
is big and nef allow one to establish some finiteness result concerning the subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F})

consisting in the germs of meromorphic function on
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}
that are holomorphic on
{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}\setminus\pi_{{\mathcal{V}}}^{-1}(\Sigma).
The analogue of this result in the arithmetic setting will be the content of Theorem
9.1.1
.

From now on, for simplicity we shall denote:

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}:={\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{an}}_{C\cup F}.

Moreover we shall denote by
E
the largest closed reduced subscheme of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
of pure dimension one that is disjoint from
|\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D|
.

Recall that the complement of the non-empty finite set
\Sigma
in the projective curve
C
defines an affine complex curve:

\mathring{C}:=C\setminus\Sigma.

We shall denote its ring of regular function by:

\mathbb{C}[\mathring{C}]:=\Gamma(\mathring{C},{\mathcal{O}}^{\rm{alg}}_{\mathring{C}})=\varinjlim_{n}\Gamma(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C}(n\Sigma)).

Clearly
{\mathcal{A}}
is a
\mathbb{C}[\mathring{C}]
-algebra.

Let us denote by
E
the (finite) union of the closed integral curves in
{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}
disjoint from

|\Gamma_{\varphi\ast}D|.

The following proposition is established by analyzing when the divisor of the pull-back
\varphi^{\ast}f
in
{\mathcal{M}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
of a rational function
f\in\mathbb{C}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}})
is contained in
\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)
. The details of its proof will be left to the reader.

Proposition 2.3.3
.

The isomorphism of field extensions of
\mathbb{C}(C)
:

\varphi^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}({{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})

restricts to an isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}[\mathring{C}]
-algebras:

\varphi^{\ast}:\Gamma\big({{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}\setminus{(E\cup\pi_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}}^{-1}(\Sigma)\big),{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{A}}.

Recall that Zariski
[
Zar54
]
has shown that, for any smooth projective complex surface
X
and any effective divisor
C
in
X
, the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra:

\Gamma(X\setminus|C|,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\simeq\varinjlim_{n}\Gamma(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(nC))

is finitely generated.
8
8

8

The paper
[
Zar54
]
is reproduced in
[
Zar73
, p. 261-274]
. The proof of this finiteness generation result, incomplete in the original publication
[
Zar54
]
, is completed in
[
Zar73
, p. 275]
.

Applied to
X:={\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
and
C:=E\cup\pi_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{alg}}}^{-1}(\Sigma)
, this implies:

Corollary 2.3.4
.

{\mathcal{A}}
is a
\mathbb{C}[\mathring{C}]
-algebra of finite type.

2.3.4.
The universal meromorphic map
\varphi:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}
may be ramified

In this subsection, we construct some examples that show that the “universal meromorphic map”

\varphi_{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{V}}\dashrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathrm{alg}}

introduced in Subsection
2.3.3
may be ramified along the section
C_{\mathcal{V}}
of
\pi_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Algebraic data

As in
2.3.1
, let us consider a connected smooth projective complex curve
C
, a connected smooth projective complex surface
X
, a surjective (necessarily flat) morphism of complex (algebraic or analytic) varieties:

\pi_{X}:X\longrightarrow C,

an (algebraic or analytic) section of
\pi_{X}
:

\varepsilon:C\longrightarrow X,

and its image:

C_{X}:=\varepsilon(C).

Let moreover
\Sigma
be a non-empty finite subset of
C
, and for every
x\in\Sigma
, let
F_{x}
be a compact connected reduced curve
F_{x}
strictly contained in
|\pi_{X}^{-1}(x)|
such that:

F_{x}\cap C_{X}\neq\emptyset,

and let:

F:=\bigcup_{x\in\Sigma}F_{x}.

To these data, we may associate an equilibrium
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D
supported by
C_{X}\cup F
by the construction of Subsection
2.2.1
9
9

9

applied to the complex surface
{\mathcal{V}}
defined as an open neighborhood in
X
of
C_{X}\cup F
that does not contain any of the fibers
\pi^{-1}(x)
for
x\in\Sigma.
, namely the unique
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor of the form:

D=C_{X}+V

with
V
supported on
F
, such that, for every component
W
of
F
, we have:

(2.3.4)

D\cdot W=0.

We shall make the following further assumptions:

•

the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D
has integral coefficients; in other words,
D
is a divisor in
X
;

•

the divisor
D
satisfies
\mathbf{CNB}
; equivalently, the intersection number
D\cdot D=C_{X}\cdot D
is positive.

The construction in
2.3.1
allows one to construct instances of such objects from any projective surface fibered over
C
endowed with a section.

Constructing analytic surfaces fibered over
C

We consider the following additional data:

•

a positive integer
e
;

•

a connected open neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
, in the analytic topology, of
C_{X}\cup F
in
X
, such that
{\mathcal{V}}
contains no fiber
\pi^{-1}(x)
,
x\in C
;

•

a smooth effective analytic divisor
10
10

10

or equivalently, a closed complex submanifold of dimension
1
in
{\mathcal{V}}
.

R
in
{\mathcal{V}}
, meeting
C_{X}
transversally, such that the following two conditions are satisfied:

(2.3.5)

R\subset X\setminus F,

and:

(2.3.6)

C_{X}\cdot R\equiv-\,C_{X}\cdot D\quad\mod e.

For every positive integer
e
, a neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of
C_{X}\cup F
and a divisor
R
in
{\mathcal{V}}
satisfying the conditions above clearly exist.

We shall denote by:

\iota:{\mathcal{V}}\longrightarrow X

the inclusion morphism.

Consider the complex analytic line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(R+C_{X}+V)
over
{\mathcal{V}}
. According to (
2.3.4
), (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.6
), the degree of its restriction to every component of
C_{X}\cup F
is divisible by
e
. Consequently, after possibly replacing
{\mathcal{V}}
by a smaller neighborhood of
C\cup F
in
X
, we may assume that there exists a complex analytic line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
over
{\mathcal{V}}
and an isomorphism of complex analytic line bundles over
{\mathcal{V}}
:

(2.3.7)

s:{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{V}}(R+C_{X}+V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes e}.

Using
{\mathcal{L}}
and
s
, we may construct the cyclic cover:

c:{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{V}}

obtained by taking the
e
-th root out of
s
; see for instance
[
EV92
, Sections 3.3-14]
.
By construction,
{\mathcal{W}}
is a normal complex analytic surface, equipped with an action of the cyclic group
G:=\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z},
and
c
defines an isomorphism of complex analytic spaces:

(2.3.8)

{\mathcal{W}}/G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{V}}.

We shall denote by
\sigma:{\mathcal{W}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{W}}
the automorphism of
{\mathcal{W}}
defined by the action of the generator
[1]
of
\mathbb{Z}/e\mathbb{Z}
.

The cover
c
is totally ramified over
C_{X}
, and therefore establishes an isomorphism:

c_{\mid C_{\mathcal{W}}}:C_{\mathcal{W}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{X}

between
C_{X}
and the image
C_{\mathcal{W}}
of some complex analytic section
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{W}}
of the complex analytic morphism:

\pi_{\mathcal{W}}:=\pi_{X}\circ\iota\circ c:{\mathcal{W}}\longrightarrow C.

We may finally consider a resolution
11
11

11

The normal analytic surface
{\mathcal{W}}
has a finite set of singular points, included in
c^{-1}((R\cup C\cup F)_{\mathrm{sing}})
, where
(R\cup C\cup F)_{\mathrm{sing}}
denotes the finite set of singular points of
R\cup C\cup F
.
:

\nu:\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{W}}

of
{\mathcal{W}}
and the morphism:

\pi_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}}:=\pi_{W}\circ r:\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\longrightarrow C.

The proper transform
C_{\widetilde{W}}
of
C_{W}
by
\nu
is the image of an analytic section of
\pi_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}}:

\varepsilon_{\widetilde{W}}:C\longrightarrow\widetilde{W}.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the finite subset of
C\setminus\Sigma
such that:

R\cap C_{X}=\varepsilon_{X}(\Sigma^{\prime}),

and let:

\widetilde{\Sigma}:=\Sigma\sqcup{\Sigma^{\prime}}.

For every
x\in\widetilde{\Sigma},
we define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{x}

\displaystyle:=(c\circ\nu)^{-1}(F_{x})\quad\quad\,\mbox{if $x\in\Sigma$}

\displaystyle:=\nu^{-1}(\varepsilon_{X}(x))\quad\quad\;\;\;\mbox{if $x\in\Sigma^{\prime}$.}

Then, for every
x\in\widetilde{\Sigma},

\widetilde{F}_{x}
is a compact connected curve in
\pi_{{\mathcal{W}}}^{-1}(x)
, which itself is non-compact, and contains
\varepsilon_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}}(x)
. Moreover the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}
:

\widetilde{D}:=\frac{1}{e^{\prime}}\,(c\circ\nu)^{\ast}D

is easily seen to be the equilibrium divisor associated to the the section
\varepsilon_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}}
and to the family
(\widetilde{F}_{x})_{x\in\widetilde{\Sigma}},

and to satisfy
\mathbf{CNB}
.

We have constructed the following morphisms of complex analytic or algebraic varieties fibered over
C
:

(2.3.9)

\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{W}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{V}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}X,

and we may consider the induced maps between fields of meromorphic functions:

(2.3.10)

\mathbb{C}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota^{\ast}}}{{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{\ast}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu^{\ast}}}{{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathcal{M}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}).

The map
\iota^{\ast}
is indeed an isomorphism as a consequence of Corollary
2.3.2
applied to
\alpha:=\iota.

The main result of this subsection is the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3.5
.

If the analytic divisor
R
is Zariski dense in
X
, then the cover
c
induces an isomorphism of fields of meromorphic functions:

(2.3.11)

c^{\ast}:{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}}).

Proof.

The action of
G
on the complex analytic surface
{\mathcal{W}}
induces an action of
G
on the field
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}})
. Moreover, according to the isomorphism (
2.3.8
), its fixed field coincides with the image of
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
by
c^{\ast}
:

{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}})^{G}=c^{\ast}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}}).

This shows that
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}})
is a cyclic extension of
c^{\ast}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})
of degree
e^{\prime}
dividing
e
, of Galois group:

\big\{\sigma^{\ast k};k\in\mathbb{Z}/e^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}\big\}.

In particular, there exists
\varphi\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}})
such that:

{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{W}})=c^{\ast}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})[\varphi]

and:

(2.3.12)

\sigma^{\ast}\varphi=e^{2\pi i/e^{\prime}}\varphi.

If
e^{\prime}>1,
then
\varphi\neq 0,
and
\varphi^{e^{\prime}}
belongs to
c^{\ast}{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{V}})=c^{\ast}\iota^{\ast}\mathbb{C}(X)
, and may therefore be written:

\varphi^{e^{\prime}}=c^{\ast}\iota^{\ast}\psi

for some
\psi
in
\mathbb{C}(X)^{\times}
. In particular, the following equality of analytic Weil divisors in
{\mathcal{W}}
holds:

(2.3.13)

e^{\prime}{\mathrm{div}\,}\varphi=c^{\ast}\iota^{\ast}{\mathrm{div}\,}\psi.

The map
c
is totally ramified over every component of
R
. Consequently the points in
c^{-1}(R)
are fixed under the action of
G
on
{\mathcal{W}}
. Together with (
2.3.12
), this implies that the support
|{\mathrm{div}\,}\varphi|
of
{\mathrm{div}\,}\varphi
contains
c^{-1}(R)
. Using (
2.3.13
), we conclude that the support
|{\mathrm{div}\,}\psi|
of
{\mathrm{div}\,}\psi
contains
R
.

This contradicts the Zariski density of
R
in
X
, and establishes the equality
e^{\prime}=1
.
∎

It is clear that we may find
{\mathcal{V}}
and
R
as in
2.3.4
such that
R
is Zariski dense in
X
. It is enough to construct
{\mathcal{V}}
and
R
satisfying (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.6
) such that
R\cap C_{X}
is non-empty, and such that, for some
x\in R\cap C_{X},
the germ of
R
at
x
is not the branch of an algebraic curve through
x
. Then
R\cap{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
would actually be Zarishi dense in
X
for every open analytic neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
of
C_{X}\cup F
in
{\mathcal{V}}
.

Corollary 2.3.6
.

If the analytic divisor
R
is Zariski dense in
X
, then the map:

(2.3.14)

\iota\circ c\circ r:\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\longrightarrow X

is “the” universal meromorphic map from
\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}
to a projective complex variety fibered over
C
.

Observe that the universal meromorphic map (
2.3.14
) is ramified, with ramification index
e
, along
\varepsilon_{\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}}.

Part II
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
Green functions on Riemann surfaces and intersection theory on quasi-projective arithmetic surfaces

Chapter 3
Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
and
L^{2}_{1}
regularity and arithmetic intersection numbers

Part 2 of this memoir is devoted to various results concerning Arakelov intersection pairings on arithmetic surfaces and Green functions on Riemann surfaces. These results will be used in Part 3 to transpose in an arithmetic setting the results in complex algebraic, analytic, and formal geometry presented in Part 1.

This chapter and the next one are devoted to some extensions of Arakelov intersection theory on arithmetic surfaces adapted to our needs. In this chapter, we
develop the version of Arakelov intersection theory in
[
Bos99
]
, that relies on the use of
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions, in a possibly non-projective situation. Moreover we show that it satisfies some nice functoriality properties. These tools allow us to establish in Theorem
3.5.10
an arithmetic version of the degree bound
à la
Nori in Proposition
1.1.1

that has been our geometric starting point in Chapter 1.

The necessity of adapting Arakelov intersection theory on arithmetic surfaces to a possible non-projective setting has led us to give a self-contained presentation of the theory, and this chapter is in principle accessible with no previous familiarity with Arakelov geometry.

3.1.
The Arakelov degree of
0
-cycles and the height of
1
-cycles

3.1.1.
The Arakelov degree of
0
-cycles

Let
X
be an arithmetic scheme, that is, a separated scheme of finite type over
\mathbb{Z}
. A
0
-cycle
on
X
is a finite formal sum:

Z=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}P_{i}

where the
P_{i}
are closed points of
X
and the
n_{i}
are integers. The residue fields:

\kappa(P_{i})={\mathcal{O}}_{X,P_{i}}/\mathfrak{m}_{X,P_{i}}

are finite, and the
arithmetic
or
Arakelov degree
of
Z
is defined as the real number:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,Z:=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}\log|\kappa(P_{i})|.

If
Y
is another arithmetic scheme and
f:X\rightarrow Y
a morphism of schemes, then the images
f(P_{i})
are closed points of
Y
, and the direct image
f_{\ast}Z
is the
0
-cycle defined by the equality:

f_{\ast}Z:=\sum_{i\in I}[\kappa(P_{i}):\kappa(f(P_{i}))]\,f(P_{i}),

Then we have, as a straightforward consequence of the definition of the Arakelov degree:

(3.1.1)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,f_{\ast}Z=\widehat{\rm deg}\,Z.

When
X
is
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z},
every
0
-cycle on
X
is of the form:

{\mathrm{div}\,}q:=\sum_{p}v_{p}(q)\,[p]

for some
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{\ast}
— where the sum runs over the prime numbers
p
and where
v_{p}
denotes the
p
-adic valuation and
[p]
the closed point
p\mathbb{Z}
of
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
— and we have:

(3.1.2)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\mathrm{div}\,}q=\sum_{p}v_{p}(q)\log p=\log|q|.

The Arakelov degree of
0
-cycles is clearly uniquely determined by its compatibility with direct images (
3.1.1
) and the relation (
3.1.2
). Observe also that, if
X
is a
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme for some prime
p
, then the
0
-cycle
Z
on
X
considered as a
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme admits a “geometric degree”, defined as in
[
Ful98
, Definition 1.4]
:

\deg_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}Z:=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}[\kappa(P_{i}):\mathbb{F}_{p}]\in\mathbb{Z},

which is related to its arithmetic degree by the equality:

(3.1.3)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,Z=\deg_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}Z.\log p.

3.1.2.
The Arakelov degree of
0
-cycles rationally equivalent to zero on proper one-dimensional schemes

In this subsection, we denote by
C
be a scheme
proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, integral and of dimension one
.

The (support of the) image the morphism
f:C\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
is a closed integral subscheme of
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, and therefore either
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, or a closed point
[p]
of
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
. Accordingly the following alternative holds: either
C
is finite and flat over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, or
C
is a proper
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme for a uniquely determined prime
p
.

When
C
is finite and flat over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, then
C
is affine, the ring
\Gamma(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})
is an order in some number field
K
, and if
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
denotes the ring of integers of
K
, then
{\rm Spec\,}{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
may be identified with the normalization of
C
.

In this case, the relation (
3.1.2
) admits the following generalization. For every
r\in\kappa(C)^{\times}=K^{\times},
the Arakelov degree of the
0
-cycle on
C
defined by the divisor
1
1

1

It is defined, as in
[
Ful98
, 1.2-3]
, by the equality:
{\mathrm{div}\,}r:=\sum_{x\in C_{0}}\mathrm{ord}_{x}r.x,
where
C_{0}
denotes the set of closed points of
C
.

{\mathrm{div}\,}r
satisfies:

(3.1.4)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\mathrm{div}\,}r=\sum_{\sigma\in C(\mathbb{C})}\log|\sigma(r)|,

where
C(\mathbb{C})
denotes the set of complex points of the scheme
C
; this set may be identified with the set, of cardinality
[K:\mathbb{Q}]
, of the field embeddings
\sigma:K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Indeed if
q:=N_{K/\mathbb{Q}}r
denotes the image of
r
by the norm map from
K
to
\mathbb{Q}
, and if
f
denotes the morphism of schemes from
C
to
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, then we have:

f_{\ast}{\mathrm{div}\,}r={\mathrm{div}\,}q

and:

|q|=\prod_{\sigma\in C(\mathbb{C})}|\sigma(r)|,

and therefore (
3.1.4
) follows from (
3.1.1
) and (
3.1.2
).

When
C
is a proper
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme, then the ring
\Gamma(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})
is a finite extension
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
C
is a geometrically integral projective curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Moreover the relation (
3.1.3
) and the basic properties of the degree of
0
-cycles on proper schemes over a field (see
[
Ful98
, 1.4]
) imply that, for any
r\in\kappa(C)^{\times}
, we have:

(3.1.5)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\mathrm{div}\,}r=0.

3.1.3.
The height of
1
-cycles with support proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}

Let us keep the notation of
3.1.2
and assume that
C
is a (necessarily closed) subscheme of some reduced, separated scheme
X
of finite type over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
. Assume moreover that
X
is endowed with a Hermitian line bundle
{\overline{L}}:=(L,\|.\|)
.

Let us consider a non-zero rational section
s
of the line bundle
L_{\mid C}
over
C
, and its divisor
{\mathrm{div}\,}s
.

In case (1), the set
C(\mathbb{C})
of complex points of
C
may be identified with a subset of
X(\mathbb{C})
, and accordingly the Hermitian metric
\|.\|
on
L_{\mathbb{C}}^{{\rm an}}
defines a Hermitian metric
\|.\|_{x}
on the fiber
L_{x}
for every
x\in C(\mathbb{C})
. We may therefore consider the positive real numbers
(\|s(x)\|_{x})_{x\in C(\mathbb{C})}
, and the relation (
3.1.4
) shows that the real number:

(3.1.6)

0pt_{{\overline{L}}}(C):=\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\mathrm{div}\,}s-\sum_{x\in C(\mathbb{C})}\|s(x)\|_{x}

does not depend on the choice of
s
. It defines the
height
of
C
with respect to
{\overline{L}}
.

In case (2), the relation (
3.1.5
) shows that
\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\mathrm{div}\,}s
does not depend on the choice of
s
. We shall denote it by
\widehat{\rm deg}\,L_{\mid C}.
According to the relation (
3.1.3
), we have:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,L_{\mid C}=\deg_{C/\mathbb{F}_{p}}L.\log p=\deg_{C/\mathbb{F}_{q}}L.\log q,

where
\deg_{C/\mathbb{F}_{p}}L
(resp.
\deg_{C/\mathbb{F}_{q}}L
) denotes the usual “geometric” degree of the line bundle
L
over
X
seen as a proper
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme (resp. a geometrically integral projective curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
).

It is convenient to use some uniform notation, covering both cases (1) and (2), and to let in case (2):

0pt_{{\overline{L}}}(C):=\widehat{\rm deg}\,L_{\mid C}.

We will also use the alternative notation:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}|C):=0pt_{{\overline{L}}}(C).

In the framework of
[
BGS94
]
, this real number would actually be denoted by
\widehat{\rm deg}\,(\hat{c}_{1}({\overline{L}})|C)
.

Observe that the height
\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}|C)
depends “additively” on
{\overline{L}}
. Namely, if
{\overline{L}}_{1}
and
{\overline{L}}_{2}
are two Hermitian line bundles over
X
, we have:

(3.1.7)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}_{1}\otimes{\overline{L}}_{2}|C)=\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}_{1}|C)+\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}_{2}|C).

The definition of
\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}|C)
extends by linearity to an arbitrary
1
-cycle
C
in
X
whose support
|C|
is proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
. Namely, any such
1
-cycle is a finite formal sum:

C=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}C_{i}

where the
C_{i}
are closed integral subschemes of
X
of dimension one, proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, and the
n_{i}
are integers, and we define:

(3.1.8)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}|C)=\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}\,\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}|C_{i}).

Proposition 3.1.1
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a morphism of reduced separated schemes of finite type over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
. For every
1
-cycle
D
over
X
such that
|D|
is proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
and every Hermitian line bundle
{\overline{L}}
over
Y
, the following equality holds:

(3.1.9)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,(f^{\ast}{\overline{L}}|D)=\widehat{\rm deg}\,({\overline{L}}|f_{\ast}D).

In the right-hand side of (
3.1.9
), we denote by
f_{\ast}D
the direct image of
D
by
f
, defined as in
[
Ful98
, 1.4]
. It is a
1
-cycle on
Y
whose support is easily seen to be proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
D
is an integral scheme
C
as in
3.1.2
above. When
C
is an horizontal curve, namely in case (1), the equality (
3.1.9
) is a straightforward consequence of the definitions. When
C
is vertical, namely in case (2), it follows from the projection formula in
[
Ful98
, Proposition 2.5 (c)]
applied to morphisms between one-dimensional projective schemes over a finite field.
∎

3.2.
Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity and
\ast
-products on Riemann surfaces

3.2.1.
Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity on a Riemann surface

Let
M
be a Riemann surface, and let
D
be a divisor on
M
. Recall that we denote by
\mathbbm{1}_{D}
be the canonical section with divisor
D
of the analytic line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}(D)
.

Definitions

A
Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity
for the divisor
D
on
M
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function:

g:M\setminus|D|\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that the equality:

\|\mathbbm{1}_{D}\|_{g}=e^{-g}

defines a metric on the restriction of
\mathcal{O}_{M}(D)
to
M\setminus|D|
that extends to a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|_{g}
on
\mathcal{O}_{M}(D)
over
M
.

This condition may be equivalently formulated by requiring that
g
has logarithmic singularities at the points of
|D|
. Namely, for every open subset
U
of
M
and every invertible function
f
on
U
such that:

\mathrm{div}f=D_{|U},

there exists
h
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{R})
such that:

(3.2.1)

g=\log|f|^{-1}+h\quad\mbox{over $U\setminus|D|.$}

Observe that this implies that, like the function
\log|f|^{-1}
, the function
g
is locally
L^{1}
on
U
, and that the distribution on
U
it defines satisfies the equality of currents on
U
:

\frac{i}{\pi}\,\partial\overline{\partial}g=-\delta_{D}+\frac{i}{\pi}\,\partial\overline{\partial}h.

This shows that a Green function
g
as defined above is locally
L^{1}
on
M
and that, considered as a distribution on
M
, it satisfies the equality:

(3.2.2)

\frac{i}{\pi}\,\partial\overline{\partial}g+\delta_{D}=\omega(g)

for some
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
real
2
-form
\omega(g)
on
M
.

Conversely, according to the ellipticity of the operator
\partial\overline{\partial}
acting on distributions on
M
, any real distribution
g
on
M
that satisfies the equality (
3.2.2
) for some
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
real
2
-form
\omega(g)
on
M
defines a Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity for the divisor
D
on
M
.

Recall that the first Chern form of a Hermitian line bundle
{\overline{L}}=(L,\|.\|)
with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric over
M
is the
2
-form over
M
defined locally by the equality:

(3.2.3)

c_{1}({\overline{L}}_{\mathbb{C}})=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial\overline{\partial}\log\|s\|^{2},

where
s
denotes a local non-vanishing complex analytic section of
L
. With this notation, if
g
is a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity for a divisor
D
on
M
, we also have:

\omega(g)=c_{1}\big({\mathcal{O}}_{M}(D),\|.\|_{g}\big).

Capacitary metrics

Let
P
be a point of the Riemann surface
M
, and let
g
be a Green function for the divisor
P
in
M
. The attached Hermitian metric
\|.\|_{g}
on
\mathcal{O}_{M}(P)
defines, by restriction to the fiber

\mathcal{O}(P)_{|P}\simeq T_{P}M,

a Hermitian metric on the one-dimensional complex vector space
T_{P}M
. By definition, this metric is the
capacitary metric

\|.\|_{g}^{\mathrm{cap}}.

If
z
is a local holomorphic coordinate on some neighborhood
U
of
P
in
M
, we have the following equality of distributions on
U
:

g=\log|z-z(P)|^{-1}+f

for some function
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U)
, and the definition of the capacitary metric
\|.\|_{g}^{\mathrm{cap}}
may be expressed by the following formula:

\|(\partial/\partial z)_{|P}\|_{g}^{\mathrm{cap}}=e^{-f(P)}.

3.2.2.
The
\ast
-product of two Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be two divisors on a Riemann surface
M
such that:

|D_{1}|\cap|D_{2}|=\emptyset,

and let
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
be Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity for
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
respectively.

The

\ast
-product
of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
is defined as the real current of degree 2 on
M
:

g_{1}\ast g_{2}:=g_{2}\,\delta_{D_{1}}+g_{1}\,\omega(g_{2}),

where, as above,
\omega(g_{2})
is defined by:

\omega(g_{2})=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g_{2}+\delta_{D_{2}}.

When additionally the intersection
\mathrm{supp}g_{1}\cap\mathrm{supp}g_{2}

of the supports of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
is compact, the
\ast
-product
g_{1}\ast g_{2}
is supported on a compact subset of
M
, and we may consider the integral:

\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

It is actually symmetric in
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
; namely:

(3.2.4)

\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}=\int_{M}g_{2}\ast g_{1}.

This equality is a direct consequence of Green’s formula — itself a consequence of Stokes formula — which asserts that, for any two distributions
\varphi
and
\psi
on
M
with disjoint singular supports such that
\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi\cap\mathrm{supp}\,\psi
is compact, the following equalities holds:

(3.2.5)

\int_{M}\varphi\,i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi=-i\int_{M}\partial\varphi\wedge\overline{\partial}\psi=-i\int_{M}\partial\psi\wedge\overline{\partial}\varphi=\int_{M}\psi\,i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi.

The integral of the
\ast
-product also satisfies the following two properties as a straightforward consequence of its definition and of Stokes formula:

\mathbf{SP}_{1}
:
If the supports of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are disjoint, then:

\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}=0;

\mathbf{SP}_{2}
:
For any two functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
such that the intersections
\mathrm{supp}\,f_{1}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,g_{2},

\mathrm{supp}\,f_{2}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,g_{1}
and
\mathrm{supp}\,f_{1}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,f_{2}
are compact, the following equality holds
:

(3.2.6)

\int_{M}(g_{1}+f_{1})\ast(g_{2}+f_{2})=\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}+\int_{M}f_{1}\,\omega(g_{2})+\int_{M}f_{2}\,\omega(g_{1})-\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{M}\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}.

For any pair of divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
on
M
with disjoint supports, the function that attaches the integral
\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}
to a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
of Green functions for the divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
such that
\mathrm{supp}\,g_{1}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,g_{2}
is compact is easily seen to be characterized by the properties
\mathbf{SP}_{1}
and
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
.

The last term in the right-hand side of (
3.2.6
) is, up to a factor
\pi
, the opposite of the
Dirichlet scalar product of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
:

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}:=i\int_{M}\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2},

which is defined for any pair of functions
(f_{1},f_{2})
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathrm{supp}f_{1}\cap\mathrm{supp}f_{2}
is compact.

Actually, for every such pair of functions, we have:

i\int_{M}\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}=i\int_{M}\Big(\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}df_{1}\wedge df_{2}\Big)=\frac{i}{2}\int_{M}(\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}+\partial f_{2}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{1}),

and, if
z=x+iy
is a local holomorphic coordinate on
M
, we may write locally:

i\Big(\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}-\frac{1}{2}df_{1}\wedge df_{2}\Big)=\frac{i}{2}\left(\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}+\partial f_{2}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{1}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x}\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial y}\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial y}\right)dx\wedge dy.

The bilinear form
\langle.,.\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
is clearly symmetric and nonnegative. When
M
is compact and connected, it induces a structure of real pre-Hilbert space on the quotient
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
.

3.2.3.
Functoriality of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity on Riemann surfaces

The functoriality properties of Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity will play an important role in the next chapters. For comparison, in this subsection we present a short description of the functoriality properties of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity.
2
2

2

We refer the reader to
3.4.2
and
4.4.1
below for some background concerning the pull-back and push-forward operations on forms and currents by analytic maps between Riemann surfaces.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

Let
M
and
N
be two Riemann surfaces, with
N
connected, and let
f:N\rightarrow M
be a non-constant complex analytic map.

1) If
D
is divisor on
M
and
g
a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity for
D
on
M
, then the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
function
f^{\ast}g
on
N\setminus f^{-1}(|D|)=N\setminus|f^{\ast}D|
is a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity for the divisor
f^{\ast}D
on
N
.

Moreover the following equality of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
2-forms on
N
is satisfied:

\omega(f^{\ast}g)=f^{\ast}\omega(g).

2) If
E
is a divisor on
N
and
h
a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity for
E
on
N,
and if
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}h}
is proper
3
3

3

This implies that
f_{\mid|E|}
is proper, and therefore that the divisor
f_{\ast}E
on
M
is well-defined.
and if
\omega(h)
vanishes on some neighborhood of the ramification locus
\mathrm{Ram}(f)
of
f
, then the distribution
f_{\ast}h
on
M
is a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity for
f_{\ast}E
on
M
.

Moreover the following equality of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
2-forms on
M
is satisfied:

\omega(f_{\ast}h)=f_{\ast}\omega(h).

3) Let
(D,g)
and
(E,h)
be as in 1) and 2). If the following additional conditions are satisfied:

(3.2.7)

f^{-1}(|D|)\cap|E|=\emptyset

and:

(3.2.8)

\mbox{${\mathrm{supp}\,}g\cap f({\mathrm{supp}\,}h)$ is compact},

then the following equality holds:

(3.2.9)

\int_{N}(f^{\ast}g)\ast h=\int_{M}g\ast(f_{\ast}h).

Observe that, in 3),
f^{\ast}g
and
h
are Green functions for the divisor
f^{\ast}D
and
E
, which have disjoint support according to (
3.2.7
); moreover, according to (
3.2.8
) and to the properness of
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}h}
, the intersection:

{\mathrm{supp}\,}(f^{\ast}g)\cap{\mathrm{supp}\,}h=f^{-1}({\mathrm{supp}\,}g)\cap{\mathrm{supp}\,}h

is compact. This shows that the left-hand side of (
3.2.9
) is well defined.

Moreover,
g
and
f_{\ast}h
are Green functions for the divisors
D
and
f_{\ast}E
; these divisors have disjoint supports as a consequence of (
3.2.7
); moreover
{\mathrm{supp}\,}g\cap{\mathrm{supp}\,}(f_{\ast}h)
is contained in
{\mathrm{supp}\,}g\cap f({\mathrm{supp}\,}h)
and is therefore compact by (
3.2.8
). This shows that the right-hand side of (
3.2.9
) is well defined.

The proof of Proposition
3.2.1
will be left as an exercise for the interested reader.

Concerning Part 3) of Proposition
3.2.1
, observe that the operation of push-forward does
not
preserve in general the class of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity. Actually this operation does not preserve
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
functions when ramification occurs.

For instance, if
e
is a positive integer, the push-forward of the function:

|.|^{2}:\mathring{D}(0;1)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad z\longmapsto|z|^{2}

by the complex analytic map:

f_{e}:\mathring{D}(0;1)\longrightarrow\mathring{D}(0;1),\quad z\longmapsto z^{e}

is the function:

f_{e\ast}|.|^{2}=e|.|^{2/e}.

It is not
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
if
e>1
.

3.3.
Arakelov divisors on arithmetic surfaces and arithmetic intersection numbers

In this section, we denote by
X
an integral normal arithmetic surface.

3.3.1.
Arakelov divisor and Hermitian line bundles

An
Arakelov divisor
(resp. an
Arakelov-Cartier divisor
) on
X
is a pair
(D,g)
, where
D
is a Weil (resp. Cartier) divisor on
X
and
g
a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity, invariant under complex conjugation, for the divisor
D_{\mathbb{C}}
on the Riemann surface
X(\mathbb{C})
.

Recall that, to the Green function
g
is associated a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|_{g}
on the analytic line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X(\mathbb{C})}(D_{\mathbb{C}})
over the Riemann surface
X(\mathbb{C})
. Accordingly, when moreover
D
is a Cartier divisor, we may associate to
(D,g)
the Hermitian line bundle with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric:

(3.3.1)

\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D,g):=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D),\|.\|_{g}).

We shall denote by
Z^{1}(X)
and
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
the additive groups of Weil and Cartier divisors on
X
, and by

\overline{Z}^{1}(X)
and
\overline{Z}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
the additive groups of Arakelov and Arakelov-Cartier divisors on
X
. The quotient group
Z^{1}(X)/Z^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
, and consequently
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)/\overline{Z}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
, is finite.

To any invertible rational function
f
in
\kappa(X)^{\times},
we may associate the following Arakelov-Cartier divisor:

\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}f:=\big({\mathrm{div}\,}f,\log|f_{\mathbb{C}}|^{-1}\big)\in\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X).

The map:

\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}:\kappa(X)^{\times}\longrightarrow{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)

is a morphism of abelian groups. Its image
\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}
is the group of Arakelov divisors
rationally equivalent to zero
, and its cokernel defines the
Arakelov-Chow group
of
X
:

\overline{CH}^{1}(X):=\overline{Z}^{1}(X)/\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}.

We shall also consider its subgroup of finite index:

\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X):=\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)/\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}.

If we denote by

\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X)
the group of isomorphism classes of Hermitian line bundles with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metrics on
X
, we define an isomorphism of abelian group:

(3.3.2)

\widehat{c}_{1}:\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)

by mapping the isomorphism class of a Hermitian line bundle with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-metric
{\overline{L}}:=(L,\|.\|)
to the class
\widehat{c}_{1}({\overline{L}})
of the Arakelov-Cartier divisor:

\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}_{{\overline{L}}}\,s:=({\mathrm{div}\,}s,\log\|s_{\mathbb{C}}\|^{-1})

of an arbitrary non-zero rational section
s
of
L
. The inverse isomorphism
\widehat{c}_{1}^{-1}
sends the class of some Cartier-Arakelov divisor
(Z,g)\in{\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)
to the isomorphism class of
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(Z,g).

An Arakelov divisor
(D,g)
will be said to be
compactly supported
when the support
|D|
of
D
is proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
and when the support
{\mathrm{supp}\,}g
of the Green function
g
is a compact subset of
X(\mathbb{C})
. The subgroup of
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)
(resp. of
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
) defined by the compactly supported Arakelov (resp. Cartier-Arakelov) divisors on
X
will be denoted by
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X)
(resp. by
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}(X)
).

3.3.2.
The Arakelov intersection pairing: definitions and basic properties

Definitions

If
{\overline{L}}:=(L,\|.\|)
is a Hermitian line bundle over
X,
with a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric
\|.\|
, and if
(D,g)
is an Arakelov divisor in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X),
we define the
Arakelov intersection pairing
of
{\overline{L}}
and
(D,g)
as the real number:

(3.3.3)

{\overline{L}}\cdot(D,g):=\widehat{\rm deg}\,\big({\overline{L}}|D\big)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,c_{1}({\overline{L}}_{\mathbb{C}}).

If
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
(resp.
(D,g)
) is an Arakelov divisor in
{\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)
(resp. in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X)
), we define their
Arakelov
or
arithmetic intersection number
as the real number:

(3.3.4)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g):=\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=\widehat{\rm deg}\,\big(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})|D\big)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,\omega(g^{\prime}).

The arithmetic intersection pairing:

(3.3.5)

.\cdot.:\overline{Z}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)\times\overline{Z}^{1}_{c}(X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

defined by (
3.3.5
) is clearly bilinear. Since
\overline{Z}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
has finite index in
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)
, the pairing (
3.3.5
) uniquely extends to a bilinear pairing:

(3.3.6)

.\cdot.:\overline{Z}^{1}(X)\times\overline{Z}^{1}_{c}(X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}.

In concrete terms, the pairing (
3.3.6
) may be described as follows. If
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
and
(D,g)
are Arakelov divisors in
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)
and
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)_{c}
respectively, we may choose a positive integer
N
such that the divisor
ND^{\prime}
is Cartier, and then, by definition, the intersection pairing of
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
and
(D,g)
is the real number:

(3.3.7)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=N^{-1}\,(ND^{\prime},Ng^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=N^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(ND^{\prime},Ng^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)\\
=N^{-1}\widehat{\rm deg}\,(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(ND^{\prime},Ng^{\prime})|D)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,\omega(g^{\prime}).

Properties of the Arakelov intersection pairing

By construction, the intersection pairing is compatible with linear equivalence in the first variable. Namely:

Proposition 3.3.1
.

For every
f\in\kappa(X)^{\times}
and every

(D,g)\in\overline{Z}^{1}(X)_{c}
, we have:

(3.3.8)

\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}f\cdot(D,g)=0.

The following proposition describes how the intersection pairing of two Arakelov divisors depends of their Green functions.

Proposition 3.3.2
.

For any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)\in\overline{Z}^{1}(X)_{c}
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\overline{Z}^{1}(X)
, and any two functions invariant under complex conjugation
f\in{\mathcal{C}}_{c}^{\infty}(X(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R})
and
f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(X(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R})
, the following equality holds:

(3.3.9)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime}+f^{\prime})\cdot(D,g+f)=(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f^{\prime}\,\omega(g)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f\,\omega(g^{\prime})-\pi^{-1}\langle f^{\prime},f\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}.

Proof.

According to the bilinearity of the Arakelov intersection pairing, the validity of (
3.3.9
) follows from the following three equalities:

(3.3.10)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(0,f)=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f\,\omega(g^{\prime}),

(3.3.11)

(0,f^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f^{\prime}\,\omega(g),

and:

(3.3.12)

(0,f^{\prime})\cdot(0,f)=-\pi^{-1}\langle f^{\prime},f\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}.

The equality (
3.3.10
) follows from the definition (
3.3.3
) of the intersection pairing and from the relation:

c_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}})=c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X(\mathbb{C})}(D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}),\|.\|_{g^{\prime}})=\omega(g^{\prime}).

Formula (
3.3.11
) is established by the following chain of equalities:

(3.3.13)

\displaystyle(0,f^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\rm deg}\,\big(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(0,f^{\prime})|D\big)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}f^{\prime}

(3.3.14)

\displaystyle=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f^{\prime}\,\delta_{D_{\mathbb{C}}}+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}f^{\prime}

(3.3.15)

\displaystyle=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}\Big(f^{\prime}\,\delta_{D_{\mathbb{C}}}+f^{\prime}\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g\Big)

(3.3.16)

\displaystyle=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f^{\prime}\,\omega(g).

Indeed (
3.3.13
) follows from the definition (
3.3.3
) of the intersection pairing and from the relation:

(3.3.17)

c_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(0,f^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}})=c_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathbb{C}}},\|.\|_{f^{\prime}})=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}f^{\prime}.

The equality (
3.3.14
)
follows from the definition (
3.1.6
), applied with
{\overline{L}}
the restriction of
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(0,f^{\prime})
to the components of
D
and with
s=1
. The equality (
3.3.15
) follows from Green’s formula (
3.2.5
), and (
3.3.16
) from the definition (
3.2.2
) of
\omega(g)
.

Finally, using again (
3.3.3
) and (
3.3.17
), we obtain:
