(0,f^{\prime})\cdot(0,f)=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}f^{\prime}=-\pi^{-1}\langle f^{\prime},f\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}.\qed

Recall that if
D
and
D^{\prime}
are two Cartier divisors on
X
, the intersection
D\cdot D^{\prime}
is a
0
-cycle on
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
which is well defined and depends symmetrically of
D
and
D^{\prime}
, up to rational equivalence supported by
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
.

Using again that
Z^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)
has finite index in
Z^{1}(X)
, this allows one to define the intersection
D\cdot D^{\prime}
of any two Weil divisor
D
and
D^{\prime}
on
X
: it is a
0
-cycle with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients supported by
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
, which is well defined and depends symmetrically of
D
and
D^{\prime}
, up to rational equivalence supported by
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
.

When moreover
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
is proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
and its generic fiber
|D_{\mathbb{Q}}|\cap|D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}|
is empty,
4
4

4

This last condition holds precisely when the divisors
D_{\mathbb{C}}
and
D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
on the Riemann surface
X(\mathbb{C})
have disjoint supports.
then
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
is a finite union closed points and of vertical curves in
X
proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, and therefore as observed in
3.1.2
above, rational equivalences supported by
|D|\cap|D^{\prime}|
preserve the Arakelov degree of
0
-cycles. Accordingly, in this situation, the Arakelov degree
\widehat{\rm deg}\,D\cdot D^{\prime}
is well defined and satisfies:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,D\cdot D^{\prime}=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D^{\prime}\cdot D.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

For any two Arakelov divisors
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\overline{Z}^{1}(X)
and
(D,g)\in\overline{Z}^{1}(X)_{c}
such that
D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
D_{\mathbb{Q}}
have disjoint supports, the following equality holds:

(3.3.18)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D^{\prime}\cdot D+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\ast g^{\prime}.

Proof.

The bilinearity of the intersection pairing easily implies that, to establish the equality (
3.3.18
), we may assume that one of the following additional conditions is satisfied: (i) the divisor
D^{\prime}
is a Cartier divisor; moreover
D^{\prime}
and
D
meet properly, namely the intersection
|D^{\prime}|\cap|D|
of their supports is a finite set of closed points; or (ii) the Arakelov divisors
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
and
(D,g)
are of the form
(V^{\prime},0)
and
(V,0)
, where
V^{\prime}
is a vertical Cartier divisor in
X
, and
V
a vertical divisor in
X
, proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
.

In case (ii), (
3.3.18
) is a straightforward consequence of the definitions. In case (i), we may argue as follows.

Applied to the restriction
{\overline{L}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
to the components of
D
and to
s=\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\prime}}
, the definition (
3.3.3
) gives:

(3.3.19)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,(\overline{\mathcal{O}}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})|D)=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D^{\prime}\cdot D-\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}\log\|{\mathbbm{1}}_{D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}}\|_{g^{\prime}}\,\delta_{D_{\mathbb{C}}}=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D^{\prime}\cdot D+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}{g^{\prime}}\,\delta_{D_{\mathbb{C}}}.

Consequently, using successively the definition of the arithmetic intersection pairing, the relation (
3.3.19
), and the definition of
g\ast g^{\prime}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g):

\displaystyle=\widehat{\rm deg}\,(\overline{\mathcal{O}}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})|D)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,c_{1}\big({\mathcal{O}}_{X(\mathbb{C})}(D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}),\|.\|_{g}^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D^{\prime}\cdot D+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}\big({g^{\prime}}\,\delta_{D_{\mathbb{C}}}+g\,\omega(g^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D^{\prime}\cdot D+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\ast g^{\prime}.\qed

Corollary 3.3.4
.

For any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
in
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)_{c}
, we have:

(3.3.20)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=(D,g)\cdot(D^{\prime},g^{\prime}).

Proof.

Using the bilinearity of the arithmetic intersection pairing, one readily sees that the symmetry property (
3.3.20
) follows from its validity in the following two special cases: (i) when
|D|_{\mathbb{Q}}\cap|D^{\prime}|_{\mathbb{Q}}
is empty; and (ii) when
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})=(D,g)
. In case (i), (
3.3.20
) follows from Proposition
3.3.3
and from the symmetry (
3.2.4
) of the integral of the
\ast
-product. In case (ii), it is clear.
∎

The Arakelov intersection pairing on projective arithmetic surfaces

In the special case where the integral normal arithmetic surface
X
is projective, and therefore
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)
and
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X)
coincide, one recovers the “classical” arithmetic intersection theory on integral normal projective arithmetic surfaces from the above constructions.

Indeed from the properties of the Arakelov intersection pairing established in
3.3.2
, one immediately derives:

Scholium 3.3.5
.

If
X
is an integral normal projective arithmetic surface, then one defines a symmetric bilinear pairing:

.\cdot.:\overline{CH}^{1}(X)\times\overline{CH}^{1}(X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

by setting:

[(D,g)]\cdot[(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})]:=(D,g)\cdot(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})

for any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
on
X
.

This pairing is the unique bilinear pairing such that the following equality holds:

[(D,g)]\cdot[(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})]=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D\cdot D^{\prime}+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\ast g^{\prime},

for any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
on
X
such that
D_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
have disjoint supports.

The starting point of Arakelov geometry has been the definition of the arithmetic intersection number attached to a pair of Arakelov divisors on some regular projective arithmetic surface, as in Scholium
3.3.5
.

The original contributions of Arakelov
[
Ara74
]
and Faltings
[
Fal84
]
focused on the arithmetic intersection pairing attached to Arakelov divisors
(Z,g)
defined by Green functions
g
satisfying a suitable normalization condition.
5
5

5

Namely, the
2
-forme
\omega(g)
was required to be a multiple of the
2
-form
\beta_{\mathrm{Ar}}
, defined by (
5.3.4
) below, on each of the connected components
X_{\sigma}(\mathbb{C})
of
X(\mathbb{C})
, which were assumed to have positive genus.
The general definition, allowing arbitrary Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity as defined in
3.2.1
above, appears in the work of Deligne
[
Del87
]
and Gillet-Soulé
[
GS90
]
.

3.3.3.
Functoriality properties

Let
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
be a morphism between two integral normal arithmetic surfaces.

The inverse image of Hermitian line bundles with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metrics defines a morphism of groups:

f^{\ast}:\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X)\longrightarrow\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X^{\prime}),

and therefore, by means of the isomorphisms:

\widehat{c}_{1}:\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widehat{c}_{1}:\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X^{\prime}),

a pull-back map between Arakelov-Cartier Chow groups:

(3.3.21)

f^{\ast}:\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)\longrightarrow\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X^{\prime}).

When
X
and
X^{\prime}
are proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
(that is, projective arithmetic surfaces), this map is compatible with the Arakelov intersection product. Namely, for every pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of elements of
\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X),
the following equality holds:

f^{\ast}\alpha\cdot f^{\ast}\beta=\deg f\;\alpha\cdot\beta,

where
\deg f
denotes the degree
6
6

6

Recall that
\deg f
vanishes when
f
is not dominant, and that
\deg f:=[\kappa(X^{\prime}):f^{\ast}\kappa(X)]
when
f
is dominant, hence generically finite.
of
f
; see for instance
[
BGS94
, 2.1.1, 2.2, and Proposition 2.3.1, (iv)]
.

When
f
is dominant, the pull-back map (
3.3.21
) “lifts” to Arakelov-Cartier divisors. Namely, using the functoriality of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-regularity by pull-back stated in Proposition
3.2.1
, we may define a map:

f^{\ast}:\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)\longrightarrow\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X^{\prime}),\quad(D,g)\longmapsto(f^{\ast}D,f_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}g),

which is easily seen to be compatible with rational equivalence and to induce the map (
3.3.21
).

One may wonder whether, as with usual Chow groups, the Arakelov-Chow groups also admit some covariant functoriality, namely whether to a map
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
as above is naturally attached a map
f_{\ast}
from
\overline{CH}^{1}(X^{\prime})
to
\overline{CH}^{1}(X)
. At the level of Arakelov cycles, it should be defined by a formula of the type:

(3.3.22)

f_{\ast}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime}):=(f_{\ast}D^{\prime},f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g^{\prime}),

say when
f
is proper, or when the Arakelov cycle
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
on
X^{\prime}
is compactly supported.

The right-hand side of (
3.3.22
) does
not
make sense in general, even if
f
is assumed to be dominant and proper (and therefore
f_{\mathbb{C}}
to be a finite morphism) because of the limited functoriality of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity discussed in Subsection
3.2.3
.
7
7

7

However it defines an Arakelov cycle on
X
when for instance the
2
-form
\omega(g^{\prime})
vanishes
on some neighborhood of the ramification locus of
f_{\mathbb{C}}
.

This lack of covariant functoriality of the “classical” Arakelov-Chow groups defined by using Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity will be addressed in the next sections of this memoir, by introducing
Green functions with weaker regularity.

3.4.
Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity on Riemann surfaces

In many applications of arithmetic intersection theory and heights on arithmetic surfaces, it is crucial to extend the original scope of the theory and make it possible to use Green functions – or, equivalently, Hermitian metrics on line bundles – with a weaker regularity than the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity we required in the discussion above. Diverse more flexible formalisms involving possibly non
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
Green functions and Hermitian metrics have been notably developed in
[
Bos99
]
,
[
Küh01
]
and
[
BGKK07
]
.

In the next two sections, we discuss the formalism introduced in
[
Bos99
]
, based on the use of Green functions with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity. We extend the results in
loc. cit.
, by developing this formalism on possibly non-projective arithmetic surfaces, and by investigating its functoriality properties.

3.4.1.
Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity and
\ast
-products

When one is interested in defining the Arakelov intersection numbers (
3.3.4
) and (
3.3.7
) in the largest possible generality, a natural regularity class for the Green functions — namely the
L^{2}_{1}
regularity — is suggested by the characterization of the map

(g_{1},g_{2})\longmapsto\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}

by properties
\mathbf{SP}_{1}
and
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
in
3.2.2
.

Recall that a distribution
\varphi
over a differentiable manifold
M
is said to be locally
L^{2}_{1}
when the current
d\varphi
is locally
L^{2}
(and therefore
\varphi
itself if locally
L^{2}
, and actually locally
L^{p}
for every
p\in[1,+\infty)
when
M
has dimension 2). A
Green function with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity
(or shortly a
L^{2}_{1}
Green function) for a divisor
D
on a Riemann surface
M
is defined as in
3.2.1
by allowing the function
h
appearing in (
3.2.1
) to be locally
L^{2}_{1}
over
U
, instead of being
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

If
g_{0}
is a Green function for
D
with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity, then the
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions for
D
are precisely the distributions on
M
of the form:

g=g_{0}+f,

where
f
is a locally
L^{2}_{1}
function on
M
.

By the elliptic regularity of the operator
\partial\overline{\partial}
, the
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions are exactly those real distributions
g
on
M
such that the current
\omega(g)
defined as in (
3.2.2
) by:

\omega(g):=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g+\delta_{D}

belongs to the space of locally
L^{2}_{-1}
currents of degree
2
over
M
.

In this paragraph, for simplicity, let us assume that the Riemann surface
M
is compact and connected.

Observe that in property
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
, if
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are assumed to be of class
L^{2}_{1}
instead of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, while
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are still Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity, then in the equality (
3.2.6
) — namely:

\int_{M}(g_{1}+f_{1})\ast(g_{2}+f_{2})=\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}+\int_{M}f_{1}\,\omega(g_{2})+\int_{M}f_{2}\,\omega(g_{1})-\frac{i}{\pi}\int_{M}\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}

— the right-hand side is still well-defined, since both
\int_{M}f_{1}\,\omega(g_{2})
and
\int_{M}f_{2}\,\omega(g_{1})
clearly are, and the Dirichlet scalar product:

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}=i\int_{M}\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}

also is, because both
df_{1}
and
df_{2}
are
L^{2}
.

As a consequence, the equality (
3.2.6
) may be used to define the integral of the
\ast
-product of any two
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions for
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, written respectively as
g_{1}+f_{1}
and
g_{2}+f_{2}
with
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
some
L^{2}_{1}
functions on
M
. This definition is readily seen to be independent of the choice of the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
Green functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
for
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Moreover, the equality (
3.2.6
) holds for any two
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
for
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, and any two functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
in the Dirichlet space
L^{2}_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})
of real
L^{2}_{1}
functions on
M
.
8
8

8

Note that, for any such
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, the last three integrals in (
3.2.6
) are well-defined, as the integrals of the product of currents in
L^{2}_{1}
and
L^{2}_{-1}
, or of two
L^{2}
1-forms.

Observe also that the validity of (
3.2.6
) implies that, when it is defined, the integral of the
\ast
-product of
{L^{2}_{1}}
-Green functions still satisfy the symmetry (
3.2.4
).

Since the space
L^{2}_{1}(M,\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
may be identified with the completion of the space
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
with respect to the Dirichlet scalar product
9
9

9

See for instance
[
Bos99
, 3.1.2]

\langle.,\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}
, this discussion shows that
L^{2}_{1}
-regularity is the weakest possible regularity condition on Green functions that makes it possible to define the intersection number of arbitrary Arakelov divisors
(Z,g)
with
g
of this regularity.

More generally, when
M
is possibly non-compact, a straightforward extension of the previous arguments allows one to define the integral of the
\ast
-product:

\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}

when
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions attached to some divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
with disjoint supports in
M
such that the intersection
{\mathrm{supp}\,}g_{1}\cap{\mathrm{supp}\,}g_{2}
is compact.

Actually properties
\mathbf{SP}_{1}
and
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
remain valid in this generality, where
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
denote some Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity, and where in
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are real valued locally
L^{2}_{1}
functions.
Moreover, for any pair of divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
on
M
with disjoint support, the function that attaches the integral
\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}
to a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})
of
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions for the divisors
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
such that
\mathrm{supp}\,g_{1}\cap\mathrm{supp}\,g_{2}
is compact is still characterized by the validity of this extension of properties
\mathbf{SP}_{1}
and
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
.

3.4.2.
Functoriality of Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity

It turns out that Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity on Riemann surfaces enjoy remarkable functoriality properties: contrary to Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity, they constitute a class of Green functions that is preserved by the operation of direct image by non-constant complex analytic maps.

Functoriality of forms and currents on complex analytic manifolds

Recall that to any complex analytic map
f:X\rightarrow Y
of complex analytic manifolds are attached a pull-back map
f^{*}
, that sends
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
differential forms on
Y
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
differential forms on
X
, and a push-forward map
f_{*}
, deduced from
f^{*}
by duality. The map
f_{\ast}
sends a current
T
of degree
d
on
X
such that the restriction
f_{|\mathrm{supp}(T)}
of
f
to the support of
T
is a proper map, to the current
f_{*}T
of degree
d-2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X+2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
on
Y
defined by:

\int_{Y}\alpha\,f_{*}T=\int_{X}f^{*}\alpha\;T

for any
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
differential form
\alpha
with compact support on
Y
of degree
2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X-d
.

The maps
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
are compatible with the decomposition of differential forms and currents into forms and currents of type
(p,q)
,
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
, and with the operators
d,

\partial
and
\overline{\partial}.
The constructions of
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
are functorial in
f
.

In this paragraph, we denote by
M
and
N
be two Riemann surfaces, with
N
connected, and by
f:N\rightarrow M
a non-constant complex analytic map.

The following propositions describe the functoriality of currents on
M
and
N
of degree 1 (resp. 0, resp. 2) with regularity
L^{2}
(resp.
L^{2}_{1}
, resp.
L^{2}_{-1}
) with respect to the map
f
. They follow from basic properties of currents on manifolds, combined with the fact that the map
f
is locally of the form
(z\mapsto w=z^{e})
for some positive integer
e
in suitable local analytic charts
z
and
w
on
N
and
M
. The details of the
proofs are left to the reader.
10
10

10

The proof of Corollary
3.4.4
may be simplified by the following observation:
a locally
L^{2}_{-1}
current of degree 2 on a Riemann surface vanishes if its support is discrete.
This observation also implies that a
L^{2}_{1}
Green current for a divisor
D
uniquely determines
D
.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

1) If
\alpha
is a locally
L^{2}
1-form on
M,
then its pull-back
11
11

11

defined pointwise, almost everywhere on
N
.

f^{\ast}\alpha
is a locally
L^{2}
1-form on
N
.

2) If
\beta
is a locally
L^{2}
1-form on
N
and if the restriction
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}\beta}
is proper, then the current
f_{\ast}\beta
is a locally
L^{2}
1-form on
M
.

3) If
\alpha
and
\beta
are as in 1) and 2), and if
{\mathrm{supp}\,}\alpha\cap f({\mathrm{supp}\,}\beta)
is compact, then the following equality holds:

\int_{N}f^{\ast}\alpha\wedge\beta=\int_{M}\alpha\wedge f_{\ast}\beta.

Proposition 3.4.2
.

1) If
\varphi
is a locally
L^{2}_{1}
function on
M
, then its pull-back
12
12

12

defined pointwise, almost everywhere on
N
.

f^{\ast}\varphi
is a locally
L^{2}_{1}
function on
N
.

2) If
\psi
is a locally
L^{2}_{1}
function on
N
and if
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}\psi}
is proper, then the distribution
f_{\ast}\psi
is a locally
L^{2}_{1}
function on
M
.

Proposition 3.4.3
.

1) If
\chi
is a locally
L^{2}_{-1}
current of degree 2 on
M
, then its pull-back
f^{\ast}\chi
may be defined as the unique locally
L^{2}_{-1}
current of degree 2 on
N
such that, for every
L^{2}_{1}
function
\psi
on
N
with compact support, the following equality holds:

(3.4.1)

\int_{N}\psi\,f^{\ast}\chi=\int_{M}f_{\ast}\psi\,\chi.

The equality (
3.4.1
) is actually satisfied for every locally
L^{2}_{1}
function
\psi
on
N
such that
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}\psi}
is proper and
{\mathrm{supp}\,}\chi\cap f({\mathrm{supp}\,}\psi)
is compact.

2) If
\omega
is a locally
L^{2}_{-1}
current of degree 2 on
N
and if
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}\omega}
is proper, then the current
f_{\ast}\omega
is a locally
L^{2}_{-1}
current of degree 2 on
M
. Moreover, for every locally
L^{2}_{1}
function
\varphi
on
M
such that
{\mathrm{supp}\,}\varphi\cap f({\mathrm{supp}\,}\omega)
is compact, the following equality holds:

(3.4.2)

\int_{N}f^{\ast}\psi\,\omega=\int_{M}\psi\,f_{\ast}\omega.

Corollary 3.4.4
.

For every locally
L^{2}_{1}
function
\varphi
on
M
, the following equality holds:

(3.4.3)

\partial\overline{\partial}(f^{\ast}\varphi)=f^{\ast}(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi).

In the left-hand side of (
3.4.3
), the function
f^{\ast}\varphi
is defined as in Proposition
3.4.2
, 1). In its right-hand side, the current
f^{\ast}(\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi)
is defined by Proposition
3.4.3
, 1) applied to
\chi=\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi
.

From the functoriality properties of
L^{2}_{1}
functions and of
L^{2}_{-1}
currents of degree 2 in Propositions
3.4.2
and
3.4.3
, one easily deduces the following functoriality properties of Green functions with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity on Riemann surfaces:

Proposition 3.4.5
.

Let
M
and
N
be two Riemann surfaces, with
N
connected, and
f:N\rightarrow M
a non-constant complex analytic map.

1) If
D
is a divisor on
M
and
g
a Green function with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity for
D
on
M
, then the function
f^{\ast}g
on
N
, pointwise defined almost everywhere on
N
, is a Green function with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity for the divisor
f^{\ast}D
on
N
.

Moreover the following equality of
L^{2}_{-1}
currents of degree 2 on
N
is satisfied:

\omega(f^{\ast}g)=f^{\ast}\omega(g).

2) If
E
is a divisor on
N
and
h
a Green function with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity for
E
on
N,
and if
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}h}
is proper
13
13

13

This implies that
f_{\mid|E|}
is proper, and therefore that the divisor
f_{\ast}E
on
M
is well defined.
, then the distribution
f_{\ast}h
on
M
is a Green function with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity for
f_{\ast}E
on
M
.

Moreover the following equality of
L^{2}_{-1}
currents of degree 2 on
M
is satisfied:

\omega(f_{\ast}h)=f_{\ast}\omega(h).

3) Let
(D,g)
and
(E,h)
be as in 1) and 2). If the following additional conditions are satisfied:

(3.4.4)

f^{-1}(|D|)\cap|E|=\emptyset

and:

(3.4.5)

\mbox{${\mathrm{supp}\,}g\cap f({\mathrm{supp}\,}h)$ is compact},

then the following equality holds:

(3.4.6)

\int_{N}(f^{\ast}g)\ast h=\int_{M}g\ast(f_{\ast}h).

The following proposition relates direct images of currents of the form
\log|\varphi|
, where
\varphi
denotes a meromorphic function, and norm maps associated to algebraic morphisms of complex curves.

Proposition 3.4.6
.

Let
f:N\rightarrow M
be a finite morphism between some smooth irreducible complex algebraic curves, and let:

N_{\mathbb{C}(N)/\mathbb{C}(M)}:\mathbb{C}(N)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(M)

be the norm map associated to the finite extension of fields of rational functions:

f^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}(N).

For every
\varphi
in
\mathbb{C}(M)^{\times},
the following equality of locally
L^{1}
distributions holds:

(3.4.7)

f_{\ast}\log|\varphi|=\log|N_{\mathbb{C}(N)/\mathbb{C}(M)}(\varphi)|.

This easily follows from the fact that both sides of (
3.4.7
) are locally
L^{1}
and from the expression (
4.4.1
) recalled in Subsection
4.4.1
below for the direct image of a continuous function.

3.5.
Arakelov intersection theory with
L^{2}_{1}
Green functions on integral normal arithmetic surfaces

3.5.1.
Construction and properties of the Arakelov intersection pairing

In this subsection, we denote by
X
an integral normal arithmetic surface.

Arakelov divisors with
{L^{2}_{1}}
Green functions

Using Green functions with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity instead of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity in the definitions in
3.3.1
, we define the group
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
of
Arakelov divisors
on
X

with
{L^{2}_{1}}
Green functions
.

We may also consider its subgroups
{\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{L^{2}_{1}}}
of Cartier-Arakelov divisors with
{L^{2}_{1}}
Green functions and
{\overline{Z}}_{c}^{1}(X)^{{L^{2}_{1}}}
of compactly supported Arakelov divisors with
{L^{2}_{1}}
Green functions, and their intersection:

{\overline{Z}}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}^{1}(X)^{{L^{2}_{1}}}:={\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{L^{2}_{1}}}\cap{\overline{Z}}_{c}^{1}(X)^{{L^{2}_{1}}}.

To this extended notion of Arakelov divisors are as associated the following
{L^{2}_{1}}
variants of the Arakelov-Chow groups of
X
:

\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}:=\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}/\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}

and:

\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}:=\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}/\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}.

We may extend the Arakelov intersection pairing defined in
3.3.2
above to Arakelov divisors with
{L^{2}_{1}}
Green functions. Indeed, from Proposition
3.3.2
and the validity of property
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
in the
{L^{2}_{1}}
setting discussed in
3.4.1
, we immediately derive:

Proposition and Definition 3.5.1
.

The Arakelov intersection pairing
3.3.6
admits a unique extension:

(3.5.1)

.\cdot.:\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}\times\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}_{c}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that the equality (
3.3.9
), namely:

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime}+f^{\prime})\cdot(D,g+f)=(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f^{\prime}\,\omega(g)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}f\,\omega(g^{\prime})-\pi^{-1}\langle f^{\prime},f\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}},

remains valid for any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)\in\overline{Z}_{c}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and any two functions invariant under complex conjugation
f\in{L^{2}_{1}}(X(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R})_{c}
and
f^{\prime}\in{L^{2}_{1}}(X(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{R})_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

In turn, Proposition
3.5.1
readily implies:

Corollary 3.5.2
.

Propositions
3.3.1
and
3.3.3
and Corollary
3.3.4
remain valid when
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)
and
{\overline{Z}}_{c}^{1}(X)
are replaced by
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and
{\overline{Z}}_{c}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
.

The Arakelov-Chow group
\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
when
X
is projective

When
X
is an integral normal projective arithmetic surface, the constructions in
3.5.1
specialize to the generalization of the classical arithmetic intersection theory on projective arithmetic surfaces introduced in
[
Bos99
]
. Indeed they immediately imply the following variant of Scholium
3.3.5
:

Proposition 3.5.3

(see
[
Bos99
, Section 5.3]
)
.

If
X
is an integral normal projective arithmetic surface, then one defines a symmetric bilinear pairing:

.\cdot.:\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}\times\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

by setting:

[(D,g)]\cdot[(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})]:=(D,g)\cdot(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})

for any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
.

This pairing is the unique bilinear pairing such that the following equality holds:

[(D,g)]\cdot[(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})]=\widehat{\rm deg}\,D\cdot D^{\prime}+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\ast g^{\prime},

for any two Arakelov divisors
(D,g)
and
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
such that
D_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
D^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
have disjoint supports.

The intersection pairing in Proposition
3.5.3
satisfies an analogue of the Hodge Index Theorem concerning the intersection pairing on the Néron-Severi group of a smooth projective surface over field, that has been proved in the setting of the original arithmetic intersection theory of Arakelov
[
Ara74
]
by Faltings
[
Fal84
]
and Hriljac
[
Hri85
]
. We refer the reader to
[
Bos99
, Section 5.5]
for details and references.

In this memoir, we will use the following simple form of this arithmetic Hodge Index Theorem:

Proposition 3.5.4
.

Let
X
be an integral normal projective arithmetic surface. If
{\overline{H}}
is an element of
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
such that:

(3.5.2)

{\overline{H}}\cdot{\overline{H}}>0,

then, for every
{\overline{C}}
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
such that:

{\overline{H}}\cdot{\overline{C}}=0,

the following inequality holds:

{\overline{C}}\cdot{\overline{C}}\leq 0.

Recall that Arakelov divisors
{\overline{H}}
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
that satisfy the condition (
3.5.2
) of positive self-intersection are easily constructed. Indeed, if
{\overline{H}}_{0}:=(H,g)
is an Arakelov divisor on
X
such that the degree
\deg H_{\mathbb{Q}}
of the divisor
H_{\mathbb{Q}}
on the projective curve
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
is positive, then for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
large enough, the Arakelov divisor
{\overline{H}}:=(H,g+\lambda)
satisfies (
3.5.2
), since:

{\overline{H}}\cdot{\overline{H}}={\overline{H}}_{0}\cdot{\overline{H}}_{0}+2\lambda\deg H_{\mathbb{Q}}.

3.5.2.
Functoriality properties

In this subsection, we denote by
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
a
dominant
morphism between two integral normal arithmetic surfaces, and we denote by
\deg f
its degree, namely the positive integer defined as the degree of the field extension:

(3.5.3)

f^{\ast}:\kappa(X)\longrightarrow\kappa(X^{\prime}).

To the map
f
are associated a pull-back and a push-forward map between suitable spaces of Arakelov divisors with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity on
X
and
X^{\prime}
, namely the following morphisms of
\mathbb{Z}
-modules:

(3.5.4)

f^{\ast}:\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}},\quad(Z,g)\longmapsto(f^{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}g)

and:

(3.5.5)

f_{\ast}:\overline{Z}_{c}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{Z}_{c}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}},\quad(Z,g)\longmapsto(f_{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g).

When moreover the morphism
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
is proper, we may also define the following maps:

(3.5.6)

f^{\ast}:\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}},\quad(Z,g)\longmapsto(f^{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}g)

and:

(3.5.7)

f_{\ast}:\overline{Z}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},\quad(Z,g)\longmapsto(f_{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g).

Proposition 3.5.5
.

If
f
is proper, then, for every
{\overline{Z}}
in
\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},
the following equality holds:

(3.5.8)

f_{\ast}f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}=\deg\!f\;{\overline{Z}}.

Proof.

This follows from the validity of similar formulas, concerning Cartier divisors in “classical” algebraic geometry, as established in
[
Ful98
, Proof of Proposition 2.3 (c)]
, and concerning
{L^{2}_{1}}
functions and Green functions.
∎

The maps (
3.5.4
) and (
3.5.7
) are compatible with linear equivalence. To formulate this compatibility, recall that to the field extension (
3.5.3
) is associated a norm map:

N_{\kappa(X^{\prime})/\kappa(X)}:\kappa(X^{\prime})\longrightarrow\kappa(X),

which defines a morphism of multiplicative groups from
\kappa(X^{\prime})^{\times}
to
\kappa(X)^{\times}.

Proposition 3.5.6
.

1) For every rational function
\varphi\in\kappa(X)^{\times},
the following equality holds in
\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}}}^{1}(X^{\prime})
:

(3.5.9)

f^{\ast}\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\varphi=\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}(f^{\ast}\varphi).

2) When the morphism
f
is proper, for every rational function
\varphi^{\prime}\in\kappa(X^{\prime})^{\times},
the following equality holds in
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)
:

(3.5.10)

f_{\ast}\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\varphi^{\prime}=\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}N_{\kappa(X^{\prime})/\kappa(X)}(\varphi^{\prime}).

Proof.

The relations (
3.5.9
) and (
3.5.10
) are straightforward consequences of the definitions, and for (
3.5.10
), of the similar relation in algebraic geometry — established in
[
Ful98
, Proposition 1.4 (b) and Example 20.1.3]
— and from Proposition
3.4.6
.
∎

Corollary 3.5.7
.

The map (
3.5.4
) defines a pull-back morphism between Arakelov-Chow groups:

(3.5.11)

f^{\ast}:\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}},\quad[(Z,g)]\longmapsto[(f^{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}g)].

When moreover
f
is proper, the map (
3.5.7
) defines a push-forward morphism between Arakelov-Chow groups:

(3.5.12)

f_{\ast}:\overline{CH}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}}\longrightarrow\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},\quad[(Z,g)]\longmapsto[(f_{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g)].

The pull-back and push-forward maps defined in (
3.5.4
) and (
3.5.7
) and the Arakelov intersection pairing satisfy the usual projection formula:

Proposition 3.5.8
.

For every
{\overline{Z}}
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and every
{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}},
the following equality holds:

(3.5.13)

f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}\cdot{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}={\overline{Z}}\cdot f_{\ast}{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}.

When moreover
f
is proper, the equality (
3.5.13
) holds for every
{\overline{Z}}
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and every
{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}}.

Proof.

Consider some Arakelov cycles with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity
{\overline{Z}}:=(Z,g)
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{\mathrm{Cart}}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and
{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}:=(Z^{\prime},g^{\prime})
in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X^{\prime})^{L^{2}_{1}}.

Both sides of (
3.5.13
) are unchanged when
{\overline{Z}}
is replaced by a linearly equivalent Arakelov-Cartier divisor; this follows from Proposition
3.3.1
, which remains valid when the Green current
g
has
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity as observed in Corollary
3.5.2
, and from Proposition
3.5.6
, 1).

Accordingly, to establish (
3.5.13
), we may assume that
|Z|
and
f(|Z^{\prime}|)
do not meet in
X_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then
|f^{\ast}Z|
and
|Z^{\prime}|
do not meet in
X^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and according to the extension of Proposition
3.3.3

mentioned in Corollary
3.5.2
, we have:

(3.5.14)

f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}\cdot{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}=(f^{\ast}Z,f_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}g)\cdot(Z^{\prime},g^{\prime})=\widehat{\rm deg}\,f^{\ast}Z\cdot Z^{\prime}+\int_{X^{\prime}(\mathbb{C})}f^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}g\ast g^{\prime}

and:

(3.5.15)

{\overline{Z}}\cdot f_{\ast}{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}=(Z,g)\cdot(f_{\ast}Z^{\prime},f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g^{\prime})=\widehat{\rm deg}\,Z\cdot f_{\ast}Z^{\prime}+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\ast f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g^{\prime}.

The equality (
3.5.13
) will follow from the following two equalities:

(3.5.16)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,f^{\ast}Z\cdot Z^{\prime}=\widehat{\rm deg}\,Z\cdot f_{\ast}Z^{\prime}

and:

(3.5.17)

\int_{X^{\prime}(\mathbb{C})}f^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}g\ast g^{\prime}=\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\ast f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g^{\prime}.

The equality (
3.5.16
) follows from the equality of
0
-cycles:

f_{\ast}(f^{\ast}Z\cdot Z^{\prime})=Z\cdot f_{\ast}Z^{\prime},

that holds up to
linear equivalence supported by the scheme
|Z|\cap f(|Z^{\prime}|)
, which is proper over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
with empty generic fiber.
This equality is an instance of the projection formula in algebraic geometry, as established in
[
Ful98
, Proposition 2.3 (c)]
.

Finally (
3.5.17
) follows from Proposition
3.4.5
.

The second part of the proposition, valid when
f
is proper, is established by a similar argument, which we leave to the reader.
∎

Corollary 3.5.9
.

When
f
is proper, for every
{\overline{Z}}_{1}
in
\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}

and every
{\overline{Z}}_{2}
in
\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
, the following equality holds:

(3.5.18)

f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}_{1}\cdot f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}_{2}=\deg\!f\;{\overline{Z}}_{1}\cdot{\overline{Z}}_{2}.

Proof.

This follows from the first part of Proposition
3.5.8
applied to
{\overline{Z}}={\overline{Z}}_{1}
and to
{\overline{Z}}^{\prime}=f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}_{2}
, and from Proposition
3.5.5
applied to
{\overline{Z}}={\overline{Z}}_{2}
.
∎

Since a suitable positive multiple of an Arakelov divisor is an Arakelov-Cartier divisor, it is possible to define
f^{\ast}{\overline{Z}}
as a “
\mathbb{Q}
-Arakelov divisor” on
X^{\prime}
for any Arakelov divisor
{\overline{Z}}
on
X
. The intersection pairing and the direct image maps also extends to
\mathbb{Q}
-Arakelov divisors, and Propositions
3.5.5
and
3.5.8
and Corollary
3.5.9
remain valid in this more general setting.

3.5.3.
Application: bounding the degree of morphisms between arithmetic surfaces

Using the formalism of arithmetic intersection theory developed in the previous sections, the statements and the proofs of Propositions
1.1.1
and
1.1.2
may be transposed to the framework of arithmetic surfaces and Arakelov intersection theory.

Theorem 3.5.10
.

Let
U
and
V
be two integral normal arithmetic surfaces, and let:

f:V\longrightarrow U

be a dominant morphism of schemes. Let
{\overline{B}}:=(B,g_{B})
be an Arakelov divisor in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(V)^{L^{2}_{1}}
, and let:

{\overline{A}}=f_{\ast}{\overline{B}}:=(f_{\ast}B,f_{\mathbb{C}\ast}g_{B})\in{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(U)^{L^{2}_{1}}

be its direct image by
f
.

If the self intersection
\overline{B}\cdot\overline{B}
is positive, then the degree
\deg f:=[\kappa(V):f^{\ast}\kappa(U)]
of
f
satisfies the following inequality:

(3.5.19)

\deg f\leq\frac{{\overline{A}}\cdot{\overline{A}}}{{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{B}}}.

Proof.

As indicated above, the proof of Theorem
3.5.10
follows the same lines as the proofs of Propositions
1.1.1
and
1.1.2
. Since Theorem
3.5.10
plays a central role in this memoir, we provide some details.

Firstly we may assume that
U
and
V
are respectively open subschemes of some integral normal
projective
arithmetic surfaces, and that
f
extends to a morphism from
Y
to
X
, which we will still denote by
f
.
Mutatis mutandis
, this follows from the construction in the proof of Proposition
1.1.2
.

After possibly multiplying
{\overline{B}}
and therefore
{\overline{A}}
by some positive integer — which leaves the right-hand side of (
3.5.19
) unchanged — we may also assume that
{\overline{A}}
is an Arakelov-Cartier divisor.

Let us denote:

\delta:=\deg f.

For every Arakelov-Cartier divisor
{\overline{D}}
on
X
, we have:

f^{\ast}{\overline{A}}\cdot f^{\ast}{\overline{D}}=\delta{\overline{A}}\cdot{\overline{D}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\overline{B}}\cdot f^{\ast}{\overline{D}}=f_{\ast}{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{D}},

as a consequence of Corollary
3.5.9
and of the projection formula in Proposition
3.5.8
. Consequently we have:

(3.5.20)

(f^{*}{\overline{A}}-\delta{\overline{B}})\cdot f^{*}{\overline{D}}=f^{*}{\overline{A}}\cdot f^{*}{\overline{D}}-\delta\,{\overline{B}}\cdot f^{*}{\overline{D}}=\delta\,{\overline{A}}\cdot{\overline{D}}-\delta\,f_{*}{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{D}}=0.

The projection formula also implies the equality:

f^{\ast}{\overline{A}}\cdot{\overline{B}}={\overline{A}}\cdot f_{\ast}{\overline{B}}.

Therefore, applied to
{\overline{D}}={\overline{A}}
, (
3.5.20
) implies:

(3.5.21)

(f^{*}{\overline{A}}-\delta{\overline{B}})\cdot(f^{*}{\overline{A}}-\delta{\overline{B}})=-\delta\,f^{*}{\overline{A}}\cdot{\overline{B}}+\delta^{2}\,{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{B}}=-\delta\,{\overline{A}}\cdot f_{\ast}{\overline{B}}+\delta^{2}\,{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{B}}=-\delta\,{\overline{A}}\cdot{\overline{A}}+\delta^{2}\,{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{B}}.

Let us choose an Arakelov-Cartier divisor
{\overline{H}}
over
X
with positive self-intersection. Again as a consequence of Corollary
3.5.9
, we have:

(3.5.22)

f^{*}{\overline{H}}\cdot f^{*}{\overline{H}}=\delta\,{\overline{H}}\cdot{\overline{H}}>0.

Moreover, according to (
3.5.20
) applied to
{\overline{D}}={\overline{H}}
, we have:

(3.5.23)

(f^{*}{\overline{A}}-\delta{\overline{B}})\cdot f^{*}{\overline{H}}=0.

From (
3.5.22
), (
3.5.23
) and the Hodge index inequality on the arithmetic surface
Y
(cf. Proposition
3.5.4
), we obtain:

(f^{*}A-\delta B)\cdot(f^{*}A-\delta B)\leq 0.

Together with (
3.5.21
), this proves (
3.5.19
) when
{\overline{B}}\cdot{\overline{B}}
is positive.
∎

Chapter 4
Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity

A Green function with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity associated to a divisor
D
on a Riemann surface
M
is a locally
L^{p}
function on
M\setminus|D|
for every
p\in(1,+\infty)
, but is possibly not essentially bounded on every non-empty open subset of
M
. Accordingly its value at a specific point of
M\setminus|D|
is not defined in general.

For this reason our initial definition of the Arakelov intersection number by the equality (
3.3.4
), namely:

(D,g)\cdot(D^{\prime},g^{\prime}):=\widehat{\rm deg}\,\big(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D,g)|D^{\prime}\big)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g^{\prime}\,\omega(g),

does not make sense in general when the Green function
g
is only assumed to have
L^{2}_{1}
regularity. Indeed, according to the above observation, it is already the case of the height
\widehat{\rm deg}\,\big(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D,g)|D^{\prime}\big),
and the integral
\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g^{\prime}\,\omega(g)
also does not make sense for a general Green function
g
with
L^{2}_{1}
regularity.

In this chapter, we introduce a class of regularity for Green functions and Hermitian metrics, the
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity, that is intermediate between the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
and the
L^{2}_{1}
regularity. The above expression for the intersection pairing of Arakelov divisors with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
Green functions will still be valid when working with Arakelov divisors with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green functions. At the same time,
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity will be much more flexible than
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity. Notably, like
L^{2}_{1}
regularity, it will be compatible with direct images by finite analytic morphisms between Riemann surfaces. Moreover the Green functions associated to compact Riemann surfaces with boundary, which will play a key role in the next chapters, turn out to have
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity.

4.1.
The spaces
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
and
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)

In this section, we denote by
M
a Riemann surface.

4.1.1.
Definitions

Definitions 4.1.1
.

We denote by
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
the space of continuous functions
f:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the current
i\partial\overline{\partial}f
is a Radon measure on
M
.

We denote by
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
the space of real Radon measures
\mu
on
M
that may be written locally on
M
as
i\partial\overline{\partial}f

for some continuous function
f
.

The space
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
may be thought of as a complex analogue of the space
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{bv}}(U)
of continuous functions with bounded variation on an open subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}
. Indeed a continuous function on
U
has bounded variation if and only if the current
df
is a measure.

The superscript
{\mathrm{cp}}
in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
stands for
c
ontinuous
p
otential.

4.1.2.
Basic properties

In this subsection, we state some basic properties of the spaces
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
and
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
. These properties are consequences of the basic properties of the operator
\partial\overline{\partial}
on a Riemann surface, and their proofs will be left to the reader.

Proposition 4.1.2
.

A continuous real-valued function
f
on
M
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
if and only if the current
i\partial\overline{\partial}f
belongs to
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
.

If
M
is compact and connected, we have an exact sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces:

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\,{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}\,\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\partial\overline{\partial}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow 0.

If
M
is connected and not compact, we have exact sequences of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces:

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(M)\,{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}\,\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\partial\overline{\partial}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)\longrightarrow 0

and

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{c}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\partial\overline{\partial}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbf{M}}_{c}^{cp}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mathcal{H}(M)
denotes the space of real-valued harmonic functions on
M
, and
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
(resp.
{\mathbf{M}}_{c}^{cp}(M)
) the subspace of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
(resp. of
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
) defined by its elements with compact support.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

A function
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
is locally
L^{2}_{1}
on
M
. A measure
\mu
in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
is a locally
L^{2}_{-1}
current on
M
. Moreover, when
{\mathrm{supp}\,}f\cap{\mathrm{supp}\,}\mu
is compact, the pairing:

\int_{M}f\,\mu

of
f
and
\mu
in which
f
is considered as a function on
M
and
\mu
as a measure coincides with the pairing of
f
and
\mu
considered as
L^{2}_{1}
and
L^{2}_{-1}
currents respectively.

In particular, when
M
is compact, the Dirichlet scalar product of two elements

f_{1}
and
f_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)

satisfies:

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Dir}}=i\int_{M}\partial f_{1}\wedge\overline{\partial}f_{2}=\int_{M}f_{1}\,i\partial\overline{\partial}f_{2}=\int_{M}f_{2}\,i\partial\overline{\partial}f_{1}.

where the last two integrals are to be understood as the pairing of the continuous function
f_{\alpha}
and the real measure
i\partial\overline{\partial}f_{\beta}
, with
\{\alpha,\beta\}=\{1,2\}
.

The following additional properties of the spaces
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
and
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
will be useful when constructing
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
Green functions.

Proposition 4.1.4
.

(1) The
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
is a subalgebra of the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{C}^{0}(M)
of continuous functions on
M
.

(2) Let
I
be an interval of
\mathbb{R}
and let
f
be an element of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
with values in
I
. If
\chi:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a function of class
1
1

1

namely a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-function with locally Lipschitz derivative.

\mathcal{C}^{1+\mathrm{Lip}}
, then the composition
\chi\circ f
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
.

(3) If
\mu
is a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
and
\rho
is a function in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
such that the measure
i\partial\overline{\partial}\rho
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure
2
2

2

or, equivalently, if
i\partial\overline{\partial}\rho
is defined by a
2
-form that is locally
L^{1}
.
, then the measure
\rho\mu
lies in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
.

4.2.
Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity and
\ast
-products

In this section, we denote by
M
a Riemann surface.

4.2.1.
Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity and Hermitian line bundles with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
metric

We define a
Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity
or, for short, a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function
for a divisor
D
on
M
is defined
as in
3.2.1
by allowing the function
h
appearing in (
3.2.1
) to be locally
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
over
U
, instead of being
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

According to the ellipticity of operator
\partial\overline{\partial}
on
M
, a real distribution
g
on
M
is a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function for
D
on
M
if and only if the current:

\omega(g):=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g+\delta_{D}

is a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
.

According to Proposition
4.1.3
, a Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity is a Green function with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity.

Let
{\overline{L}}=(L,\|.\|)
be a Hermitian line bundle over
M
. We will say that the Hermitian metric is
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
when it satisfies the following equivalent conditions, where
\|.\|_{0}
denotes a
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
metric on
L
:

(1)

there exists a function
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}(M)
such that
\|.\|=e^{-f}\|.\|_{0};

(2)

for every non-vanishing
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
section
s
of
L
over some open subset
U
of
M,
the continuous function
\|s\|
on
U
belongs to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}(U)
.

When this holds, the “first Chern form”
c_{1}({\overline{L}})
of
{\overline{L}}
, defined locally by the equality (
3.2.3
), is a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
. With the notation of (i), we have:

c_{1}({\overline{L}})=c_{1}(L,\|.\|_{0})+\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}f.

The datum of a Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity for a divisor
D
in
M
is equivalent to the datum of a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}(D)
. As in
3.2.1
, the Green function
g
is associated to the metric
\|.\|_{g}
such that:

\|\mathbbm{1}_{D}\|_{g}=e^{-g}\quad\mbox{on $M\setminus|D|.$}

Moreover the definition of the capacitary metrics in
3.2.1
still makes sense for Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity.

4.2.2.
Approximating Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity by
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
functions

The following approximation results will play a key role when dealing with the
\ast
-products of Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Let
D=\sum_{P}n_{P}P
be a divisor in
M
with finite support, let
g
be a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function with compact support for
D
in
M
, and let

\mu
be a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M).

(1) There exists a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}_{c}(M)
such that:

(1)

for any neighborhood
V
of
|D|
in
M
, we have:

g_{n}=g\quad\mbox{on $M\setminus V$}

for
n
large enough;

(2)

the sequence of measures
\big(i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)\big)
converges weakly to
0
;

(3)

for every
P
in
|D|
such that
n_{P}>0
(resp.
n_{P}<0
), the sequence
(g_{n})
is increasing (resp. decreasing) on some neighborhood of
P
in
M
.

(2) For every sequence
(g_{n})
in
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
satisfying conditions
1
and
2
, the limit:

(4.2.1)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{M}g_{n}\,\mu

exists in
\mathbb{R}
. It is independent of the choice of the sequence
(g_{n})
in
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
satisfying conditions
1
and
2
.

Regarding condition
2
, observe that the current:

(4.2.2)

i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)=i\partial\overline{\partial}g_{n}-\pi\omega(g)+\pi\delta_{D}

is indeed a real measure on
M
. The sequence of measures
\big(i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)\big)
is said to converge weakly to
0
when, for every
\varphi
in
\mathcal{C}^{0}_{c}(M)
, we have:

(4.2.3)

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{M}\varphi\,i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)=0.

This holds precisely when the sequence of total masses
\big(|||i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)|||\big)
of the signed measures (
4.2.2
) is bounded, and when (
4.2.3
) holds for every
\varphi
in
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(M)
.

Proof.

(1) The sequence
(g_{n})
may be constructed by a standard truncation procedure. Namely, for every point
P
in
|D|
, choose a complex analytic chart:

z_{P}:U_{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D(0,1)

defined on some open neighborhood
U_{P}
of
P
in
X
, such that
z_{P}(P)=0.
We may assume that the open subsets
(U_{P})_{P\in|D|}
of
X
are pairwise disjoint. For every
P
in
|D|
, the restriction of the Green function
g
to
U_{P}
may be written:

g_{|U_{P}}=n_{P}\log|z_{P}|^{-1}+h_{P}

for some function
h_{P}
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(U_{P}).

For any real number
r
with
0<r<1
, we define a function
\widetilde{g}_{r}
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
by letting:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{r}(x)

\displaystyle:=n_{P}\min(\log r^{-1},\log|z_{P}|^{-1})+h_{P}(x)\quad\quad\mbox{if $x\in U_{P}$},

\displaystyle:=g(x)\quad\quad\quad\quad\mbox{if $x\in M\setminus\coprod_{P\in|D|}U_{P}.$}

Indeed, over
U_{P}
, the following equality of currents holds:

\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\widetilde{g}_{r}=n_{P}\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\log^{-}\frac{r}{|z_{P}|}+\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}h_{P}

and, consequently, over
M
, we have:

\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}(\widetilde{g}_{r}-g)=\sum_{P\in|D|}n_{P}(\delta_{P}-z_{P}^{*}\mu_{r}),

where
\mu_{r}
is the measure on the open unit disk
D(0,1)
defined by:

\mu_{r}(\varphi):=\int_{0}^{1}\varphi(e^{2i\pi t}\,r)\,dt.

Clearly, the measure
i\partial\overline{\partial}(\widetilde{g}_{r}-g)
converges weakly to
0
as
r
goes to
0
, and, for every decreasing sequence
(r_{n})
in
(0,1)
converging to
0
, the sequence
(g_{n}):=(\widetilde{g}_{r_{n}})
satisfies Conditions
a., b., and c..

(2) Consider a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}_{c}(M)
satisfying conditions
1
and
2
. Let us choose some complex analytic charts
(z_{P})_{P\in|D|}
as in (1) and, for every
P\in|D|
, choose
\varphi_{P}
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(U_{P})
such that:

\mu_{|U_{P}}=i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{P}.

According to
1
, there exists a positive integer
N
and a compact subset
K
of
\bigcup_{P\in|D|}U_{P}
such that, for any
n\geq N
,
g
coincides with
g_{n}
on
M\setminus K
. In particular, if
n
and
n^{\prime}
are two integers with
n,n^{\prime}\geq N
, the function
(g_{n^{\prime}}-g_{n})_{|U_{P}}
is compactly supported, and therefore, according to Green’s formula:

(4.2.4)

\int_{U_{P}}(g_{n^{\prime}}-g_{n})\,\mu=\int_{U_{P}}(g_{n^{\prime}}-g_{n})\,i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{P}=\int_{U_{P}}\varphi_{P}\,i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n^{\prime}}-g_{n}).

According to condition
2
, the compactly supported measure
i\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n^{\prime}}-g_{n})
on
U_{P}
converges weakly to
0
when
\min(n,n^{\prime})
goes to infinity. Together with (
4.2.4
), this proves that
\int_{U_{P}}(g_{n^{\prime}}-g_{n})\mu
converges to
0
when
\min(n,n^{\prime})
goes to infinity, and therefore that the sequence
\big(\int_{U_{P}}g_{n}\mu\big)
converges in
\mathbb{R}
.

Since, for every
n\geq N
, we have:

\int_{M}g_{n}\,\mu=\sum_{P\in|D|}\int_{U_{P}}g_{n}\,\mu+\int_{M\setminus\bigcup_{P\in|D|}U_{P}}g\,\mu,

this proves the existence of the limit (
4.2.1
). Its independence of the choice of a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfying
1
and
2
is clear.
∎

With the notation of Proposition
4.2.1
, we may
define
the quantity
\int_{M}g\,\mu
as follows:

(4.2.5)

\int_{M}g\,\mu:=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{M}g_{n}\,\mu.

One should beware that, in spite of the existence of the limit in the right-hand side of (
4.2.5
), the Green function
g
might not be integrable with respect to the positive measure
|\mu|
. However, this kind of pathology is seldom encountered in practice, thanks to the following consequence of Proposition
4.2.1
:

Corollary 4.2.2
.

With the notation of Proposition
4.2.1
, assume that every element
P
of
|D|
admits a neighborhood
U
in
M
such that either
\mu_{\mid U}
, the restriction of
\mu
to
U
, or
-\mu_{\mid U}
is a positive measure. Then the Green function
g
is integrable with respect to
|\mu|
, and the integral
\int_{M}g\,\mu
of
g
with respect to
\mu
coincides with the limit of the sequence
\left(\int_{M}g_{n}\,\mu\right).

Proof.

It is enough to show that, if
P
is any point of
|D|
, the Green function
g
is integrable with respect to
|\mu|
on some neighborhood
U
of
P
in
M
. To prove this, we may assume that the divisor
D
is actually
P
itself. In this situation, the Corollary follows from Proposition
4.2.1
(2) applied to a sequence
(g_{n})
satisfying conditions a., b., and c. and from Lebesgue’s Monotone Convergence Theorem.
∎

4.2.3.
The
\ast
-product of Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let
D
be a divisor in
M
with finite support, let
g
be a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function for
D
with compact support, and let
\varphi
be a function in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
. Then the following equality holds:

\int_{M}g\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=\int_{M}\varphi\,\omega(g)-\int_{M}\varphi\,\delta_{D},

where the left-hand side is defined by the limit procedure (
4.2.5
) with
\mu=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi.

Proof.

Choose a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfying conditions
1
and
2
in Proposition
4.2.1
. For any positive integer
n
, we have:

(4.2.6)

\int_{M}g_{n}\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi=\int_{M}\varphi\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g_{n}=\int_{M}\varphi\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)+\int_{M}\varphi\,(\omega(g)-\delta_{D}).

According to b., the quantity
\int_{M}\varphi\,\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}(g_{n}-g)
in (
4.2.6
) converges to
0
when
n
goes to infinity.
∎

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be two divisors on
M
with disjoint supports, and let
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
be two Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity for
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. If
{\mathrm{supp}\,}g_{1}\cap{\mathrm{supp}\,}g_{2}
is compact, then the following equality holds:

(4.2.7)

\int_{M}g_{1}\ast g_{2}=\int_{M}g_{2}\,\delta_{D_{1}}+\int_{M}g_{1}\,\omega(g_{2}).

The left-hand side of (
4.2.7
) is defined by means of the construction in Subsection
3.4.1
, since
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are Green functions with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity.

In the right-hand side of (
4.2.7
), the first integral is well-defined since
g_{2}
is continuous on
M\setminus|D_{2}|
, which contains
|D_{1}|
. The second integral is defined by the equality (
4.2.5
). Corollary
4.2.2
shows that it is the actual integral of an integrable function when the measure
\omega(g_{2})
has a well-defined sign on some neighborhood of any point of
|D_{1}|.

Proof.

Using the validity of Property
\mathbf{SP}_{1}
in the
{L^{2}_{1}}
framework discussed in
3.4.1
above and Proposition
4.1.4
, one readily sees that, to prove (
4.2.7
), one may assume that
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are compactly supported. Then the validity of (
4.2.7
) easily follows from its validity when
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity, and from Property
\mathbf{SP}_{2}
and Propositions
4.1.3
and
4.2.3
. We leave the details to the interested reader.
∎

4.3.
Examples

4.3.1.
Equilibrium potentials on compact Riemann surfaces with boundary

As in
[
Bos20
, 10.5.1]
, we define a
Riemann surface with boundary
as a pair
(V,V^{+})
where
V^{+}
is a (germ of a) Riemann surface (without boundary) along a closed
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
submanifold with boundary
V
of
V^{+}
, of codimension
0
in
V^{+}
.

The
interior
of
(V,V^{+})
is defined as the interior
\mathring{V}
of
V
, and its
boundary
is:

\partial V:=V\setminus V^{+}.

We say that
(V,V^{+})
is compact (resp. connected) when
V
is.

For simplicity’s sake, we shall often write
V
for the Riemann surface with boundary
(V,V^{+}),
but shall denote by:

\alpha:V^{+}\longrightarrow N

a map from
(V,V^{+})
to some manifold
N
to emphasize that
\alpha
is a germ of map along
V
. For instance, when
N
is a complex analytic manifold, an analytic map
\alpha
as above will be a map analytic up to the boundary.

Let
V
be a
connected compact
Riemann surface with boundary such that
\partial V

is nonempty
, and let
P
be a point of the interior
\mathring{V}
of
V
.

One defines the
Green function

g_{V,P}
of
P
in
V
as the unique function:

g_{V,P}:V^{+}\setminus\{P\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

g_{V,P}
is continuous on
V^{+}\setminus\{P\}
and vanishes on
V^{+}\setminus\mathring{V};

(2)

g_{V,P}
is harmonic on
\mathring{V}\setminus\{P\};

(3)

g_{V,P}
admits a logarithmic singularity at
P
; namely, if
z
denotes a uniformizing parameter on
V
at
P
, the difference
g_{V,P}-\log|z|^{-1}
is bounded on some (pointed) neighborhood of
P
in
\mathring{V}
.

See for instance instance
[
Tay11
, 5.1-2]
, or
[
Bos99
, Appendix]
for a construction in a more general setting.

The function
g_{V,P}
is a Green function with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}
regularity for
P
in
V^{+}
, supported on
V
and the measure:

\mu_{V,P}:=\omega(g,P)=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g_{V,P}+\delta_{P}

is supported on the boundary
\partial V
.
In particular, the restriction of
g_{V,P}
to the Riemann surface
\mathring{V}
is a Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity for
P
in
\mathring{V}
. A straightforward application of the maximum principle for harmonic functions shows that
g_{V,P}
is positive on
\mathring{V}
.

The measure
\mu_{V,P}
is the so-called
harmonic measure
attached to the point
P
in
V
. It is a probability measure defined by a positive
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
density on the smooth compact curve
\partial V
.

The capacitary metric on
T_{P}V
associated to
g_{V,P}
will be denoted by:

\|.\|_{V,P}^{\mathrm{cap}}:=\|.\|_{g_{V,P}}^{\mathrm{cap}}.

Example 4.3.1
.

When
(V,P)=(\overline{D}(0,1),0)
, we have:

g_{V,P}=\log^{+}|z|^{-1}

and therefore:

\|(\partial/\partial z)_{|P}\|^{\mathrm{cap}}_{\overline{D}(0,1),0}=1.

4.3.2.
Some integrability results

Let
\Omega
be an open neighborhood of
{\overline{D}}(0,1)
in
\mathbb{C}
and let
\mu
be a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\Omega)
.

We may apply Proposition
4.2.1
to the Green function with
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity for the point
0
in
\mathbb{C}
:

g:=\log^{+}|z|^{-1},

introduced in Example
4.3.1
, and to its truncations:

g_{n}:=\min(g,\log r_{n}^{-1}),

where
(r_{n})
denotes a decreasing sequence in
(0,1)
, of limit
0
.
We obtain the existence of the limit:

\int_{{\overline{D}}(0,1)}\log|z|^{-1}\,d\mu(z):=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\Big(\int_{{\overline{D}}(0,1)\setminus{\overline{D}}(0,r_{n})}\log|z|^{-1}\,d\mu(z)+\log r_{n}^{-1}\mu({\overline{D}}(0,r_{n}))\Big).

Moreover, if
\mu
is positive, then
\log|z|^{-1}
is
\mu
-integrable near
0
, as already observed in Corollary
4.2.2
.

Any continuous subharmonic function on a Riemann surface
M
, and consequently the difference
f
of two such functions, is an element of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
, by the very definition of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(M)
.

Moreover, if
z
is a local coordinate on
M
, the function
\log|z|^{-1}
is locally integrable with respect to the measure
|\mu|
where:

\mu:=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}f.

This follows directly from Corollary
4.2.2
.

4.3.3.
Some wild
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}
functions on
\mathring{D}(0,1)

Let us emphasize that there exist instances of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
functions
f
for which the above property of integrability of
\log|z|^{-1}
with respect to the measure
|\partial\overline{\partial}f|
does not hold. Such functions may not be written locally as the difference of two continuous subharmonic functions.

For instance, let
\alpha
be a real number with
1<\alpha<2
and consider the function
\varphi_{\alpha}:\mathring{D}(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by:

\varphi_{\alpha}(z)=(\log|z|^{-1})^{-\alpha}\cos(\log|z|^{-1})\quad\mbox{if $z\in\mathring{D}(0,1)\setminus\{0\}$},

and:

\varphi_{\alpha}(0)=0.

Then
\varphi_{\alpha}
belongs to
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}(\mathring{D}(0,1)),
but
\log|z|^{-1}
is not integrable with respect to
|\mu_{\alpha}|
near
0
, where:

\mu_{\alpha}:=\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi_{\alpha},

Relatedly, for every
\alpha
with
1<\alpha<2
, the measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{\alpha}
on
\mathring{D}(0,1)
defined by:

\widetilde{\mu}_{\alpha}:=|z|^{-2}(\log|z|^{-1})^{-\alpha}\cos(\log|z|^{-1})\,dx\,dy

is an element of
{\mathbf{M}}(\mathring{D}(0,1))^{{\mathrm{cp}}}
such that
\log|z|^{-1}
is not integrable with respect to
|\widetilde{\mu}_{\alpha}|
near
0
, and cannot be written, on any open neighborhood
\Omega
of
0
in
\mathring{D}(0,1)
, as the difference of two positive measures in
{\mathbf{M}}(\Omega)^{{\mathrm{cp}}}
.

We leave the proofs of these assertions to the interested reader.

4.4.
Functoriality of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green functions

4.4.1.
Functoriality of functions and measures on Riemann surfaces

In paragraph
3.4.2
, we recalled the functorial constructions — the pull-back
f^{\ast}
of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
-forms and the push-forward
f_{\ast}
of currents — associated to a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of (complex) manifolds.

The pull-back map
f^{*}
extends to differential forms with continuous coefficients. Dually, the push-forward
f_{*}T
of a current with measure coefficients on
X
is well-defined as a current with measure coefficients on
Y
.

We are interested in the properties of
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
in the situation where
X
and
Y
are Riemann surfaces and the holomorphic map
f
is “nowhere locally constant,” namely, it is non-constant when restricted to any connected component of
X
. In this case, the maps
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
satisfy the following additional properties.

Consider a continuous function
\varphi
on
X
such that
f_{\mid{\mathrm{supp}\,}\varphi}
is proper. Then the push-forward
f_{*}\varphi
is (the current defined by) a continuous function on
Y
, namely the function defined by:

(4.4.1)

f_{*}\varphi(y)=\int_{X}\varphi\,\delta_{f^{*}(y)}.

Here
f^{*}(y)
denotes the divisor on
X
inverse image by
f
of the divisor
y
in
Y
, namely:

f^{*}(y):=\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}e_{x}\,x,

where
e_{x}
is the ramification index of
f
at
x
. If
\varphi
has compact support, then
f_{*}\varphi
has compact support. The expression (
4.4.1
) for
f_{\ast}\varphi
also shows that, if
f
is proper of degree
\delta
, then for any continuous function
\psi
on
Y
, we have:

f_{\ast}f^{\ast}\psi=\delta\psi.

Dually, if
\mu
denotes a measure on
Y
, we may define its inverse image
f^{*}\mu
on
X
as the measure such that, for every continuous function
\varphi
on
X
with compact support, the following equality holds:

(4.4.2)

\int_{X}\varphi\,f^{*}\mu=\int_{Y}f_{*}\varphi\,\mu.

The construction of
f^{*}\mu
is compatible with the weak topology on the space of measures, which is defined by duality with the space of continuous functions with compact support. Moreover, when
\mu
is a
2
-form with continuous coefficients on
Y
, the pull-back measure
f^{*}\mu
coincides with the pull-back of
\mu
as a differential form. Finally, when the measure
\mu
defines a
L^{2}_{-1}
current, the measure
f^{\ast}\mu
defined by (
4.4.2
) coincides with the
L^{2}_{-1}
current
f^{\ast}\mu
defined by the construction in Proposition
3.4.3
1) applied to the

L^{2}_{-1}
current
\mu
.

4.4.2.
Functoriality for
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
functions and
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}
measures

The construction of
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
functions and
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}
measures is compatible with the pull-back and push-forward operations on continuous functions and measures on Riemann surfaces described above:

Proposition 4.4.1
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a holomorphic map between two Riemann surfaces. Assume that
f
is nowhere locally constant.

(1)

The pull-back maps
f^{*}
defined above satisfy:

f^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(Y))\subset\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(X)

and

f^{*}({\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(Y))\subset{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(X).

Moreover, for any
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(Y),
the following equality holds in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(X):

(4.4.3)

f^{*}(i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi)=i\partial\overline{\partial}f^{*}\psi.

(2)

For any
\varphi
in
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(X),
(resp. any
\mu
in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(X)
) such that the restriction of
f
to the support of
\varphi
(resp. to the support of
\mu
) is proper, the direct image
f_{*}\varphi
(resp.
f_{*}\mu
) belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}(Y)
(resp.
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(Y)
).

Proof.

To prove (i), it is enough to show that, for every
\psi
in
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}(Y)
, the equality (
4.4.3
) is satisfied, where
f^{*}(i\partial\overline{\partial}\psi)
is defined by the relation (
4.4.2
) and where
i\partial\overline{\partial}f^{*}\psi
is defined as a current on
X
. This is a consequence of Corollary
3.4.4
and of the compatibility between the pull-back maps
f^{\ast}
on continuous functions and measures and on
L^{2}_{1}
functions and
L^{2}_{-1}
currents.

Assertion (ii) is a straightforward consequence of the compatibility of
\partial\overline{\partial}
with the direct images of currents. This implies the equality of currents:

f_{*}(i\partial\overline{\partial}\varphi)=i\partial\overline{\partial}(f_{*}\varphi),

and proves that
i\partial\overline{\partial}(f_{*}\varphi)
is a measure.
∎

Together with the definition of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green functions, Proposition
4.4.1
implies immediately:

Corollary 4.4.2
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a holomorphic map between two Riemann surfaces. Assume that
f
is nowhere locally constant.

(1)

If
E
is a divisor on
Y
and
g
is a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function on
Y
for
E
, then
f^{*}g
is a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function on
X
for the divisor
f^{*}E
. Moreover, the following equality holds in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(X)
:

f^{*}\omega(g)=\omega(f^{*}g).

(2)

If
D
is a divisor on
X
and
g
is a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function on
X
for
D
such that the restriction of
f
to the support of
g
is proper, then
f_{*}g
is a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green function on
Y
for the divisor
f_{*}D
on
Y
. Moreover, the following equality holds in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(Y)
:

f_{*}\omega(g)=\omega(f_{*}g).

In
(i)
, the pull-back of
f^{*}g
is defined a priori as a
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
function on the complement
X\setminus f^{-1}(|E|)
of the support of
f^{*}E
.

In
(ii)
, the properness of
f
on the support of
g
implies that the restriction of
f
is proper on a neighborhood of
|D|
, which implies that the divisor
f_{*}D
is well-defined.

Observe also that these constructions of direct and inverse images of Green current with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
regularity are compatible with the constructions of direct and inverse images of Green current with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity in Proposition
3.4.5
.

Observe also that part
(i)
of Corollary
4.4.2
holds for Green functions with
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
regularity instead of
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
Green functions. However, part
(ii)
does not hold when
f
is not étale, since the direct image of a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function by a ramified map is not
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in general, as discussed in Subsection
3.2.3
.

Finally the construction in paragraph
4.2.2
of the integral
\int_{M}g\,\mu
where
g
is a
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
Green function with compact support and
\mu
is a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(M)
, satisfies the following projection formulas:

Proposition 4.4.3
.

(i) With the notation of Corollary
4.4.2
, (i), if
{\mathrm{supp}\,}f^{\ast}g
is compact, then for every measure
\mu
in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(X),
the following equality holds:

\int_{X}f^{\ast}g\,\mu=\int_{Y}g\,f_{\ast}\mu.

(ii) With the notation of Corollary
4.4.2
, (ii), if
{\mathrm{supp}\,}g
is compact, then for every measure
\mu
in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(Y),
the following equality holds:

\int_{X}g\,f^{\ast}\mu=\int_{Y}f_{\ast}g\,\mu.

This is a simple consequence of the definitions, and the details of the proof will be left to the reader.

4.5.
Application to intersection theory on arithmetic surfaces

Let
X
be an integral normal arithmetic surface.

4.5.1.
Arakelov divisors with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}
Green functions

By using Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}
regularity instead of Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity in the definitions in
3.3.1
, we define the group
{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}}
of Arakelov divisors on
X
with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
Green functions.

This group lies in between the groups of Arakelov divisors with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
and
{L^{2}_{1}}
Green functions:

{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)\subset{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}\subset{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X)^{{L^{2}_{1}}}.

Similarly we may define its subgroups
\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},

\overline{Z}_{c}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},
and
\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}},c}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},
and the associated Arakelov-Chow groups:

\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}:=\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}/\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}

and

\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}=\overline{Z}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}/\overline{{\mathrm{div}\,}}\kappa(X)^{\times}.

These groups lies between the previously defined Arakelov-Chow groups
\overline{CH}^{1}(X)
and
\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
, and
\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)
and
\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
respectively.

The isomorphism (
3.3.2
) extends to an isomorphism:

(4.5.1)

\widehat{c}_{1}:\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}},

where
\overline{{\rm Pic}}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
denotes the group of isomorphism classes of Hermitian line bundles with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
metric on
X
. As for the isomorphism (
3.3.2
), the inverse of (
4.5.1
) sends the class of some Cartier-Arakelov divisor
(Z,g)\in{\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
to the isomorphism class of the Hermitian line bundle:

\overline{\mathcal{O}}(Z,g):=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D),\|.\|_{g}).

4.5.2.
Heights, arithmetic intersection, and
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
regularity

The following proposition asserts that the relation (
3.3.3
) — which was our starting point when developing the arithmetic intersection theory for Arakelov divisors defined by Green functions with
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity — still holds in the
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
framework.

Proposition 4.5.1
.

For every Arakelov divisor
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
(resp.
(D,g)
) in
{\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
(resp. in
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
), the following equality holds:

(4.5.2)

(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})\cdot(D,g)=\widehat{\rm deg}\,\big(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})|D\big)+\int_{X(\mathbb{C})}g\,\omega(g^{\prime}).

The left-hand side of (
4.5.2
) is defined by the construction in
3.5.1
. Indeed
(D^{\prime},g^{\prime})
and
(D,g)
belong to
{\overline{Z}}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
and
{\overline{Z}}^{1}_{c}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}}
respectively. The integral in the right-hand side of (
4.5.2
) is defined by the construction in
4.2.2
. According to Corollary
4.2.2
, it is an “actual” integral when the measure
\omega(g^{\prime})
has a well-defined sign near each point of
|D_{\mathbb{C}}|.

Proof.

The relation (
4.5.2
) is valid when
g^{\prime}
and
g
have
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}
regularity. Using Propositions
3.5.1
and
4.2.3
, one readily sees that it extends to the general case.
∎

4.5.3.
Functoriality properties

The functoriality properties of
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
functions and of Green functions established in Section
4.4

immediately imply that the pull-back and push-forward maps constructed in
3.5.2
between groups of Arakelov divisors (resp. Arakelov-Chow groups) with
{L^{2}_{1}}
regularity define by restriction some pull-back and push-forward maps between groups of Arakelov divisors (resp. Arakelov-Chow groups) with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
-regularity.

In the next chapters of this memoir, unless otherwise specified, by “Green function,” we shall mean “Green function with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
regularity,” by “Arakelov divisor,” we shall mean “Arakelov divisor with
{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
Green function.”

For simplicity, we will write

{\overline{Z}}^{1}(X),

\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}}}^{1}(X)
,
\overline{Z}_{c}^{1}(X)
, instead of
\overline{Z}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}},

\overline{Z}_{{\mathrm{Cart}}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}},

\overline{Z}_{c}^{1}(X)^{L^{2}_{1}},
and
\overline{CH}^{1}(X)
and
\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)
instead of

\overline{CH}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
and
\overline{CH}_{\mathrm{Cart}}^{1}(X)^{{\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}}
.

Chapter 5
The Archimedean Overflow
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha:(V,P)\rightarrow N)

In this chapter, we introduce an invariant, the “overflow”
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha:(V,P)\rightarrow N)
, attached to
a pointed compact connected Riemann surface with (non-empty) boundary
(V,P)
, a Riemann surface
N
, and a non-constant map, analytic up to the boundary:

\alpha:V\longrightarrow N.

This invariant
will play a key role when computing self-intersections of Arakelov-cycles on arithmetic surfaces attached to morphisms from formal-analytic arithmetic surfaces. In this chapter, we investigate it from a purely analytic perspective. The central result of the chapter — and arguably one of the key computations in this memoir — is the alternative expression for
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha:(V,P)\rightarrow N)
in Theorem
5.4.1
in terms of a Green function on the Riemann surface
N
.

In the special case when
\alpha
is étale at
P
and
N
is compact, the invariant
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)
has been introduced in 2009 in some unpublished work of A. Chambert-Loir and the first named author of this monograph. Propositions
5.1.2
and
5.2.2
were also established in this special case.

5.1.
The invariant
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)

5.1.1.
Definition of
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)

In this section, we consider the following data:

(1)

two Riemann surfaces
M
and
N
, with
M
connected, and a non-constant complex analytic map:

\alpha:M\longrightarrow N;

(2)

a point
P
in
M
, and a Green function
g
(with
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity) with compact support for the divisor
P
in
M
.

We denote by
e
the ramification index of
\alpha
at
P
: if we let
Q:=\alpha(P),

e
is the multiplicity of
P
in the effective divisor
\alpha^{*}(Q)
on
M
.

Definition 5.1.1
.

With the notation above, we let:

(5.1.1)

\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g):=\int_{M}g\,\delta_{\alpha^{*}(Q)-eP}+\int_{N}\alpha_{*}g\,\alpha_{*}\omega(g).

Observe that the first integral in the right-hand-side of (
5.1.1
) is a well-defined real number since the support of the divisor

R:=\alpha^{*}(Q)-eP

does not contain
P
by definition of the ramification index
e
. The second integral also is a well-defined real number; indeed
\alpha_{*}g
is a Green function with compact support and
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{b}\Delta}}
regularity for the divisor
Q
in
N
, and

\alpha_{*}\omega(g)=\omega(\alpha_{*}g)

is a measure in
{\mathbf{M}}^{{\mathrm{cp}}}(N)
, and the construction in
4.2.2
applies.

Using the projection formula in Proposition
4.4.3
, (ii), the definition (
5.1.1
) of the invariant
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)
may be rewritten as follows:

(5.1.2)

\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)=\int_{M}g\left(\delta_{\alpha^{*}(Q)-eP}+\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}\omega(g)\right).

5.1.2.
The invariant
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)
and Deligne pairings

When the Riemann surface
N
is compact and connected, and therefore defines a connected smooth projective complex curve, the invariant
\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha,g)
occurs naturally when one investigates the metric properties of certain canonical isomorphisms that are attached canonically to the pointed Riemann surface
(M,P)
and the map
\alpha:M\rightarrow N
.

We keep the notation of the previous subsection and we assume that
N
is compact and connected.

Consider the canonical isomorphism:

\iota_{1}:T_{Q}N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{P}M^{\otimes e}

defined as the composition of the “obvious” isomorphisms:
