\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X_{1})\geq\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{2}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X_{2})+\log(2).

Proof.

Since
p
is birational,
p
defines an isomorphism between the generic fibers of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. In particular, the ramification indices
e(\gamma_{1})
and
e(\gamma_{2})
coincide. We denote this integer by
e:=e(\gamma_{1})=e(\gamma_{2})
. By definition, for
i=1
and
i=2
, we have:

\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{i}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow S_{i})=\widehat{\deg}(P.(\widehat{\gamma}_{i}^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{i}(P))-eP)).

We have:

\widehat{\gamma}_{1}^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))=(\widehat{\gamma}_{2}^{*}\circ p^{*})(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))=\widehat{\gamma}_{2}^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{2}(P))+\widehat{\gamma}_{2}^{*}(p^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))-\widehat{\gamma}_{2}(P)).

As a consequence, we may write:

(9.1.6)

\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1})=\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{2})+\widehat{\deg}(P.\widehat{\gamma}_{2}^{*}(p^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))-\widehat{\gamma}_{2}(P)).

Since the divisor
p^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))-\widehat{\gamma}_{2}(P)
is clearly effective, this proves (
9.1.4
). The inequality (
9.1.5
) follows from Proposition
7.2.5
.

Assume that equality holds in (
9.1.4
) and let
\overline{S}_{1}
be a normal, projective compactification of
S_{1}
. Let
S^{\prime}_{2}
be a projective compactification of
S_{2}
, and let
\overline{S}_{2}
be the normalization of the closure of the graph of
p
in
\overline{S}_{1}\times S^{\prime}_{2}.
Let
\overline{p}
denote the projection of
\overline{S}_{2}
onto
\overline{S}_{1}
. We obtain a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{j_{2}}
\textstyle{\overline{S}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{p}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{2}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{2}}
\textstyle{\overline{S}_{1}}

in which
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
are open immersions.

We argue by contradiction and assume that
p
– hence
\overline{p}
– is not an isomorphism above a neighborhood of
\gamma_{1}(P)
in
S_{2}
. By Zariski’s main theorem, there exists a nonzero effective Weil divisor
\overline{D}
on
\overline{S}_{2}
that intersects
\gamma_{2}(P)
and such that
\overline{p}(\overline{D})
is a closed point in
\gamma_{1}(P).

Let
D
be the intersection of
\overline{D}
with
S_{2}
. This is a nonempty effective Weil divisor that intersects
\gamma_{2}(P)
. By construction, the effective divisor the divisor
p^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))-\widehat{\gamma}_{2}(P)
contains
D
, namely,

p^{*}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1}(P))-\widehat{\gamma}_{2}(P)-D

is effective. Applying (
9.1.6
), we find:

\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{1})\geq\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{2})+\widehat{\deg}(P.\widehat{\gamma}_{2}^{*}D)>\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\gamma}_{2}),

which finishes the proof.
∎

Blow-ups and contractions

The following statement is standard, at least for morphisms between regular arithmetic surfaces. We offer a short proof for the sake of completeness.

Proposition 9.1.3
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
be a non-constant morphism from
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
to a smooth, quasi-projective scheme over
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. Let
x
be a closed point of
X
lying over a finite prime
\mathfrak{p}
of
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. If the preimage of
x
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
is not finite, then it is equal to the fiber of
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
above
\mathfrak{p}
, and there exists a unique lift
\widehat{\alpha}^{\prime}
of
\widehat{\alpha}
to the blow-up

\pi:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X

of
X
at
x
.

Proof.

We first compute the preimage of
x
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
. This may be done by a local computation as follows: let
\mathfrak{p}
be the nonzero prime ideal of
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
which is the image of
X
, and let
R
be the completion of
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
at
\mathfrak{p}
. Consider a morphism

\mathrm{Spf}R[[X]]\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spf}R[[Y]]

given by a power series

f=\sum_{i\geq 0}a_{i}X^{i}\in R[[X]].

Let
\varpi
be a uniformizer of
R
. This is a local model for
\widehat{\alpha}
near any point in the preimage of
x
. The closed point of
\mathrm{Spf}R[[Y]]
is defined by the ideal
(Y,\varpi)
. Its preimage in
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
is the closed subscheme defined by the ideal
(f,\varpi)
in
R[[X]].
This closed subscheme is not finite if and only if all the
a_{i}
are divisible by
\varpi
, in which case

(f,\varpi)=\varpi.

In particular, the preimage of
x
is the divisor defined by
\varpi
, which is Cartier.

The remaining part of the proposition now follows from the universal property of the blow-up, see e.g.
[
Sta
, Tag 085U]
, whose proof applies in the setting of formal schemes.
∎

Keeping the notation of the previous proposition, a standard computation shows that
\widehat{\alpha}^{\prime}
factors through the smooth locus of the structure map
X^{\prime}\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
.

Let
E
be the irreducible Cartier divisor contracted to
x
– namely, the preimage of
\mathfrak{p}
in
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
. If
D
is a Cartier divisor on
X
containing
x
with multiplicity
1
, we may write

\widehat{\alpha}^{*}D=\widetilde{D}+eE

for some positive integer
e
, where
\widetilde{D}
is an effective Cartier divisor which does not admit
E
as a component – namely,
\widetilde{D}
is the strict transform of
D
. With the notation of the proof,
\varpi^{e}
is the largest power of
\varpi
that divides all the
a_{i}
. It is immediate to check that if
D^{\prime}
is a Cartier divisor on
X^{\prime}
containing the image of
\mathfrak{p}
in
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\simeq P
with multiplicity
1
we have:

\widehat{\alpha}^{\prime*}D^{\prime}=\widetilde{D}^{\prime}+(e-1)E,

where
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
is effective and does not contain
E
as a component.

As a consequence of the previous paragraph, we may apply Proposition
9.1.3
repeatedly to obtain the following result:

Proposition 9.1.4
.

Let
\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
be a nonconstant morphism from
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
to a smooth, quasi-projective scheme over
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. Then there exists a morphism
\pi:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
which is a composition of blow-ups of closed points, such that
\widehat{\alpha}
lifts to a quasi-finite morphism
\widehat{\alpha}^{\prime}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
.

A finiteness result for exceptional curves

The following finiteness theorem is a special case of a result on
A
-mod-affine
A
-schemes that will appear in the forthcoming work
[
BC
]
and holds in arbitrary dimension. We provide a proof of the special case we are interested in.

Theorem 9.1.5
.

Let
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
be a pseudoconcave formal-analytic arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
and let

\alpha=(\widehat{\alpha},(\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}):{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

be a nonconstant morphism from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to an integral, normal, quasi-projective arithmetic surface
X
. Then there exist only finitely many irreducible Weil divisors
D
on
X
such that both
\widehat{\alpha}^{-1}(D)
and the
(\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})^{-1}(D_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})
are empty – i.e., such that
\alpha
factors through
X\setminus D
.

Proof.

Let
D
be an irreducible Weil divisor
D
on
X
such that both
\widehat{\alpha}^{-1}(D)
and the various
(\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})^{-1}(D_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})
are empty as
\sigma
runs through the complex embeddings of
K
.

Let
P
be the
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-point of
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
corresponding to
|{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}|,
let
g_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}
denote the equilibrium potential on the disjoint union of the Riemann surfaces
V_{\sigma}
, and let
\overline{L}
be the
\mathbb{Q}
-Hermitian line bundle on
X
defined by the Arakelov divisor:

\alpha_{*}(P,g_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}):=(\widehat{\alpha}_{*}(P),\alpha^{\mathrm{an}}_{*}g_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}).

Proposition
8.2.6
shows that
\overline{L}
is nef and big. Additionally, as
\alpha^{\mathrm{an}}_{*}g_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}
vanishes outside the images of the Riemann surfaces
V_{\sigma}
, the Arakelov degree of the restriction of
\overline{L}
to
D
is zero.

By the arithmetic Hilbert-Samuel formula of
[
Zha95
, Theorem 1.4]
, we may find a nonzero section
\sigma
of
\overline{L}
such that
\sigma
has norm strictly smaller than
1
at every complex point of
X
. In particular, since the Arakelov degree of the restriction of
\overline{L}
to
D
is zero,
\sigma
vanishes identically on
D
. This proves that
D
is contained in the divisor of
\sigma
.

The set of Weil divisors considered in the statement of the theorem is contained in those Weil divisors that are components of the divisor of
\sigma
, which proves the result.
∎

Corollary 9.1.6
.

Let

\alpha=(\widehat{\alpha},(\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}):{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

be a nonconstant morphism from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to an integral, normal, affine arithmetic surface
X
. Let
D_{\mathrm{ex}}
be the reunion of those irreducible divisors
D
on
X
such that
\alpha
factors through
X\setminus D
. Then
D_{\mathrm{ex}}
is the support of a Cartier divisor on
X
, and the open subset
X\setminus D_{\mathrm{ex}}
is the smallest open affine subset
U
of
X
such that
\alpha
factors through
U
.

Proof.

Theorem
9.1.5
shows that
D_{\mathrm{ex}}
is a finite union of irreducible Weil divisors on
X
. As a consequence of the general results on arithmetic surfaces recalled in
0.6.3
,
D_{\mathrm{ex}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, so it is the support of a Cartier divisor and
X\setminus D_{\mathrm{ex}}
is affine.

Let
U
be an open affine subset of
X
such that
\alpha
factors through
U
. As
X
is normal, the complement of
U
in
X
is a Weil divisor
D
on
X
which is necessarily contained in
D_{\mathrm{ex}}
, so that
U
contains
X\setminus D_{\mathrm{ex}}.

∎

9.1.3.
A universal morphism to an affine arithmetic surface

As before, let
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
be a pseudoconcave smooth f.-a. arithmetic surface over
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. We will construct a universal morphism from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to an affine arithmetic
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-scheme.

Existence of a quasi-finite morphism

Proposition 9.1.7
.

Assume that the algebra
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
contains a non-constant element. Then there exists a morphism

\beta:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow Y

to an affine regular arithmetic surface
Y
that is quasi-finite, namely, the preimage of any closed point of
Y
is finite in
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
.

Proof.

The elements of
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
correspond bijectively to the morphisms from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}.
Consider a non-constant morphism

\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}.

Applying Proposition
9.1.4
, we may find a quasi-projective, regular arithmetic surface
Y^{\prime}
and a morphism

\pi:Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}

that is a composition of blow-ups of closed points, such that
\gamma
lifts to a quasi-finite morphism

\gamma^{\prime}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}.

Let
D
be the reunion of those components of the fibers of the structure morphism
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
that do not intersect
\gamma^{\prime}(P)
. It is readily checked, since
\gamma^{\prime}(P)
lies in the smooth locus of
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, that
D
meets every component of every fiber of
Y^{\prime}\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. As a consequence of the discussion in
0.6.3
,
Y^{\prime}\setminus D
is affine and, by construction,
\gamma^{\prime}
factors through a morphism
\beta:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Y:=Y^{\prime}\setminus D
which satisfies the conditions of the proposition.
∎

Consider the morphism
\beta:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Y
of Proposition
9.1.7
. As in Definition
8.1.1
, we introduce:

D(\beta):=\frac{\beta_{\ast}\big(P,g_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}\big)\cdot\beta_{\ast}\big(P,g_{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}_{\mathbb{C}}}\big)}{\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}}

so that:

D(\beta)=e(\beta)+\frac{1}{\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{N}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}}\Big(\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\beta}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{Y}_{\beta(P)})+\sum_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{Ex}(\beta_{\sigma}:(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\longrightarrow Y_{\sigma})\Big).

Consider a commutative diagram of morphisms over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
:

(9.1.7)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.77779pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 29.77779pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.19446pt\raise-15.93501pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.19446pt\raise-24.53671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-5.77779pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.83003pt\raise-25.75893pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.18057pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.95691pt\raise-9.35666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.60413pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 31.18057pt\raise-31.87003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
Y^{\prime}
is an integral, normal, affine arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. By Theorem
8.1.2
, we have:

(9.1.8)

\deg(f)\leq D(\varphi).

Since
\beta
is quasi-finite,
\beta^{\prime}
is quasi-finite. In particular, Proposition
5.2.2
guarantees that we may choose
\beta^{\prime}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
in such a way that the pair
(\deg f,-\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\beta}^{\prime}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow\widehat{Y}_{\beta^{\prime}(P)}))
is maximal for the lexicographic order.

Proposition 9.1.8
.

Consider a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}}

in which
Z
is an affine, integral arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. Then there exists an open subset
U
of
Y^{\prime}
such that
\beta^{\prime}
factors through
U
and

g_{|g^{-1}(U)}:g^{-1}(U)\longrightarrow U

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is smooth,
\gamma
lifts to a morphism from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to the normalization of
Z
. As a consequence, we may assume that
Z
is normal.

By definition of
Y^{\prime}
, the degree of
g\circ f
is bounded above by the degree of
f
, so that the degree of
g
is
1
, i.e.,
g
is a birational morphism. Proposition
9.1.2
together with the choice of
\beta^{\prime}
shows that
g
is an isomorphism above a neighborhood of
\beta^{\prime}(P)
in
Y^{\prime}
.

Let
U
be the smallest open subset of
Y^{\prime}
on which
g^{-1}
is defined. Then
g
contains
\beta^{\prime}(P)
. Furthermore, let
\sigma
be a complex embedding of
K
. Then, as
g
has degree
1
,
g_{\sigma}:Z_{\sigma}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}_{\sigma}
is an open immersion whose image contains
\beta^{\prime\mathrm{an}}_{\sigma}(V_{\sigma})=g_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{an}}_{\sigma}(V_{\sigma})).
This proves that
U_{\sigma}\subset Y^{\prime}_{\sigma}
contains
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}(V_{\sigma})
. As a consequence,
\beta^{\prime}
factors through
U\subset Y^{\prime}
. Since the birational map
g^{-1}
is defined on
U
, the morphism

g_{|g^{-1}(U)}:g^{-1}(U)\longrightarrow U

is an isomorphism.
∎

We want to improve on Proposition
9.1.8
by using the finiteness statement of Theorem
9.1.5
.

Let
X
be the largest open affine subscheme of
Y^{\prime}
such that
\beta^{\prime}
factors through
X
. The existence of
X
is guaranteed by Corollary
9.1.6
.

We denote by
\alpha
the natural morphism:

\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X.

Proposition 9.1.9
.

For any morphism over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}

\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow Z

from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to an integral, affine arithmetic surface
Z
and any commutative diagram of morphisms over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{X,}

p
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Proposition
9.1.8
shows that there exists an open subset
U
of
X
such that
\alpha
factors through
U
and

p_{|p^{-1}(U)}\longrightarrow U

is an isomorphism. Since the complement of any effective Weil divisor in
X
is affine, any nonzero Weil divisor in
X
meets
U
.

Consider
\mathcal{O}(X)
s a sub-algebra of
\mathcal{O}(Z)
via
p^{*}:\mathcal{O}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(Z)
. Let
f
be an element of
\mathcal{O}(Z)
. Then
f
lies in
Frac(\mathcal{O}(Z))=Frac(\mathcal{O}(Z))
. Furthermore,
f
defines a regular function on
U\subset X
. Since
X
is normal and every nonzero Weil divisor in
X
meets
U
, this implies that
f
defines a regular function on
X
. This proves that
p^{*}:\mathcal{O}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(Z)
is an isomorphism. Since
Z
is affine, this proves that
p
is an isomorphism.

∎

We may finally prove that
\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
is universal for morphisms to arithmetic schemes.

Theorem 9.1.10
.

Let
\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Z
be a morphism over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
to an affine arithmetic scheme
Y
. There exists a unique morphism

f:X\longrightarrow Z

such that the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{Z}

commutes.

Proof.

The unicity of
f
is clear. As
X
is integral, any
f
as in the statement factors through the the reduced subscheme of
Z
, so we may assume that
Z
is reduced. Finally, we may replace
Z
with an irreducible component of
Z
containing the image of
\gamma
and assume that
Z
is integral.

Consider the morphism

\delta=\alpha\times\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X\times_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}Z.

By Theorem
8.2.1
, the closed subset
\mathrm{im}(\delta)
has dimension
2
. Let
Z^{\prime}
be the normalization of
\mathrm{im}(\delta)
. It is an integral, normal, affine arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, and there is a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{X}

where
p
is the composition
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X\times_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}Z\rightarrow X.
By Proposition
9.1.9
,
p
is an isomorphism. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be the composition:

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z

where
q
is the composition
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X\times_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}Z\rightarrow Z.

Then

f\circ\alpha=q\circ p^{-1}\circ\alpha=q\circ\delta=\gamma.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Clearly, the Theorem above characterizes the morphism

\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

uniquely as it represents the functor sending an arithmetic scheme
Z
to the morphisms from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to
Z
. We will denote
X
by
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{aff}}.

9.1.4.
Application to the algebra
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})

The following result immediately implies Theorem
9.1.1
.

Theorem 9.1.11
.

The morphism

\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{aff}}

induces an isomorphism of
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-algebras:

\alpha^{*}:\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{aff}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}).

In particular, the
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-algebra
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
is finitely generated and there is a canonical isomorphism:

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{aff}}\simeq{\rm Spec\,}\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}).

Proof.

The morphism
\alpha
induces an injective morphism:

\alpha^{*}:\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{aff}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})

that sends
f
to
f\circ\alpha.

An element
\gamma
of
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
may be identified with a morphism over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, also denoted by
\gamma
:

\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}.

Theorem
9.1.10
shows that there exists a morphism
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}}

commutes. When we identify
f
with an element of
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathrm{aff}})
,
\alpha^{*}(f)
is the composition

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},

namely,
\alpha^{*}(f)=\gamma.
This proves that
\gamma
lies in the image of
\alpha^{*}
, and shows that
\alpha^{*}
is an isomorphism. The rest of the statement is clear.
∎

A quasi-projective variant

We offer without proof the following variant of Theorem
9.1.10
and Theorem
9.1.11
which does not use affineness assumptions. It follows from the same arguments.

Proposition 9.1.12
.

Assume that there exists a nonconstant morphism
\alpha_{0}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X_{0}
to an integral, quasi-projective arithmetic surface
X_{0}
over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. There exists a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{X_{0}}

in which
X
is a normal, integral, quasi-projective arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
and the pair:

(\deg f,-\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\alpha}))

is maximal for the lexicographic order. Given such a diagram, let
X
be the complement in
X^{\prime}
of those finitely many irreducible Weil divisors
D
in
X^{\prime}
such that
\alpha^{\prime}
factors through
X\setminus D.
Let
\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
be the morphism induced by
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Let
\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Z
be a morphism over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
to a quasi-projective arithmetic surface
Y
. Then there exists a finite, closed subset
F
of
X\setminus\alpha(P)
such that, letting
U
be the complement of
F
in
X
, there exists a morphism

f:U\longrightarrow Y

such that the diagram

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{Z}

commutes. The rational map
f:X\dashrightarrow Z
is unique.

Without affineness assumptions, we cannot get rid of the choice of the open subset
U
of
X
: indeed, we may always take
Z
to be the blow-up of
X
along a finite subscheme of
X\setminus\alpha(P).

There are cases where the algebra
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
is empty even though
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
comes from algebraic geometry, in some sense. In particular, there might exist non-constant morphisms from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to arithmetic surfaces over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
even though
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
is reduced to
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
.

Indeed, let
X
be a projective, integral, normal arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. Let
P
be an
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-point of
X
. Assume that the structure map
X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
is smooth along
P
. For every complex embedding
\sigma
of
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, let
V_{\sigma}
be a Riemann surface with boundary embedded in
X_{\sigma}
and containing
P_{\sigma}
in its interior. Assume that the
V_{\sigma}
are invariant under complex conjugation. Letting
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
be the formal completion of
X
along
P
defines a formal-analytic arithmetic surface
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}=({\widehat{\mathcal{V}}},(V_{\sigma},\iota_{\sigma})_{\sigma:K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}})
by letting the
\iota_{\sigma}
be the natural isomorphisms of complex formal schemes.

Assume that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is pseudoconcave – this is the situation considered in
[
Bos99
]
, and we refer to
[
Bos99
, section 7]
for explicit examples. By Theorem
9.1.5
, there exist only finitely many closed, irreducible Weil divisors
Z
on
X
such that the intersection of
Z
with
P
and of
Z_{\sigma}
with the
V_{\sigma}
are all empty. After enlarging the embedded Riemann surfaces with boundary
V_{\sigma}
, we may assume that any such Weil divisor
Z
is vertical, namely, it does not intersect the generic fiber
X_{K}
.

By construction, there is a non-constant morphism
i:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
defined by the immersion
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
and
V_{\sigma}\rightarrow X_{\sigma}.

Proposition 9.1.13
.

The
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-algebra
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
is reduced to
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. In other words, any morphism

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Z

over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to an affine scheme
Z
over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
is constant.

As both the Archimedean and formal components of
i
are immersions, the setup of the Proposition is the one considered in
[
Bos99
]
and
[
BCL09
]
. The argument below is very close to the arguments in those papers.

Proof.

Let
\beta
be an element in
\mathcal{O}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
. We consider
\beta
as a morphism:

\gamma:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}.

The morphism
i:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X
satisfies:

\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{i})=0

and, for any complex embedding
\sigma
of
K
:

\mathrm{Ex}(i_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})=0.

As a consequence of Theorem
8.1.2
, any commutative diagram

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X}

satisfies
\deg f=1.
Finally, let
D
be the reunion of those vertical Weil divisors of
X
that do not meet
i(P).
Then
i:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X\setminus D
satisfies the conclusion of Proposition
9.1.12
: there exists an open subset
U
of
X\setminus D
that is the complement of finitely many closed points, and a morphism
f:U\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
such that
\alpha
factors through
U
and the diagram

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}

commutes.

By construction, the generic fiber
U_{K}
of
U
coincides with
X_{K}
, so that it is proper over
K
. As a consequence, the morphism

f_{K}:X_{K}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{K}

is constant. Since
X
is irreducible, this implies that
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
is constant, so that
\beta
is constant.
∎

9.2.
Arithmetic Lefschetz-Nori theorems on étale fundamental groups

9.2.1.

Let
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
be a smooth formal-analytic arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, and consider a morphism over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
:

\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

to a normal, integral, quasi-projective arithmetic surface
X
over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
.
Let
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a complex embedding of
K
. The image
P
of
|{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}|\simeq{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
in
X
is an
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
-point
Q
of
X
. The composition

{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma^{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Spec\,}K\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}

defines a geometric point
Q_{\sigma}
of
X
, and we may consider the étale fundamental group

\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma})

of
X
with respect to the geometric point
Q_{\sigma}
.
Consider the fundamental group

\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma).

The morphism

\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

defines a morphism:

(9.2.1)

\widehat{\alpha}^{\sigma}_{*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma)\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma}).

Similarly, consider the fundamental group

\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma}),

namely the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex manifold
V_{\sigma}
. The holomorphic map

\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}:V_{\sigma}\longrightarrow X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\sigma}

sends the point
P_{\sigma}
of
V_{\sigma}
to the complex point
\alpha^{\mathrm{an}}_{\sigma}(P_{\sigma})
of
X_{\sigma}
, which we will aslo denote by
Q_{\sigma}
. We obtain a morphism

\alpha_{\sigma,*}^{\mathrm{an}}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma},Q_{\sigma})

obtained by composing the morphism

\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}},Q_{\sigma})

with the natural morphism

\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}},Q_{\sigma})\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma},Q_{\sigma}),

which is actually an isomorphism by the GAGA theorem. In particular, we obtain a morphism:

(9.2.2)

\alpha_{\sigma,*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma}).

Fix a complex embedding
\sigma_{0}:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. For any other complex embedding
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, choose an automorphism
\varphi_{\sigma}
of
\mathbb{C}
such that

\sigma=\varphi_{\sigma}\circ\sigma_{0}.

The induced morphism

\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}:{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{C}

satisfies by construction:

\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}\circ\eta_{\sigma_{0}}=\eta_{\sigma}

and as a consequence it induces an isomorphism of étale fundamental groups

f_{\sigma}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}}).

When
\sigma=\sigma_{0}
, we choose
\varphi_{\sigma_{0}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{C}}
so that
f_{\sigma_{0}}
is the identity.
We denote by
\alpha_{*}
the morphism of groups:

\alpha_{*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})\ast\Big(\bigast_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\Big)\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}}),

where
\ast
denotes the free product, with components
\widehat{\alpha}_{\sigma_{0},*}
and
f_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma,*}
.
Note that the morphism
\alpha_{*}
depends on the choice of
\sigma_{0}
and on the choices of the automorphisms
\varphi_{\sigma}
of
\mathbb{C}
for
\sigma\neq\sigma_{0}
– the dependence on the automorphisms
\varphi_{\sigma}
only relies on the restriction of
\varphi_{\sigma}
to the algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
of
\mathbb{Q}
in
\mathbb{C}
.

9.2.2.

We may state the main result of this subsection and some of its consequences.

Theorem 9.2.1
.

Assume that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is pseudoconcave and define, with the notation of Theorem
8.1.2
:

D(\alpha):=e(\alpha)+\frac{1}{\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{N}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}}\Big(\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{X}_{\alpha(P)})+\sum_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{Ex}(\alpha_{\sigma}:(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\longrightarrow X_{\sigma})\Big).

Then the index of the closure of the subgroup

\alpha_{*}\Big(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})\ast\Big(\bigast_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\Big)\Big)

in
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is finite and bounded above by
D(\alpha)
.

In other words, the closed subgroup generated by the image of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})
and the groups
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})
in
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
has index bounded above by
D(\alpha)
.
As an immediate corollary of Theorem
9.2.1
, we find, in the setting of Theorem
9.2.1
:

Corollary 9.2.2
.

Assume that the Riemann surfaces with boundary
V_{\sigma}
are all simply connected. Then the index of
\alpha_{*}\Big(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})\Big)
in
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is bounded above by
D(\alpha)
.

Finally, we recover the following extension of the main result of
[
Bos99
]
, in the setting of Theorem
9.2.1
again:

Corollary 9.2.3
.

Assume that
\alpha
is an immersion
1
1

1

Namely, both
\widehat{\alpha}
and the holomorphic maps
\alpha^{\mathrm{an}}_{\sigma}
are immersions.
. Then the subgroup

\alpha_{*}\Big(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})\ast\Big(\bigast_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\Big)\Big)

is dense in
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
9.2.1
, the vanishing of the Archimedean overflow proved in Proposition
5.2.2
and the obvious vanishing of the term
\mathrm{Ex}(\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{X}_{\alpha(P)})
when
\widehat{\alpha}
is an immersion.
∎

9.2.3.

Before proving Theorem
9.2.1
, we prove some preliminary results. We keep the notation above.
Let
Y
be a normal, integral, quasi-projective arithmetic surface over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, and let
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
be a finite étale cover over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
. Let
R_{\sigma_{0}}
be a complex point of
Y
mapping to
Q_{\sigma_{0}}
by
f
. Let
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a complex embedding. Define
R_{\sigma}
to be the complex point
\varphi_{\sigma}\circ\eta_{Y,\sigma_{0}}
of
Y
. We denote also by
R_{\sigma}
the corresponding complex point of
Y_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 9.2.4
.

Assume that the image of the morphism
f_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma,*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is contained in
\pi_{*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})).
Then the image of the morphism
\alpha_{\sigma,*}^{\mathrm{an}}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma},Q_{\sigma})
is contained in
\pi_{\sigma,*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y_{\sigma},R_{\sigma}).

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram of profinite groups:

\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y_{\sigma},R_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\sigma,*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\sigma,*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\mathrm{an}}_{\sigma,*}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma},Q_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\eta_{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})}

where
g_{\sigma}
is induced by
\varphi_{\sigma}
just as
f_{\sigma}
is and the unlabeled arrows are the natural ones. Note that both
g_{\sigma}
and
f_{\sigma}
are isomorphisms. As the two leftmost vertical maps are injective and their images have finite index, both equal to the degree of the covering
\pi
, the left-hand square in the diagram above is cartesian.
Since the image of the morphism
f_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma,*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is contained in the subgroup
\pi_{*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})),
the image of the morphism
\alpha_{\sigma,*}^{\mathrm{an}}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma},Q_{\sigma})
is contained in
\pi_{\sigma,*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y_{\sigma},R_{\sigma}).

∎

Lemma 9.2.5
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

Assume that the image of the morphism
\widehat{\alpha}^{\sigma_{0}}_{*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is contained in
\pi_{*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})).
Then:

\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

lifts to a morphism
\widehat{\beta}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Y
such that
\pi\circ\widehat{\beta}=\widehat{\alpha}.

(2)

Let
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a complex embedding. Assume that the image of the morphism
f_{\sigma}\circ\alpha_{\sigma,*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is contained in
\pi_{*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})).
Then

\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}:V_{\sigma}\longrightarrow X_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}

lifts to a morphism
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}:V_{\sigma}\rightarrow Y_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
such that
\pi_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}\circ\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}=\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}.

Furthermore, if
(1)
and
(2)
both hold, then we may choose the lifts
\widehat{\beta}
and
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
above in such a way that, for any
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, the point
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}(P_{\sigma})
is the complex point of
Y_{\sigma}
defined as the base change by
\sigma
of the
K
-point

{\rm Spec\,}K\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\simeq|{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y

of
Y
.

Proof.

With the assumption of
(1)
, the general theory of the étale fundamental group shows that the composition

{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}=|{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}|{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}X

lifts to a morphism
|\widehat{\beta}|:{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\rightarrow Y
such that the composition

{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{\sigma_{0}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|\widehat{\beta}|}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y

is the complex point
R_{\sigma_{0}}.
In turn, since
{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}
is smooth over
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, this implies that
\widehat{\alpha}
lifts to a morphism
\widehat{\beta}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow Y
such that
the composition

{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{\sigma_{0}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y

is the complex point
R_{\sigma}.

If the assumption of
(2)
holds, Lemma
9.2.4
shows the image of the morphism
\alpha_{\sigma,*}^{\mathrm{an}}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\rightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X_{\sigma},Q_{\sigma})
is contained in
\pi_{\sigma,*}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y_{\sigma},R_{\sigma}).

As in
(i)
, this implies that
\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
lifts to a morphism

\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}:V_{\sigma}\longrightarrow Y_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}

with
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}(P_{\sigma})=R_{\sigma}.

To finish the proof, it remains to check that
R_{\sigma}
is indeed the complex point of
Y_{\sigma}
defined as the base change by
\sigma
of the
K
-point

{\rm Spec\,}K\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\simeq|{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y.

This is clear by the properties of the liftings
\widehat{\beta}
and
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
described above.
∎

Lemma 9.2.6
.

Assume that the morphism

\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

lifts to a morphism
\widehat{\beta}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow Y
such that
f\circ\beta=\alpha
, and assume that, for all complex embeddings
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, the holomorphic map
\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}:V_{\sigma}\longrightarrow X_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
lifts to a morphism
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}:V_{\sigma}\longrightarrow Y_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
such that
f_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}\circ\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}=\alpha^{\mathrm{an}}
and
\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}(P_{\sigma})
is the complex point of
Y_{\sigma}
defined as the base change by
\sigma
of the
K-point

{\rm Spec\,}K\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}\simeq|{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y

of
Y
.
Then

\beta:=(\widehat{\beta},(\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}))

defines a morphism
\beta:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow Y
such that
f\circ\beta=\alpha.

Proof.

To prove the lemma, it suffices to show that
(\widehat{\beta},(\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}))
does define a morphism from the formal-analytic arithmetic surface
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to
Y
, as, if it does, the equality
f\circ\beta=\alpha
follows from the similar componentwise equalities.
Write

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}=({\widehat{\mathcal{V}}},(V_{\sigma},\iota_{\sigma})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}))

as in
6.1.1
. We want to prove that the datum
(\widehat{\beta},(\beta_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}))
is compatible with the gluing data
(\iota_{\sigma})_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}
. This follows immediately from the unicity of the liftings of the holomorphic maps
\alpha_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{an}}
with prescribed value at
P_{\sigma}
.
∎

9.2.4.

We may finish the proof of Theorem
9.2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
9.2.1
.

Let
G
be the closure of the subgroup

\alpha_{\ast}\Big(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\sigma_{0})\ast\bigast_{\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V_{\sigma},P_{\sigma})\Big)

in
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
.
We argue by contradiction and assume that the index of
G
– be it finite or infinite – is strictly larger than
D(\alpha)
. As a consequence, since the group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
is profinite, we may find a closed finite-index subgroup
H
of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})
containing
G
such that the index of
H
is
d>D(\alpha).

The group
H
correspomds to a finite étale cover of pointed arithmetic surfaces:

\pi:(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})\longrightarrow(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})

that satisfies the following property: the image of the morphism of profinite groups

\pi_{*}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(Y,R_{\sigma_{0}})\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,Q_{\sigma_{0}})

equals
H
. In particular, the degree of
\pi
is
d>D(\alpha)
, and the image of
\pi_{*}
contains
G
.
Lemma
9.2.5
and Lemma
9.2.6
guarantee that the morphism
\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X
lifts to a morphism
\beta:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow Y
such that
\pi\circ\beta=\alpha
. As the degree
d
of
\pi
is strictly larger than
D(\alpha)
, this contradicts Theorem
8.1.2
.
∎

9.3.
Applications: arithmetic surfaces with finite étale fundamental group and integral models of modular curves

In this final section, we present applications of Theorem
9.2.1
to the construction of arithmetic surfaces that admit a
finite étale fundamental group
, in the spirit of the paper
[
Iha94
]
of Ihara and of
[
Bos99
]
— where arithmetic surfaces with trivial étale fundamental group were constructed — and of the applications in
[
CDT21
]
of the “arithmetic holonomicity theorem” to modular curves.

We have not attempted to formulate our results in a definitive form of maximal generality. Instead we have tried to spell out some simple qualitative consequences of our results on étale fundamental groups of arithmetic surfaces, in the specific case where the “base arithmetic curve”
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
is
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
.

9.3.1.

When
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}={\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, the statement of Theorem
9.2.1
becomes especially simple. Indeed there is a unique field embedding from
K=\mathbb{Q}
into
\mathbb{C}
(!!) and the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z},\sigma_{0})
is trivial according to Minkowski’s Theorem. Accordingly Theorem
9.2.1
takes the following form:

Corollary 9.3.1
.

Let
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}:=({\widehat{\mathcal{V}}},(V,O),\iota)
be a smooth formal-analytic arithmetic surface over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, and let:

\alpha:=(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha^{\rm an}):{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

be a morphism over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}

to a normal integral arithmetic surface
X
such that
\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, or equivalently
\alpha^{\rm an}
, is non-constant.
Let:

\alpha_{\ast}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V,O)\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\alpha^{\rm an}(O))

be the continuous morphism of profinite groups defined by the composite map:

V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha^{{\rm an}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow X.

If
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is pseudoconcave, then
\alpha_{\ast}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V,O))
is a subgroup of finite index in
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\alpha_{\mathbb{C}}(O))
. Moreover:

(9.3.1)

[\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\alpha^{\rm an}(O))):\alpha_{\ast}(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(V,O))]\leq D(\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X).

In turn, specialized to the situation where the compact Riemann surface with boundary
V
is simply connected, or equivalently when
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is an instance of the f.-a.​​ arithmetic surfaces
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}({\overline{D}}(0;1),\psi)
introduced in Section
6.4
, Corollary
9.3.1
becomes:

Corollary 9.3.2
.

Let
\psi
be a formal series in:

G_{\mathrm{for}}(\mathbb{R}):=\mathbb{R}^{\ast}X+X^{2}\mathbb{R}[[X]],

and let:

\alpha:=(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha^{\rm an}):{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}({\overline{D}}(0;1),\psi)\longrightarrow X

be a morphism over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}

to a normal integral arithmetic surface
X
such that
\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, or equivalently
\alpha^{\rm an}
, is non-constant.

If
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}({\overline{D}}(0;1),\psi)
is pseudoconcave, or equivalently if
|\psi^{\prime}(0)|<1,
then the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
is finite. Moreover its cardinality satisfies:

(9.3.2)

\big|\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)\big|\leq D(\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X).

In (
9.3.2
),
\ast
denotes an arbitrary geometric point of
X
. This same notation will be used in Propositions
9.3.3
and
9.3.5
below.

9.3.2.

From Corollary
9.3.2
, we may derive a finiteness criterion for the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
of an arithmetic surface
X
equipped with a section of the structure map
X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
that makes no reference to formal-analytic arithmetic surfaces.

Proposition 9.3.3
.

Let
X
be a normal integral arithmetic surface and let
Q\in X(\mathbb{Z})
be a section of the structure morphism
X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
the image of which lies in the regular locus of
X
.

Let moreover:

(9.3.3)

a:{\overline{D}}(0;1)^{+}\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C})

be a non-constant map, analytic up to the boundary and compatible with complex conjugation, such that:

a(0)=Q_{\mathbb{C}}.

Let
e
denote the ramification index of
a
at
0
, let:

a^{[e]}(0):T_{{\overline{D}}(0;1),0}^{\otimes e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}T_{X(\mathbb{C}),Q_{\mathbb{C}}}

be the
e
-th jet of
a
at
0,
and let
\|.\|_{a}
be the Hermitian norm on
T_{X(\mathbb{C}),Q_{\mathbb{C}}}\simeq(N_{Q}X)_{\mathbb{C}}
defined by:

(9.3.4)

\|a^{[e]}(0)((\partial/\partial z)^{\otimes e})\|_{a}=1.

If the Hermitian line bundle over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
:

{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X:=(N_{Q}X,\|.\|_{a})

satisfies the positivity condition:

(9.3.5)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X>0,

then the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
is finite, and its cardinality satisfies:

(9.3.6)

\big|\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)\big|\leq e+e\,\frac{{\mathrm{Ex}}\big(a:{\overline{D}}(0;1)\rightarrow X(\mathbb{C})\big)}{\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X}.

The proof of Proposition
9.3.3
is more transparent in the “unramified case” where
e=1.
As only this special case of Proposition
9.3.3
will be used in the following subsections, we first present its simpler proof when
e=1
.

Proof of Proposition
9.3.3
when
e=1
.

Let us consider the formal completion
{\widehat{X}}_{Q}
of
X
along
Q
. Since the image of
Q
lies in the regular locus of
X,
the formal scheme over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
:

{\widehat{X}}_{Q}\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z},

equipped with the section
Q
, defines a pointed smooth formal curve over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, in the sense
[
Bos20
, 10.4]
.

Since
e=1,
the formal germ of
a
at
0
defines an isomorphism of smooth formal complex curves:

\widehat{a}_{0}:\widehat{{\overline{D}}(0;1)}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{X_{\mathbb{C}}}_{,Q_{\mathbb{C}}}\simeq({\widehat{X}}_{Q})_{\mathbb{C}}.

Therefore the triple:

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}:=({\widehat{X}}_{Q},{\overline{D}}(0;1),\widehat{a}_{0}^{-1})

defines a smooth f.-a.​​ arithmetic surface over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
.

If we define:

\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{X}}_{Q}\longrightarrow X

as the “inclusion” morphism, and:

\alpha^{{\rm an}}:=a:{\overline{D}}(0;1)^{+}\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C}),

then the pair:

\alpha:=(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha^{{\rm an}}):{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\longrightarrow X

defines a non-constant morphism with ramification index
e(\alpha)=1
.
Moreover the Hermitian line bundle
{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is canonically isomorphic to
{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X
and the excess
{\mathrm{Ex}}(\widehat{\alpha}:{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X)
clearly vanishes.

Consequently the positivity condition (
9.3.5
) is satisfied if and only if
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is pseudoconcave, and when this holds:

\displaystyle D(\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X)

\displaystyle=e(\alpha)+\big(\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\big)^{-1}\Big({\mathrm{Ex}}({\widehat{\alpha}}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X)+{\mathrm{Ex}}(\alpha^{\rm an}:({\overline{D}}(0;1),0))\rightarrow X(\mathbb{C}))\Big)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{{\mathrm{Ex}}(a:{\overline{D}}(0;1)\rightarrow X(\mathbb{C})\big)}{\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X}.

Therefore the finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
when (
9.3.5
) holds and the bound (
9.3.6
) on its cardinality follows from Corollary
9.3.2
and from the bound (
9.3.2
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.3.3
.

We now establish Proposition
9.3.3
when the ramification index
e
is an arbitrary positive integer.

The smooth formal curve
{\widehat{X}}_{Q}
over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
is isomorphic to
{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[T]]
(see for instance
[
Bos20
, 10.4.2]
), and we may choose an isomorphism of formal schemes:

j:{\widehat{X}}_{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[T]].

The data of the isomorphism
j
is indeed equivalent to the one of
t:=j^{\ast}T,
which is
a non-zero element of
\Gamma({\widehat{X}}_{Q},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\widehat{X}}_{Q}})
such that

{\mathrm{div}\,}t=Q.

Restricted to the first order neighborhood of
Q
in
X,
the formal function
t
defines a trivialization of the normal bundle of
Q
in
X
:

N_{Q}X\simeq\mathbb{Z}\,(\partial/\partial t).

The morphism of smooth formal complex curve:

j_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\widehat{a}_{0}:{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{C}[[z]]\simeq\widehat{{\overline{D}}(0;1)}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widehat{a}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}({\widehat{X}}_{Q})_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}}}{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{C}[[T]].

is defined by the series:

\varphi:=(j_{\mathbb{C}}\circ\widehat{a}_{0})^{\ast}T\in\mathbb{R}[[z]],

which is of the form:

\varphi=\mu z^{e}+O(z^{e+1}),

with
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{\ast}.

In other words, we have:

a^{[e]}(0)((\partial/\partial z)^{\otimes e})=\mu(\partial/\partial t)_{\mathbb{C}}.

This implies the relations:

\|\mu(\partial/\partial t)_{\mathbb{C}}\|_{a}=1,

and:

(9.3.7)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X=\log\|(\partial/\partial t)_{\mathbb{C}}\|_{a}^{-1}=\log|\mu|.

We shall write:

\mu=\varepsilon|\mu|,\quad\mbox{where $\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}$.}

There exists a unique series
\rho
in
\mathbb{R}[[z]]
such that:

\rho(0)=0,\quad\rho^{\prime}(0)=|\mu|^{1/e},

and:

\varphi(z)=\varepsilon\rho(z)^{e}.

We shall denote by
\psi
its compositional inverse:

\psi:=\rho^{-1}\in G_{\mathrm{for}}(\mathbb{R}):=\mathbb{R}^{\ast}X+X^{2}\,\mathbb{R}[[X]],

and consider
the smooth f.-a.​​ arithmetic surface over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
:

{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}:={\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}({\overline{D}}(0;1),\psi)=({\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[X]],{\overline{D}}(0;1),i),

where
i
is the isomorphism of
smooth formal complex curves:

i:({\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[X]])_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{C}[[X]]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{{\overline{D}}(0;1)}_{0}\simeq{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{C}[[z]]

defined by:

i^{\ast}(z):=\psi(X).

Using the expression (
6.4.3
) for
\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}},
we get:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}=\log|\psi^{\prime}(0)|^{-1}=\log|\rho^{\prime}(0)|=(1/e)\log|\mu|.

Together with (
9.3.7
), this establishes the equality:

(9.3.8)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}=(1/e)\,\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X.

We may define a morphism of formal scheme from
{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[X]]
to
X
as the composition:
2
2

2

The same letter
X
denotes both the arithmetic surface
X
and an indeterminate. Hopefully this would not introduce any confusion.

\widehat{\alpha}:{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[X]]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle X\mapsto\varepsilon X^{e}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[T]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\widehat{X}}_{Q}{\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow}X,

or equivalently by the relation:

(9.3.9)

\widehat{\alpha}^{\ast}t=\varepsilon X^{e}.

Its excess
{\mathrm{Ex}}(\widehat{\alpha}:{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{Z}[[X]]\rightarrow X)
vanishes. Indeed, with the notation of
7.2.2
, we have
\widehat{\alpha}^{\ast}(Q)=eP.

Moreover the analytic map:

\alpha^{{\rm an}}:=a:{\overline{D}}(0;1)^{+}\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C})

is compatible with complex conjugation, and satisfies:

(9.3.10)

\alpha^{\rm an}\circ i=\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{C}}:{\rm Spf\,}\mathbb{C}[[X]]\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C}).

Indeed the following equality holds in
\mathbb{C}[[X]]
:

(\alpha^{\rm an}\circ i)^{\ast}t_{\mathbb{C}}=i^{\ast}\varphi(z)=\varphi(\psi(X))=\varepsilon\rho(\psi(X))^{e}=\varepsilon X^{e}=\widehat{\alpha}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}t_{\mathbb{C}}.

The equality (
9.3.10
) shows that the pair
(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha^{{\rm an}})
defines a morphism from the f.-a.​​ arithmetic surface
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to the arithmetic surface
X
:

\alpha:=(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha^{{\rm an}}):{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}:={\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}({\overline{D}}(0;1),\psi)\longrightarrow X.

Clearly it is non-constant, of ramification index
e(\alpha)=e
.

According to (
9.3.8
),
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is pseudoconcave if and only if the positivity condition (
9.3.5
) is satisfied. Moreover when this holds, we have:

\displaystyle D(\alpha:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X)

\displaystyle=e(\alpha)+\big(\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}_{P}{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\big)^{-1}\Big({\mathrm{Ex}}({\widehat{\alpha}}:{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}\rightarrow X)+{\mathrm{Ex}}(\alpha^{\rm an}:({\overline{D}}(0;1),0))\rightarrow X(\mathbb{C}))\Big)

\displaystyle=e+e\,\frac{{\mathrm{Ex}}(a:{\overline{D}}(0;1)\rightarrow X(\mathbb{C})\big)}{\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X}.

Here again, the finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
when (
9.3.5
) holds and the bound (
9.3.6
) on its cardinality follows from Corollary
9.3.2
and from the bound (
9.3.2
).
∎

Proposition
9.3.3
applies notably to models of elliptic curves over
\mathbb{Q}
:

Corollary 9.3.4
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
, and let
0_{E}\in E(\mathbb{Q})
be its zero element. If
{\mathcal{E}}
is an integral regular arithmetic surface that is a model of
E
, and if
0_{E}
extends to an integral point
0_{{\mathcal{E}}}
in
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathbb{Z})
, then the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{E}},\ast)
is finite.
3
3

3

Where
\ast
denotes an arbitrary geometric point of
X
.

In particular,
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{E}},\ast)
is finite if
{\mathcal{E}}
is the connected Néron model of
E
. Conversely the finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{E}},\ast)
when
{\mathcal{E}}
is the connected Néron model of
E
is easily seen to imply its finiteness for every model
{\mathcal{E}}
of
E
that satisfies the assumptions of Corollary
9.3.4
.

The generalization of Corollary
9.3.4
where
E
is replaced by an arbitrary abelian variety over
\mathbb{Q}
is actually valid, and may be derived from Weil’s classical results concerning abelian varieties over finite fields and the Frobenius action on their Tate module, as shown by Katz and Lang in
[
KL81
]
.
4
4

4

To derive Corollary
9.3.4
from the results in Katz-Lang, observe that we may assume that
{\mathcal{E}}
is smooth over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z},
and that
[
KL81
, Lemma 2]
implies that
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{E}},\ast)
is a quotient of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(E_{{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}},\ast)
, and therefore is abelian. Accordingly
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{E}},\ast)
may be identified with the group
\mathrm{Ker}(X/S)
investigated in
[
KL81
]
when
X={\mathcal{E}}
and
S={\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, and is therefore finite by
[
KL81
, Theorem 1]
.

We have explicitly stated Corollary
9.3.4
for the striking simplicity of its derivation from the general finiteness criterion in Proposition
9.3.3
.

Proof.

Let us denote by:

\exp_{E(\mathbb{C})}:{\rm Lie\,}E_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow E(\mathbb{C})

the exponential map of the complex Lie group
E(\mathbb{C})
, and let us choose a non-zero element
v
of
{\rm Lie\,}E_{\mathbb{R}}
. For every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}_{+},
we may introduce the following étale complex analytic map, compatible with complex conjugation:

a_{\lambda}:{\overline{D}}(0;1)^{+}\longrightarrow E(\mathbb{C}),\quad z\longmapsto\exp_{E(\mathbb{C})}(z\lambda v).

It is straighforward that, with the notation of Proposition
9.3.3
, the norm
\|.\|_{a_{\lambda}}
on
(T_{0_{E}}E)_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\rm Lie\,}E_{C}
satisfies:

\|\lambda v\|_{a_{\lambda}}=1.

Consequently:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a_{\lambda}}_{0_{\mathcal{E}}}{\mathcal{E}}=\log\lambda+\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a_{1}}_{0_{\mathcal{E}}}{\mathcal{E}}.

Therefore, if
\lambda
is large enough, the positivity condition
9.3.5
is satisfied by
X:={\mathcal{E}},

Q:=0_{\mathcal{E}}
, and
a:=a_{\lambda}
.
∎

Using the general form of the “arithmetic Lefschetz-Nori theorem” stated in Theorem
9.2.1
, which is valid over an arbitrary number field
K,
a similar argument establishes more generally that,
if
\pi:{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
is an integral regular model over
{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
of an elliptic curve
E
over
K
such that the zero elements
0_{E}\in E(K)
extends to an integral point
0_{\mathcal{E}}
in
{\mathcal{E}}({{\mathcal{O}}_{K}})
, then:

\ker\big(\pi_{\ast}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\mathcal{E}},\ast)\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\pi(\ast))\big)

is a finite group.
5
5

5

Actually abelian with two generators, since it is a quotient of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(E_{{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}})\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, say by
[
KL81
, Lemma 2, (2)]
.
We leave the details to the interested reader.

9.3.3.

If one is interested in the qualitative aspect of Proposition
9.3.3
only, namely in the finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
, its hypotheses may be slightly relaxed.

For instance, one may assume that the map
a
introduced in (
9.3.3
) is a non-constant analytic map:

(9.3.11)

a:\mathring{D}(0;1)\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C}),

defined only on the open disk
\mathring{D}(0;1)
. Alternatively, one may replace the positivity condition (
9.3.5
) by the weaker condition:

(9.3.12)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X\geq 0.

In the first case, the finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
follows from Corollary
9.3.3
applied to

a_{r}:{\overline{D}}(0;1)^{+}\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C}),\quad z\longmapsto a(rz),

where
r\in(0,1)
is chosen close enough to
1
so that:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a_{r}}_{Q}X>0.

In the second case, it follows again from Corollary
9.3.3
applied to
a_{r}
, where now
r
is chosen close enough to 1 in
(1,+\infty)
to make
a_{r}
defined on an open neighborhood of the closed disk
{\overline{D}}(0;1)
.

It is actually possible to derive from Corollary
9.3.3
a variant which covers both the more general definition (
9.3.11
) of
a
, and the limit case of (
9.3.12
) when:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X=0.

Proposition 9.3.5
.

Let
X
be a normal integral arithmetic surface and let
Q\in X(\mathbb{Z})
be a section of the structure morphism
X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
the image of which lies in the regular locus of
X
.

Let moreover:

(9.3.13)

a:\mathring{D}(0;1)\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C})

be an analytic map, compatible with complex conjugation and étale at
0
,
6
6

6

Namely, such that its differential
Da(0):T_{\mathring{D}(0;1),0}\rightarrow T_{X(\mathbb{C}),Q_{\mathbb{C}}}
is an isomorphism.
such that:

a(0)=Q_{\mathbb{C}},

and let
\|.\|_{a}
be the Hermitian norm on
T_{X(\mathbb{C}),Q_{\mathbb{C}}}
defined by:

(9.3.14)

\|Da(0)(\partial/\partial z)\|_{a}=1.

If the Hermitian line bundle over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
:

{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X:=(N_{Q}X,\|.\|_{a})

satisfies:

(9.3.15)

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X=0,

and if the map
a
is
not
a universal covering of
X(\mathbb{C}),
then the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
is finite.

Proof.

According to the uniformization theorem, there exists a universal covering:

u:(M,0)\longrightarrow(X(\mathbb{C}),Q_{\mathbb{C}})

of the pointed Riemann surface
(X(\mathbb{C}),Q_{\mathbb{C}})
where
M
is either
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
,
\mathbb{C}
, or
\mathring{D}(0;1)
.
7
7

7

The complex line
\mathbb{C}
is embedded in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
by the usual map
(z\mapsto(1:z))
; in particular
0
denotes the point
(1:0)
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
.

Since
\mathring{D}(0;1)
is simply connected, there exists a unique complex analytic map between pointed Riemann surfaces:

\widetilde{a}:(\mathring{D}(0;1),0)\longrightarrow(M,0)

such that the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathring{D}(0;1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{a}}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X(\mathbb{C}).}

By construction
\widetilde{a}(0)=0,
and we may consider the derivative
\widetilde{a}^{\prime}(0)
of
\widetilde{a}
at
0
.

When
M=\mathring{D}(0;1),
the complex analytic map
\widetilde{a}
is not a diffeomorphism — since
a
is not a universal covering of
X(\mathbb{C})
— and according to the Schwarz lemma, the following estimates holds:

|\widetilde{a}^{\prime}(0)|<1.

This implies the following relations between Hermitian norms on
T_{Q_{\mathbb{C}}}X(\mathbb{C})
:

\|.\|_{u}=|\widetilde{a}^{\prime}(0)|\,\|.\|_{a}<\|.\|_{a},

where
\|.\|_{u}
is defined as in (
9.3.14
) with
u
instead of
a
,
and consequently:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{u}_{Q}X:=\widehat{\rm deg}\,(N_{Q}X,\|.\|_{a})>\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X\geq 0.

The finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
therefore follows from the first variant of Proposition
9.3.3
mentioned above, applied with
a:=u
and
e=1.

When
M
is
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
or
\mathbb{C}
, then, for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\ast},
we may consider the map:

u_{\lambda}:{\overline{D}}(0;1)^{+}\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C}),\quad z\longmapsto u(\lambda z).

The the following equality between Hermitian norms on
T_{Q_{\mathbb{C}}}X(\mathbb{C})
is satisfied:

\|.\|_{u_{\lambda}}=\lambda^{-1}\,|\widetilde{a}^{\prime}(0)|\,\|.\|_{a},

and therefore:

\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{u_{\lambda}}_{Q}X=\log(\lambda/|\widetilde{a}^{\prime}(0)|)+\widehat{\rm deg}\,{\overline{N}}^{a}_{Q}X.

When
\lambda
is large enough, this is positive, and therefore the finiteness of
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
follows from Proposition
9.3.3
applied with
a:=u_{\lambda}
and
e=1.
8
8

8

Actually, when
M=\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C}),
the fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
is easily seen to be trivial.

∎

In applications, it is convenient to combine Proposition
9.3.5
and the following result of independent interest about étale fundamental groups of arithmetic surfaces.

Proposition 9.3.6
.

Let
X
be a normal integral arithmetic surface, and let
D
be a closed reduced subscheme of pure dimension 1 in
X
.

If
D
is regular and contained in the regular locus of
X
and if, for every irreducible component
9
9

9

or equivalently, according to the regularity of
D
, for every connected component.

C
of
D
, the structure morphism:

C\longrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}

is surjective, then the inclusion morphism:

\iota:X\setminus D\longrightarrow X

induces an isomorphism between étale fundamental groups:

(9.3.16)

\iota_{\ast}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X\setminus D,\ast)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast).

In (
9.3.16
),
\ast
denotes an arbitrary geometric point of
X\setminus D.

Proposition
9.3.6
follows from a variation on the proof of the triviality of the group:

\ker\left(\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\},\ast)\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\ast^{\prime})\right)

due to T. Saito presented in
[
Iha94
, Appendix]
; see also
[
Ems99
]
for related results and references.

Proof.

Let us assume that
D
is regular and contained in the regular locus of
X
, and that, for every irreducible component
C
of
D
, the structure morphism
C\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
is surjective, or equivalently, that for every prime
p
, there exists a closed point
x
of
C
such that the finite field
\kappa(x):={\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}
has characteristic
p
.

Consider a finite étale covering:

f:Y\longrightarrow X\setminus D,

with
Y
connected, and therefore integral since
Y
like
X\setminus D
is normal.

Establishing that the map (
9.3.16
) is an isomorphism amounts to proving that any such finite étale covering of
X\setminus D
extends to a finite étale covering of
X
:

(9.3.17)

\tilde{f}:\widetilde{Y}\longrightarrow X.

To achieve this, we define
\widetilde{Y}
and
\tilde{f}
as in (
9.3.17
) as the normalization of
X
in the function field
\kappa(Y)
of
Y
. Since
Y
is normal and
f
is finite, over the open subscheme
X\setminus D
of
X
this normalization may be identified to
f:Y\rightarrow X\setminus D
, and by purity we are left to show that the ramifications indices of
\tilde{f}
over every component
C
of
D
are equal to one.

This follows from the absolute Abhyankar Lemma in
[
Gro71
, Chapitre XIII, Proposition 5.2]
, which shows that, for any closed point
x
of
C
these ramification indices are not divisible by the characteristic of
\kappa(x).
Indeed
f
is tamely ramified along
C
, since
C
is horizontal and therefore
\kappa(C)
has characteristic zero.
∎

Corollary 9.3.7
.

With the notation of Proposition
9.3.5
, for every geometric point
\ast
of
X\setminus Q
, the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X\setminus Q,\ast)
is isomorphic to
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
and therefore finite.

9.3.4.

Proposition
9.3.5
and Corollary
9.3.7
are tailored to applications to integral models of modular curves.

For instance, for every integer
N\geq 3,
we may consider the scheme
\mathcal{Y}(N)^{\mathrm{arith}}
defined as in
[
Kat76
, Section 2.5]
10
10

10

where it is denoted by
M(\Gamma(N)^{\mathrm{arith}})
.
as representing the functor that maps a base scheme
S
to the isomorphism classes of pairs
({\mathcal{E}},\iota)
, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is an elliptic curve over
S
and
\iota
is an isomorphisms of finite flat group schemes over
S
:

\iota:(\mu_{N}\times\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})_{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{E}}[N].

It is a smooth affine curve over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, with geometrically irreducible fibers, and admits a partial compactification, defined by a similar moduli problem, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is now a generalized elliptic curve. Moreover the “cusp at infinity”
\Gamma(N).\infty
extends to a section
\infty_{N}
over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
of this partial compactification, and the union of
\mathcal{Y}(N)^{\mathrm{arith}}
and of the image of
\infty_{N}
defines a quasi-projective arithmetic surface
X
smooth over
{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
, with geometrically irreducible fibers.

The formal neighborhood of
\infty_{N}
in
X
is described by the “Tate curve” over
\mathbb{Z}[[q^{1/N}]]
, the analytification of which defines a complex analytic map:

a:\mathring{D}(0;1)\longrightarrow X(\mathbb{C})

which satisfies:

a(0)=\infty_{N,\mathbb{C}}

and:

a(w):=\mbox{isomorphism class of $(\mathbb{C}^{\times}/w^{N\mathbb{Z}},\iota_{w})$},

where:

\iota_{w}:\mu_{N}(\mathbb{C})\times Z/N\mathbb{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbb{C}^{\times}/w^{N\mathbb{Z}})[N],\quad(\zeta,[b])\longmapsto[\zeta w^{b}].

We may apply Proposition
9.3.5
to the arithmetic surface
X
equipped with the section
Q:=\infty_{N}
and to the map
a
just defined.
Indeed, the fact that the “formal parameter”
q^{1/N}
describing the formal neghborhood of
\infty_{N}
in
X
coincides with the “analytic parameter”
w
in the open unit disk in the above analytic parametrization of
X(\mathbb{C})
shows that the vanishing condition (
9.3.15
) is satisfied. Moreover the map
a
is étale at
0
, and clearly is not a universal covering of
X(\mathbb{C})
.
11
11

11

The inverse image of
\infty_{N,\mathbb{C}}
is the point
0,
while the inverse image by
a
of every point of
X(\mathbb{C})\setminus\{\infty_{N,\mathbb{C}}\}
is infinite.

Therefore, according to Corollary
9.3.7
, we obtain:

Corollary 9.3.8
.

For every integer
N\geq 3
and every geometric point
\ast
of
\mathcal{Y}(N)^{\mathrm{arith}}
, the étale fundamental group
\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}(N)^{\mathrm{arith}},\ast)
is finite.

We are not aware of any example of an integral regular arithmetic surface
X
whose structure map
X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}\mathbb{Z}
admits a section, and whose étale fundamental group

\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)
is infinite.

We leave it to the reader to establish generalizations
12
12

12

concerning the finiteness of the kernel of the map
\pi_{\ast}:\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}(X,\ast)\longrightarrow\pi^{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}_{1}({\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}},\pi(\ast))
.
of the results of this subsection to normal integral arithmetic surfaces
X
over
{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
— where
K
is an arbitrary number field — that are equipped with a section
Q\in X({{\mathcal{O}}_{K}})
of the structure morphism
\pi:X\rightarrow{\rm Spec\,}{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}
, and to apply them to integral models of modular curves defined by classical moduli problems over ring of integers of cyclotomic fields.
